# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 25

## Page 12453

VOLUME TWENTY-FIVE
THE BOOK OF JURISPRUDENCE
PART FIVE:
ZAKAT AND FASTING

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

THE BOOK OF ZAKAT

Shaykh al-Islam Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that none is worthy of worship except Allah alone, without partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bestow blessings and abundant peace upon him.

To proceed:

Allah Most High has blessed His servants with Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He is the greatest blessing bestowed upon them. Whoever accepts this blessing has Allah’s favor completed upon him, his religion perfected for him, and is made a member of the best community brought forth for mankind. Allah sent him with guidance and the religion of truth, revealed to him the Book and wisdom, and made His Book a guardian over the scriptures that preceded it. In it He commanded

---

## Page 12454

the worship of Allah and kindness to His creation. Allah Most High says:

“Worship Allah and associate nothing with Him, and be good to parents, relatives, orphans, the needy, the near neighbor, the distant neighbor, the companion at your side, the traveler, and those whom your right hands possess. Indeed, Allah does not love anyone who is arrogant and boastful.”

He made His religion consist of three levels: Islam, then faith, then excellence. He made Islam rest upon five pillars. Among the most firmly enjoined of these is prayer, comprising five obligatory prayers, and He coupled it with zakat. Prayer is thus among the most firmly enjoined acts of worship, followed by zakat: prayer is worship of Him, while zakat is kindness to His creation. He therefore repeatedly enjoined prayer in numerous verses of the Quran, never mentioning it without coupling it with zakat. Among these is His saying, Most High:

“Establish prayer and give zakat.”

He also says:

“But if they repent, establish prayer, and give zakat, then they are your brothers in religion.”

And He says:

“They were commanded only to worship Allah, devoting their religion sincerely to Him, inclining to truth, and to establish prayer and give zakat. That is the upright religion.”

The two Sahīhs relate a hadith from Abū Hurayrah, also narrated by Muslim from Umar:

“Gabriel asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about Islam

---

## Page 12455

, and he replied: ‘To testify that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, establish prayer, give zakat, fast Ramadan, and perform pilgrimage to the House.’”

It is also narrated from him that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I have been commanded to fight the people until they say, ‘There is no god but Allah and Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah,’ establish prayer, and give zakat. If they do so, they have secured their lives and property against me, except where a rightful claim applies, and their reckoning rests with Allah.”

When he sent Muʿādh to Yemen, he told him:

“You are going to a people from among the People of the Book. Let the first thing you call them to be the testimony that there is no god but Allah and that I am the Messenger of Allah. If they accept this from you, inform them that Allah has prescribed for them five prayers each day and night. If they accept this from you, inform them that Allah has prescribed for them obligatory charity, to be taken from their wealthy and returned to their poor. If they accept this from you, take it from them, but avoid taking their choicest possessions. Beware of the supplication of one who has been wronged, for there is no barrier between it and Allah.”

Section

Prayer and zakat are mentioned in the Quran in general terms, and the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained them in detail. His explanation, too, is revelation, for Allah, glorified be He, sent down to him the Book and wisdom.

---

## Page 12456

Hassān ibn ‘Atiyyah said:

“Gabriel would descend to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with the Sunnah, teaching it to him just as he taught him the Quran.”

I have already devoted an earlier section to prayer. Our purpose here is to discuss zakat, so we shall mention what we can of its rulings, some hadiths, and some statements of the jurists. Allah has called zakat both charity and zakat.

Linguistically, the word zakat denotes growth. Crops are said to flourish when they grow, and they grow only when cleared of weeds. Hence, in Islamic law this word denotes purification:

“Successful indeed is the one who purifies it.”

“Successful indeed is the one who purifies himself.”

The giver’s soul grows in purity, as does his wealth: it is purified and increases in true worth. Islamic law makes clear that zakat was prescribed to provide assistance to others. Such assistance can only come from wealth one possesses, so minimum thresholds were set for it, and it was imposed on wealth capable of growth. This includes wealth that grows by itself, such as livestock and crops, and wealth that grows through being converted into other forms and put to use, such as money.

The amount taken was made proportionate to the effort involved. Wealth found from the pre-Islamic era requires the least effort, so one-fifth is due on it. Where effort is required in one respect, half of a fifth—that is, one-tenth—is due, as with crops watered by rainfall. Where effort is required in two respects, a quarter of a fifth—that is, one-twentieth—is due, as with crops irrigated by drawing water. Where effort is required throughout the year, as with money, one-eighth of that fifth—that is, one-fortieth—is due.

---

## Page 12457

Section

Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him, opened the Book of Zakat in his Muwatta’ with the hadith of Abū Sa‘īd, because it is the most authentic report on this subject. Muslim did likewise in his Sahīh. This hadith mentions the minimum thresholds for silver, camels, grain, and fruit.

Since livestock and money must be held for a full year, Mālik followed this with reports from Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and Ibn ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with them, establishing this requirement. Even if Mu‘āwiyah and Ibn ‘Abbās differed with them, what the caliphs narrated or stated constitutes authoritative evidence against those who disagree with them, especially al-Siddīq, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs after me.”

He also said:

“If the people obey Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, they will be rightly guided.”

Mālik then discussed “the minimum threshold for gold.” The evidence for it is weaker than that for silver, which is why he placed it later.

He then discussed the types of wealth on which zakat is levied, citing the relevant hadiths and Quranic verses. The strongest of these are the hadith of ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb and his written instructions concerning zakat. He also reported from ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz that zakat is due only on money and crops

---

## Page 12458

and livestock. Mālik adopted this view.

Ibn ‘Abd al-Barr said: “There is consensus that zakat is due on the things mentioned.” Imām Abū Bakr ibn al-Mundhir al-Naysābūrī said: “The scholars unanimously agree that zakat is obligatory on nine things: camels, cattle, sheep and goats, gold, silver, wheat, barley, dates, and raisins, provided that each category reaches the minimum amount on which zakat is due.”

Section

The two Sahīhs report from Abū Sa‘īd al-Khudrī that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No zakat is due on less than five wasqs, on fewer than five camels, or on less than five ūqiyyahs.” He indicated five with his fingers.

Another wording reads:

“No zakat is due on less than five wasqs of dates or grain.”

Another version has thamar (fruit), with the three-dotted letter thā’. Yet another wording reads:

“No zakat is due on less than five ūqiyyahs of silver.”

Muslim also narrated it from Jābir. Muslim further narrated from Jābir that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“One-tenth is due on what is watered by rivers and rain clouds, and half of one-tenth on what is irrigated using a water-drawing animal.”

Al-Bukhārī narrated it from Ibn ‘Umar with the wording:

“One-tenth is due on what is watered by rain and springs, or is watered naturally, and half of one-tenth on what is irrigated by drawing water.”

The Muwatta’ has the wording “springs and al-ba‘l.” Al-ba‘l is vegetation that draws water through its roots and spreads through the ground

---

## Page 12459

and needs no irrigation, as with grapevines and date palms. ‘Atharī refers to crops watered by rain, which the common people call al-‘idhy. Alternatively, it is said to refer to crops for which rainwater is collected into channels through which the water reaches them.

Abū ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-Barr said:

Among the lessons of the first hadith are that zakat is obligatory on the stated amount and that none is due on anything below it. A dhaw d is a group of three to ten camels. An ūqiyyah denotes a weight of forty dirhams; a nashsh is half an ūqiyyah; and a nawāh is five dirhams, as stated by Abū ‘Ubayd al-Qāsim ibn Sallām.

As for anything exceeding two hundred dirhams—the equivalent of five ūqiyyahs—the apparent meaning of this hadith is that zakat is obligatory on it, since the text does not exempt the excess, whereas it explicitly exempts anything below that amount. This establishes the obligation of zakat on five ūqiyyahs and above. This is the position of most scholars. It is related from ‘Alī and Ibn ‘Umar, and is the view of Mālik, al-Thawrī, al-Awzā‘ī, al-Layth, Ibn Abī Laylā, al-Shāfi‘ī, Abū Yūsuf, Muhammad, Ahmad, Ishāq, Abū ‘Ubayd, and Abū Thawr.

Another group held that no zakat is due on the excess until it reaches forty dirhams, or, in the case of gold, four dinars. This is related from ‘Umar and was the view of Sa‘īd, al-Hasan, Tāwūs, ‘Atā’, al-Zuhrī, Makhūl, ‘Amr ibn Dīnār

---

## Page 12460

and Abū Hanīfah. As for any amount exceeding five wasqs, all agree that zakat is due on it.

Section

“The zakat threshold for silver” is two hundred dirhams, according to this hadith, which states:

“Five ūqiyyahs of silver.”

There is consensus on this. The hadith of Anas in the two Sahīh collections also states:

“On silver, one quarter of a tenth is due.”

As for “the zakat threshold for gold,” Mālik said in al-Muwatta’: “The established Sunnah, concerning which there is no disagreement among us, is that zakat is obligatory on twenty dinars, just as it is obligatory on two hundred dirhams.”

Thus Mālik reported the consensus of the people of Medina. No disagreement has been reported except from al-Hasan, who said: “Nothing is due on gold until it reaches forty mithqāls.” Ibn al-Mundhir transmitted this. As for the hadith related on this matter, it is weak.

If an amount below twenty dinars is worth less than two hundred dirhams, there is consensus that no zakat is due on it. If it is less than twenty dinars but is worth two hundred dirhams, some scholars of the early generations held that zakat is due on it.

The Quran and hadith establish that zakat is obligatory on gold, just as it is on silver. Allah Most High says:

“And those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend it in

---

## Page 12461

the way of Allah”—to the end of the verse. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There is no owner of gold or silver who does not pay its zakat…”

The hadith will be cited later, Allah willing. This applies equally whether the gold and silver are minted as dinars and dirhams or are unminted.

Section

Should gold and silver be combined to reach the zakat threshold, making zakat payable, or not? There are six opinions:

One opinion is that neither is combined with the other. This is the view of al-Shāfiʿī and is also reported from Sharīk and al-Hasan ibn Sālih.

Another opinion is that gold is added to silver because it is subsidiary, but silver is not added to gold because silver is the basis.

Another opinion permits combining them, provided that the smaller amount is treated as subordinate to the larger. This is the view of al-Shaʿbī and al-Awzāʿī.

Another opinion is that they are combined according to their value. This is the view of Abū Hanīfah and al-Thawrī.

Another opinion is that they are combined according to the proportion each represents of its respective zakat threshold. This is the view of al-Hasan, Qatādah and al-Nakhaʿī

---

## Page 12462

and this is the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah’s disciple, Abū Yūsuf. According to them, anyone who possesses ten dinars and one hundred dirhams must pay zakat. If, however, the ten dinars are worth one hundred and fifty dirhams and he also possesses fifty dirhams, zakat is not obligatory, because, for zakat purposes, one dinar counts as ten dirhams. The two are combined according to their respective proportions of the zakat threshold, not their value.

Section

The passage of a lunar year is a condition for zakat to become obligatory on gold and silver and on livestock. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to send out his zakat collectors every year. The caliphs followed this practice with livestock and gold and silver, based on their knowledge of his Sunnah. Mālik narrated in his Muwattaʾ from Abū Bakr al-Siddīq, ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar that they said:

“This is the month when your zakat is due.”

They also said:

“No zakat is obligatory on wealth until a lunar year has passed over it.”

Abū ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Barr said: This has also been reported from ʿAlī and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd. It is the position of the jurists, past and present, apart from what has been reported from Muʿāwiyah and Ibn ʿAbbās, as noted earlier.

Anyone who acquires gold or silver amounting to the zakat threshold and retains ownership of it for a lunar year must pay zakat on it. If he acquires less than the threshold and subsequently acquires enough to complete it, the year for the first amount is reckoned from the acquisition of the second. Thus, the reckoning begins on the day the threshold is reached. If he acquires

---

## Page 12463

an amount reaching the zakat threshold and then, after some time, acquires another such amount, the lunar year is reckoned separately for each. Profits are combined with the principal, and zakat on them is paid when the principal’s lunar year ends, provided that the principal itself reaches the threshold, according to the majority. If the principal is below the threshold but, together with its profits, reaches it at the end of the lunar year, zakat is due according to Mālik, may Allah have mercy on him. If someone owns merchandise for trade and then acquires enough to complete the threshold, he must pay zakat.

Section

Zakat is due on merchandise held for trade. Ibn al-Mundhir said:

“The scholars unanimously agree that zakat is due on merchandise intended for trade once a lunar year has passed over it.”

This has been reported from ʿUmar, his son and Ibn ʿAbbās. It is also the position of the seven jurists, al-Hasan, Jābir ibn Zayd, Maymūn ibn Mihrān, Tāwūs, al-Nakhaʿī, al-Thawrī, al-Awzāʿī, Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad, Ishāq and Abū ʿUbayd. It has been related from Mālik and Dāwūd that no zakat is due on it.

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, Samurah said:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to instruct us to pay zakat on what we prepared for sale.”

It is reported that Himās said:

“ʿUmar passed by me and said, ‘Pay zakat on your wealth.’ I replied, ‘All I have is quivers and hides.’ He said, ‘Assess their value, then pay zakat on them.’”

This account became widely known without anyone objecting, so it constitutes a consensus.

---

## Page 12464

As for Mālik, his position is that traders fall into two categories: those who hold their stock awaiting a market and those who trade continuously.

The first is someone who buys goods and waits for a market for them; the goods may remain with him for years. According to Mālik, no zakat is due from him until he sells the goods, at which point he pays zakat for one year. His reasoning is that zakat was prescribed on wealth that grows. If the trader pays zakat on the goods every year—even though they may be unsaleable—their value will fall below their purchase price, causing him loss. If zakat is paid when they are sold, then any profit they have yielded was latent in them, and he pays zakat on it. He pays no zakat until he sells for an amount reaching the zakat threshold. Thereafter, he pays zakat on whatever he sells, whether much or little.

The second is someone who sells goods throughout the lunar year, so that no stock remains permanently in his possession. He pays zakat annually on everything: he designates a particular month in which he calculates the value of the goods and cash he holds, together with debts owed to him by solvent, trustworthy debtors, and pays zakat on the total. This applies provided that cash comes into his hands during the year, even a single dirham. If he never sells for cash at all, then, according to Mālik, no zakat is due from him.

Section

As for “jewelry,” if it is for women, no zakat is due on it according to Mālik, al-Layth, al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and Abū ʿUbayd. This is also reported from ʿĀ’ishah and Asmā’

---

## Page 12465

and Ibn 'Umar, Anas and Jābir—may Allah be pleased with them—and from a number of the Successors.

Another view is that zakat is due on it. This is reported from 'Umar, Ibn Mas'ūd, Ibn 'Abbās, Ibn 'Umar and a number of the Successors. It is the position of Abū Hanīfah, al-Thawrī and al-Awzā'ī.

As for men’s ornaments, no zakat is due on those that are permissible, such as sword ornaments and silver rings. Zakat is due, however, on items that are forbidden to possess, such as vessels. Where the permissibility of ornamentation is disputed—as with belts, helmets, cuirasses and similar items—there is also disagreement over zakat. According to Mālik and al-Shāfi'ī, zakat is due on these, and possessing them is impermissible. Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad permit them when the ornamentation is silver.

As for adornments for horses—for saddles, bridles and non-Arabian horses—zakat is due on these according to the majority of scholars. Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad prohibit possessing them. Likewise, according to the majority, zakat is due on inkwells, kohl containers and similar items, whether made of silver or gold.

Section

Zakat is obligatory on the wealth of orphans according to Mālik, al-Layth, al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad and Abū Thawr. This is also reported from 'Umar, 'Ā'ishah, 'Alī, Ibn 'Umar and Jābir—may Allah be pleased with them. 'Umar said:

“Trade with the wealth of orphans

---

## Page 12466

so that zakat does not consume it.” 'Ā'ishah said the same. This is also reported from al-Hasan ibn 'Alī, and is the position of 'Atā', Jābir ibn Zayd, Mujāhid and Ibn Sīrīn.

Section

Property taken by force, lost property and the like.

Mālik said that no zakat is due on it until its owner recovers it, whereupon he pays zakat for one year. Likewise, in his view, zakat on a debt owed to someone is not paid until he receives repayment; he then pays zakat on it once. Mālik’s position is also reported from al-Hasan, 'Atā' and 'Umar ibn 'Abd al-'Azīz.

Another view is that, upon recovering it, he pays zakat for each of the preceding years. Al-Shāfi'ī has two positions on the matter.

Section

Mineral deposits: when someone extracts the minimum amount of gold or silver liable to zakat, zakat becomes due upon extraction, according to Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad. Ahmad also includes rubies, peridot, rock crystal, agate, kohl, sabaj (black ornamental stone) and zarnīkh.

According to Ishāq and Ibn al-Mundhir, a full lunar year must elapse from its acquisition before zakat is paid. Abū Hanīfah holds that one-fifth is due on it. He also has a position that this payment is due only on malleable materials, such as iron, lead and copper, and not on others.

---

## Page 12467

As for what is taken from the sea, such as pearls and coral, the majority hold that no zakat is due on it. Others hold that zakat is due: this is the position of al-Zuhrī and al-Hasan al-Basrī, and one reported position of Ahmad.

Section

Debt cancels the obligation of zakat on monetary wealth according to Mālik, Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad, and according to one of al-Shāfi'ī’s two positions. This is also the position of 'Atā', al-Hasan, Sulaymān ibn Yasār, Maymūn ibn Mihrān, al-Nakha'ī, al-Thawrī, al-Awzā'ī, al-Layth, Ishāq and Abū Thawr.

They cite as evidence what Mālik relates in al-Muwatta' from al-Sā'ib ibn Yazīd, who said: I heard 'Uthmān—may Allah be pleased with him—say:

“This is the month for your zakat. Whoever owes a debt should repay it, so that your wealth is free of debt and you may pay zakat from it.”

According to Mālik, if a person has non-monetary assets sufficient to cover the debt, he leaves his monetary wealth intact and sets those assets against the debt. These are the assets that a judge would sell to settle the debt: whatever exceeds his essential needs. If he is himself owed a debt by a solvent, trustworthy person, he likewise sets it against his own debt and pays zakat on his monetary wealth. If, however, he has nothing beyond the wealth in his possession, the obligation of zakat lapses.

Section

There is disagreement over whether zakat is due on honey. The scholars of Medina differed on this matter.

---

## Page 12468

Thus al-Zuhrī held that zakat is due on honey. This is also the position of al-Awzā'ī, Abū Hanīfah and his companions, Rabī'ah and Yahyā ibn Sa'īd. The amount due is one tenth. According to Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad, however, no zakat is due on it.

Section

As for the second hadith, namely the Prophet’s statement—may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“On what is watered by rain and springs, one tenth is due.”

—and the remainder of the hadith—it establishes a point on which the scholars agree: the proportion collected from produce subject to the tithe. They differed, however, over which kinds of produce are subject to one tenth or one twentieth.

One group held that one tenth is due on all grains and vegetables cultivated by people, and on the fruit produced through their commercial activities, whether the quantity is small or large. This position is reported from Hammād ibn Abī Sulaymān, Abū Hanīfah and Zufar.

Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad said that it is due only on crops whose yield can be stored, provided the quantity reaches five wasqs.

Ahmad said that one tenth is due on produce that can be dried and stored, is measured by volume, and amounts to five wasqs or more. For him, it makes no difference whether it is a staple food, such as wheat, barley, rice and dhurah (a cereal); a pulse, such as broad beans and lentils; a spice, such as coriander, cumin and caraway; or seeds such as flaxseed

---

## Page 12469

and sesame, as well as all other grains. In his view, zakat is also due on produce combining these characteristics, such as dates, raisins, almonds, hazelnuts and pistachios. It is not due on fruits or vegetables. This is also the position of Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad. The view of Ibn Habīb of the Mālikī school is similar.

He held the same view as Mālik but went further, saying:

“Zakat is collected on all tree fruits, whether they can be stored or not.”

He also said:

“If a man’s crop of a single kind is estimated to amount to five wasqs, and it is a kind that can be dried—such as walnuts, almonds or pistachios—he pays one tenth of it. If it is a kind that cannot be dried, such as pomegranates, apples, peaches, quinces and similar fruits, and its estimated volume while fresh reaches five wasqs, zakat becomes due on it: if he sells it, he pays one tenth of the sale price; if he does not sell it, he pays one tenth of its estimated volume.”

According to the well-known position of Mālik and his companions, zakat is due on wheat, barley, sult (hull-less barley), dhurah, millet, rice, chickpeas, lentils, julubbān (a pulse), rashsh, bisillah, sesame, mung beans, radish seeds and similar edible grains that can be stored.

It is also due on three kinds of fruit: dates, raisins and olives.

---

## Page 12470

Al-Shāfiʿī said:

“Zakat is due on produce that can be dried and stored and serves as a staple food, whether eaten as it is, cooked, or made into roasted grain flour.”

He held two opinions concerning olives. In his view, zakat is due on dates and raisins.

Al-Layth ibn Saʿd said:

“Zakat is due on anything from which bread is made.”

He also held that zakat is due on dates, raisins and olives. Al-Thawrī likewise held that zakat is due on olives, as did al-Awzāʿī and al-Zuhrī. This is also reported from Ibn ʿAbbās.

Al-Awzāʿī said:

“The established Sunnah is that zakat is due on wheat, barley, sult (hull-less barley), dates, grapes and olives.”

Ishāq said:

“Zakat is due on anything from which bread is made.”

According to Ibn al-Mundhir, zakat is due only on nine things, as mentioned earlier: dates, raisins, wheat, barley, silver, gold, camels, cattle, and sheep and goats.

All these scholars apply the five-wasq threshold, except that Mujāhid and Abū Hanīfah are reported to require zakat even on a small amount. They also require grain to be dried and cleaned, and fruit to be dried. As for olives that yield no oil, grapes that cannot be made into raisins, and fresh dates that cannot be made into dried dates, zakat is paid from their sale proceeds or from the produce itself. Mālik said: “If it amounts to five wasqs and is sold, zakat is paid from its sale proceeds.”

---

## Page 12471

Section

Wheat, barley and sult (hull-less barley) are combined for zakat purposes, and al-qatāfī are combined with one another. The crops of a single year are combined, even if some are summer crops and others winter crops. The same applies to fruit, even when grown in different regions, provided it belongs to one owner. As for joint owners, each owner’s share must reach the zakat threshold.

Section

A wasq equals sixty sāʿs, and a sāʿ equals four mudds measured by the mudd of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace (a mudd equals five and one-third Baghdadi ratls, and a Baghdadi ratl equals twenty-eight dirhams) (*).

The dirhams meant here are those dating from the time of ʿAbd al-Malik: every ten weigh seven mithqāls. Thus, the zakat threshold amounts to one thousand six hundred Baghdadi ratls. In Damascene measure, it amounts to three hundred and forty-two ratls and six-sevenths of a ratl.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 202):
> An omission occurred here. The correct wording is: “The Baghdadi ratl (a unit of weight) equals one hundred and twenty-eight dirhams and four-sevenths of a dirham,” as the Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—stated in vol. 25, p. 51.

---

## Page 12472

Section

If someone sells fruit, gives it away, or dies leaving it after it has begun to ripen, he remains liable for its zakat. If this occurs before it has begun to ripen, the zakat falls upon the purchaser, recipient or heir, provided each person’s share reaches the zakat threshold.

The yield of date palms and grapevines is estimated for zakat, and their owners are then left free to dispose of it: they may eat it or sell it as they wish. Allowances should be made for them. Whatever is eaten of grain crops or pulses while still green and immature is not subject to zakat.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When you estimate the yield, leave out one-third; if you do not leave out one-third, then leave out one-quarter.”

Reported by Abū Dāwūd.

He also said:

“Be lenient with people, for the produce must provide for passersby and those who eat from it, as well as gifts of fruit.”

Reported by Abū ʿUbayd. He explained:

“Passersby” refers to wayfarers, so called because they tread through fruit-growing lands on their journeys. “Gifts of fruit” refers to giving someone the fruit of one or more date palms to eat. “Those who eat from it” refers to the owners of the produce, who eat some of it.

Section

Zakat is due only on five wasqs of a single category. According to Mālik, wheat, barley and sult (hull-less barley) constitute a single category. Thus, if the combined amount of these three

---

## Page 12473

reaches the zakat threshold, zakat becomes due, and the amount due is taken proportionately from each.

The same applies to pulses: chickpeas, broad beans, lentils and similar crops constitute a single category according to him. The rate collected depends on the labour and expense involved, as stated in the hadith:

“On crops irrigated by rainwater, rivers or springs, one-tenth is due. On crops irrigated by drawing water, by water-drawing camels or by waterwheels—these are names for the same thing; sāniya and nādih refer to camels used to draw drinking water—half of one-tenth is due. Where half is irrigated by one method and half by the other, or each method is used for half the year, three-quarters of one-tenth is due.”

Section

Whoever owns a crop as it grows must pay its zakat. Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, spend from the good things you have earned and from what We have brought forth for you from the earth. Do not deliberately select inferior things to give, when you yourselves would not accept them.”

To the end of the verse.

This applies whether he owns the land, rents it, has been granted its use by the ruler to benefit from its yield, borrows it, or holds it as the beneficiary of an endowment.

Ibn al-Mundhir said:

“All the scholars whose views I have recorded agree that any land whose inhabitants embraced Islam before being subdued remains theirs, and that they must pay zakat on what they grow there.”

Land held under a peace treaty is thus as he described. The same applies to land conquered by force if

---

## Page 12474

it is subject to land tax, he pays that tax and pays zakat on what remains.

Whoever rents land for cultivation must pay zakat on its produce according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī, Ahmad, Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad. Likewise, holders of land grants must pay the tithe. If the entire crop belongs to the grantee and he pays the farmer a wage, the entire tithe is due from him. If the crop is shared, with one-half or one-third belonging to the farmer and one-half or one-third to the grantee, each must pay the tithe on his own share, since he owned that share while it was growing. This is the position of the scholars of Islam.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, used to collect the tithe from the Companions and distribute it to those entitled to it, and he commanded them to wage jihad with what remained of their wealth. If soldiers have been given resources from the public treasury with which to wage jihad, they are all the more obliged to pay the tithe on them. Thus, when the ruler grants someone land to derive revenue from and support jihad, and he cultivates it and owns the growing crop on land subject to the tithe, no scholar holds that he owes no tithe.

The scholars have differed concerning someone who acquires the right to use land in exchange for compensation, such as someone who rents it for dirhams, his own services or something similar. The majority say that he must pay the tithe. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa’s two companions, Mālik, al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad. Abū Hanīfa, however, says that the tithe is due from the landowner. Thus, if these holders of land grants are assumed to have been hired in return for the use of the land and to have offered

---

## Page 12475

their own services, then according to the majority they must pay the tithe, while according to the other view it is due from the one who hired them.

Anyone who says that the tithe Allah has prescribed for those entitled to zakat is waived has contradicted scholarly consensus. Moreover, these soldiers are not like hired workers. Rather, they are Allah’s soldiers, fighting His servants in His cause. They receive these stipends from the public treasury to help them wage jihad. What they receive does not belong to the ruler; it is Allah’s wealth, which the ruler distributes among those entitled to it. Whoever regards them as hired workers regards their jihad as being for someone other than Allah.

It is related in a hadith:

“Those of my community who go forth to fight and accept what they are given are like the mother of Moses, who nurses her son and receives her wages.”

Section

If the owner of crops and fruit owes a debt, does this waive the obligation of zakat? There are three opinions on this.

One view is that it is not waived under any circumstances. This is the position of Mālik, al-Awzā'ī and al-Shāfi'ī, and one position reported from Ahmad.

Another view is that debt waives it. This is the position of 'Atā', al-Hasan and Sulaymān ibn Yasār

---

## Page 12476

and Maymūn ibn Mihrān, al-Nakha'ī, al-Layth, al-Thawrī and Ishāq. The same applies to livestock: camels, cattle, sheep and goats.

Another view is that debt incurred for expenditure on one’s crops and fruit waives the obligation of zakat, whereas debt incurred to support one’s family does not. Yet another view is that both kinds of debt waive it. The former is the view of Ibn 'Abbās, adopted by Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others; the latter is the view of Ibn 'Umar.

Section

Regarding fresh dates that do not become dried dates, olives that are not pressed for oil, and grapes that are not made into raisins, Mālik and others said: Zakat is paid from their sale proceeds if the produce amounts to five wasqs, even if its price does not reach two hundred dirhams.

If, however, the produce is of a kind that reaches full maturity but is sold before doing so, one view is that zakat is paid from its sale proceeds; another is that it is paid from its grain or oil.

---

## Page 12477

Section

This, then, is the zakat on monetary wealth and agricultural produce established by the preceding hadiths and the noble Quranic verses. As for “zakat on livestock”—camels, cattle, sheep and goats—it is established by authentic hadiths. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) issued written instructions concerning it, as did Abū Bakr, ʿUmar and other Companions. In the Sahīh, Anas ibn Mālik reports—the wording here is al-Bukhārī’s—that Abū Bakr wrote him the following document when he sent him to Bahrain:

“In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

“This is the obligatory zakat prescribed by the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) for the Muslims, and commanded by Allah and His Messenger. Any Muslim who is asked to pay it as prescribed should do so; whoever is asked for more should not pay the excess.

“For 24 camels or fewer, one sheep or goat is due for every five camels. When the number reaches 25 to 35, one one-year-old she-camel is due. When it reaches 36 to 45, one two-year-old she-camel is due. When it reaches 46 to 60, one three-year-old she-camel fit for mating with a male camel is due. When it reaches 61 to 75, one four-year-old she-camel is due. When it reaches 76 to 90, two two-year-old she-camels are due. When it reaches 91 to 120, two three-year-old she-camels are due

---

## Page 12478

, both fit for mating with a male camel.

“If the number exceeds 120, one two-year-old she-camel is due for every forty, and one three-year-old she-camel for every fifty. Whoever has only four camels owes nothing on them unless their owner wishes to give voluntarily. When their number reaches five, one sheep or goat is due.

“As for zakat on grazing sheep and goats: from forty to 120, one sheep or goat is due. Above 120 up to 200, two are due. Above 200 up to 300, three are due. Above 300, one is due for every hundred. If a man’s grazing flock falls short of forty by one, no zakat is due unless its owner wishes to give voluntarily.

“On silver money, one-fortieth is due. If it amounts to only 190 dirhams, nothing is due unless its owner wishes to give voluntarily.”

Anas also reported the following from this same document:

“If a man’s camels reach the number at which a four-year-old she-camel is due as zakat, but he has no four-year-old she-camel and has a three-year-old instead, the three-year-old shall be accepted from him. Along with it, he shall give two sheep or goats, if readily available to him, or twenty dirhams. If a three-year-old she-camel is due as zakat, but he has no three-year-old she-camel and has a four-year-old instead, the four-year-old shall be accepted from him, and the zakat collector shall give him twenty dirhams or two sheep or goats. If a three-year-old she-camel is due as zakat, but he has only a two-year-old she-camel, the two-year-old shall be accepted from him, and he shall give two sheep or goats or twenty dirhams. If a two-year-old she-camel is due as zakat and he has a three-year-old she-camel, it shall be accepted from him, and the zakat collector shall give him

---

## Page 12479

twenty dirhams or two sheep or goats. If a two-year-old she-camel is due as zakat, but he has none and has a one-year-old she-camel instead, the one-year-old shall be accepted from him, together with twenty dirhams or two sheep or goats. Separate herds must not be combined, nor combined herds divided, for fear of zakat. Joint owners shall settle their respective shares equitably between themselves. No decrepit or defective animal, nor a male goat, shall be taken as zakat unless the zakat collector so wishes.”

Anas also reported from this same document:

“If a one-year-old she-camel is due as zakat, but he has none and has a two-year-old she-camel instead, it shall be accepted from him, and the zakat collector shall give him twenty dirhams or two sheep or goats. If he has no suitable one-year-old she-camel but has a two-year-old male camel, it shall be accepted from him, with nothing further due.”

Mālik ibn Anas related the document of Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, in his Muwatta, with this wording or something close to it, except that he did not mention substitution accompanied by twenty dirhams.

Section

Imam Abū Bakr ibn al-Mundhir said:

There is consensus on this up to 120 camels. The report attributed to Alī that five sheep or goats are due on twenty-five camels is not authentic.

---

## Page 12480

And his wording in this hadith,

“Concerning grazing sheep and goats,”

is a point of disagreement among the scholars, since grazing animals are those that feed on pasture. Mālik’s position is that working camels, working cattle, and fodder-fed rams are subject to zakat.

Abū Umar said: “This is also the view of al-Layth, and I know of no one other than these two who held it.”

As for al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Abū Hanīfa, al-Thawrī, al-Awzā‘ī and others, they held that no zakat is due on such animals. This was also reported from a number of the Companions: Alī, Jābir and Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal. Umar ibn Abd al-Azīz issued written instructions to this effect.

In the hadith narrated by Bahz ibn Hakīm, from his father, from his grandfather, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“For grazing camels, one two-year-old she-camel is due for every forty.”

He thus restricted the ruling to grazing animals. Unqualified wording is understood in light of qualified wording when both concern the same category, without disagreement. The same applies to the hadith of Abū Bakr concerning grazing sheep and goats.

As for his words,

“If the zakat due on a man’s camels is a four-year-old she-camel, but he has none,”

and the remainder of the passage, Mālik did not adopt this ruling. Rather, he said that if an animal of the required age—whether a four-year-old she-camel or otherwise—is unavailable, he must purchase one: “I do not favor his paying its price instead.” He also said: “If the animal of the age due as zakat on the livestock is unavailable, the collector shall take neither an older nor a younger animal, nor accept additional dirhams. The owner must purchase for him an animal of the required age.”

Al-Thawrī, al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad adopted the ruling given in the hadith: that if he does not

---

## Page 12481

find an animal of the required age, he takes one of those available and gives two sheep or goats or twenty dirhams, or receives the same, as stated in the hadith. The position of Abū Hanīfa and his two disciples is that he may take its monetary value or, if he wishes, take a better animal and pay the difference. Mālik did not adopt this ruling because he transmitted only Umar’s document, which does not contain the additional provisions found in Abū Bakr’s document. Such is the way of scholars.

As for his words in this hadith:

“When their number exceeds one hundred and twenty, one two-year-old she-camel is due for every forty, and one three-year-old she-camel for every fifty.”

Abū Umar said that this is a point of disagreement, meaning when the number increases by one. Mālik said: “When their number exceeds one hundred and twenty by one, the zakat collector may choose between taking two three-year-old she-camels and three two-year-old she-camels.” Al-Zuhrī said: “Three two-year-old she-camels are due until the number reaches one hundred and thirty, when one three-year-old she-camel and two two-year-old she-camels are due.” This was also the view of al-Awzā‘ī, al-Shāfi‘ī, Abū Thawr and Abū Ubayd, as well as Muhammad ibn Ishāq. It is the position of the leading scholars of the Hijaz and is the view favored by the scholars.

As for the position of the Kufan scholars, the assessment starts afresh for the number exceeding one hundred and twenty, with one sheep or goat due for every five camels.

---

## Page 12482

Section

His words:

“No aged animal, defective animal, or male goat is to be taken as zakat.”

This is the position of the jurists of the major centres, because what is taken as zakat should be of fair quality. As Umar—may Allah be pleased with him—said: “A fair specimen from the livestock of fair and choice quality.”

“Aged” means a sheep or goat advanced in years. With the pronunciation ʿawār, the phrase refers to an animal with a defect; with the pronunciation ʿuwār, it refers to one that has lost an eye. Such animals do not suffice for zakat. The sheep or goat taken as zakat on camels must be of the requisite age: a jadhaʿa in the case of sheep and a thaniyya in the case of goats. If its monetary value is paid instead, there are two opinions.

His words:

“Separate herds must not be combined, nor combined herds separated, for fear of zakat.”

This means separating or combining livestock for fear of zakat. There is disagreement over whether this addresses the owners, the zakat collector, or both. This concerns combined herds: their owners may owe a certain number of sheep or goats, but separating the herds would reduce the number due. Alternatively, a certain number may be due on separate herds, but combining them would reduce it. They were therefore forbidden to do this.

There are various examples of this: three people may each own forty sheep or goats, so that three animals are due altogether; if their herds are combined, only one becomes due. Or two men may own two hundred sheep or goats

---

## Page 12483

and two, each owning one hundred and one. They jointly owe three sheep or goats, but if their herds are separated, each owes only one—and similarly in other cases.

His words:

“The two owners of a combined herd shall settle between themselves equitably.”

This means that if a sheep or goat is taken from the flock of one of the two owners, he is entitled to reimbursement from the other for the value of the latter’s share of the liability.

Section

His words in the hadith:

“For grazing sheep and goats, when their number reaches forty, one sheep or goat is due, up to one hundred and twenty. If their number exceeds that, two are due, up to two hundred. If their number exceeds two hundred, three are due, up to three hundred. If their number exceeds three hundred, one is due for every hundred.”

This, too, is agreed upon concerning zakat on sheep and goats, and sheep and goats are treated alike. Grazing is a condition for zakat to be obligatory, except according to Mālik and al-Layth—as noted earlier—for they require zakat even on livestock that do not graze. There is no disagreement among the jurists that sheep and goats are counted together for zakat. Likewise, camels of different varieties are counted together, as are cattle and buffalo.

---

## Page 12484

They differed, however, concerning animals of the same kind that vary in quality. One view is that the collector may take whichever he wishes; another is that he should take an animal of average quality.

Section

As for “zakat on cattle,” it is established that Mu‘ādh ibn Jabal—may Allah be pleased with him—reported:

“When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, sent him to Yemen, he instructed him to collect as zakat on cattle one yearling, male or female, from every thirty, and one two-year-old cow from every forty, and to collect one dinar in jizya (poll tax) from every male who had reached puberty.”

Ahmad, al-Nasā’ī and al-Tirmidhī narrated this through Masrūq, from Mu‘ādh. The same ruling appears in the document that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, wrote for ‘Amr ibn Hazm. Mālik also narrated it in his Muwatta’ through Tāwūs, from Mu‘ādh. Abū ‘Ubayd reported a consensus on this. The overwhelming majority of scholars hold that nothing is due on fewer than thirty cattle. It is reported from Sa‘īd and al-Zuhrī, however, that one sheep or goat is due on every five, as with camels.

One condition for zakat to be obligatory on cattle is that they graze, as indicated by the hadith narrated by ‘Amr ibn Shu‘ayb, from his father, from his grandfather, in which the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“No zakat is due on working animals.”

Abū Dāwūd narrated it. It is also reported that ‘Alī, Mu‘ādh and Jābir said: “No zakat is due on cattle used for work.” Mālik and al-Layth, however, hold that zakat is due on them.

---

## Page 12485

For every thirty cattle, a male is given, and for every forty, a female. If a male is given instead, does this discharge the obligation? There are two opinions. Ibn al-Qāsim said that it does, while Ashhab said that it does not. The latter is also the position of Ahmad and a number of other scholars. If the entire herd consists of males, however, an animal from among them is given. When the herd reaches one hundred and twenty, the owner may choose between giving three two-year-old cows and four male yearlings.

A yearling is an animal that has completed one year and entered its second. A two-year-old cow is one that has completed two years.

Section

“Buffalo” are treated like cattle; Ibn al-Mundhir reported a consensus on this. As for “wild cattle,” the majority hold that no zakat is due on them, though some hold that it is.

If an animal is born of a wild and a domestic parent, al-Shāfiʿī holds that no zakat is due, while Ahmad holds that it is subject to zakat. Mālik distinguishes between the mothers and the fathers: if the mothers are domestic, zakat is paid; otherwise, it is not.

The young of every category of livestock are counted along with the adults, but only average animals are taken. If all the animals are young, one view is that zakat is taken from among them; another is that the owner must purchase adult animals.

---

## Page 12486

Section

Owners who mingle their livestock are those whose animals remain distinguishable from one another’s. If their respective livestock are not distinguishable, they are joint owners. When two owners mingle their herds, they pay zakat as though the livestock constituted a single holding. For example, if each owns forty sheep, only one sheep is due on their combined herds, and they settle its cost between themselves.

Three conditions are stipulated for such mingling to count; according to another view, two conditions suffice, and according to another, just one. These concern sharing the bucket, watering trough, enclosure, overnight resting place, herdsman and breeding male.

Another view considers a shared herdsman alone sufficient, because he brings the two herds together, whereas the other things may be shared more widely.

Must each owner possess the minimum number subject to zakat for their mingling to count? Mālik held that this is required, while others held that it is not.

Section

If a person owns livestock that then reproduce, and the mothers constitute the minimum number subject to zakat, the majority hold that zakat is paid on the offspring along with their mothers, and that the offspring follow their mothers’ zakat year. If the mothers fall below that minimum,

---

## Page 12487

and they reproduce—even one day before the end of the zakat year—bringing the total up to that minimum, Mālik holds that zakat must be paid, with the offspring following their mothers’ zakat year. If a person exchanges a holding that meets the zakat threshold for another of the same kind, the replacement continues the original holding’s zakat year. If he uses gold or silver meeting the zakat threshold to purchase livestock likewise meeting the threshold, before a full zakat year has elapsed on the former, then, according to one of the two views, the livestock continue the zakat year of the gold or silver.

Section

The zakat of each locality is to be distributed there: the zakat of Syria in Syria, and that of Egypt in Egypt. May it be transferred for a beneficial purpose—for example, from Syria to the Prophet’s city, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or elsewhere? Scholars hold two views on this.

Mālik said: “There is no harm in transferring it when there is a need.” If the people of the locality are not eligible recipients, it may be transferred without disagreement.

When Muʿādh ibn Jabal transferred zakat from Yemen to Medina, ʿUmar objected, saying: “I did not send you as a revenue collector.”

He replied: “I found no one to receive it.”

According to al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, it is not to be transferred, whereas Mālik permits its transfer.

Section

As for “the distribution of zakat,” Allah has explained this in the Quran, saying:

“Zakat is only for the poor, the needy, those employed to administer it, and those whose hearts are to be reconciled

---

## Page 12488

and for freeing those in bondage, those in debt, Allah’s cause, and the stranded traveller—an obligation ordained by Allah. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

Imam Abū Jaʿfar al-Tabarī said:

“Most scholars hold that whoever administers its distribution may allocate it to whichever of the eight categories he wishes. Allah named these eight categories only to make it known that zakat must not go to anyone outside them, not to require its division among all eight categories.”

It was narrated through his chain of transmission from Hudhayfah and Ibn ʿAbbās that they both said: “If you wish, you may allocate it to one, two, or three categories.”

He said: “It was narrated that ʿUmar said: ‘Whichever category you give it to, you have fulfilled your obligation.’ It was also narrated concerning him that ʿUmar (1) would take the obligatory zakat and allocate it to a single category. This was also the view of Abū al-ʿĀliyah, Maymūn ibn Mihrān, and Ibrāhīm al-Nakhaʿī.”

He said: “Some later scholars held that a person must distribute it among six categories: since he distributes it himself, the zakat administrator is excluded, and those whose hearts are to be reconciled are also excluded.”

He said that the correct view is that Allah has assigned zakat to two purposes:

The first is meeting Muslims’ needs.

The second is supporting and strengthening Islam. Whatever serves to support Islam may be given to rich and poor alike, such as one fighting in Allah’s cause and others like him. Those whose hearts are to be reconciled are given zakat under this heading. As for what is intended to meet Muslims’ needs

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 12489

Shaykh al-Islām said:

Section

The Second Foundation: Zakat

In this matter too, they follow the Sunnah of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his caliphs, adopting the middle of the three views, or the best of them, concerning grazing livestock.

Regarding numbers of camels falling between the prescribed zakat thresholds, they adopted and followed the written directive of al-Siddīq, may Allah be pleased with him. It stipulates that, in large camel herds, one two-year-old she-camel is due for every forty, and one three-year-old she-camel for every fifty. This was the later of the two rulings issued by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, unlike the document stipulating that the assessment begins afresh after one hundred and twenty camels. That document predates this one, since the appointment of ʿAmr ibn Hazm to govern Najrān occurred some time before the Prophet’s death.

As for al-Siddīq’s document, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had written it but had not sent it out to the zakat collectors; it was Abū Bakr who sent it out.

Regarding agricultural produce subject to the tithe, they adopted a middle position between the scholars of the Hijāz and those of Iraq. For the scholars of

---

## Page 12490

Iraq, such as Abū Hanīfah, require the tithe on everything the earth produces, except reeds and the like, whether the yield is small or large. This is based on the view that the tithe is a levy on the land, like the land tax. Accordingly, they do not impose both the tithe and the land tax together. The scholars of the Hijāz, however, require the tithe only when the prescribed minimum of five wasqs is reached. Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad agreed with them on this. Of fruits, they require it only on dates and raisins, and of crops, only on staple foods. They do not require it on honey or anything else. Al-Shāfi‘ī follows the position of the scholars of the Hijāz.

As for Ahmad and the other jurists of hadith, they agree with the scholars of the Hijāz concerning the minimum threshold, because of the authentic reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stating: “No zakat is due on less than five wasqs.” They do not require zakat on vegetables, since the practice of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his caliphs was not to collect it, and because of the report transmitted from him. Ahmad does, however, require it on grains and fruits that can be stored, even if they are neither dates nor raisins, such as pistachios and hazelnuts. He thus treats durability in produce subject to the tithe as analogous to the passage of a year in the case of livestock and the threshing floor. A distinction is therefore made between vegetables and produce that can be stored.

Goods measured by weight may also be included alongside those measured by volume, such as cotton, according to one of the two positions reported from him, on the basis of reports from the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them. He also requires zakat on honey because of the reports concerning it that he himself collected, even though others received them only through a weak chain of transmission, and in order to accord equal treatment to the kinds of things Allah sends down from

---

## Page 12491

the sky and what He brings forth from the earth. They require both the tithe and the land tax, since the tithe is a due on the crop, while the land tax is a due on the land. Abū Hanīfah’s two companions hold the same position as Ahmad, or one close to it.

As for the measures of the sāʿ and the mudd, there are three opinions:

The first is that a sāʿ is five and one-third ratls, and a mudd is one-quarter of that. This is the position of the scholars of the Hijāz regarding both foodstuffs and water. The account of Mālik and Abū Yūsuf concerning this is well known. This is also the position of al-Shāfiʿī and many, or most, of Ahmad’s followers.

The second is that a sāʿ is eight ratls, and a mudd is one-quarter of that. This is the position of the scholars of Iraq in all these cases.

The third is that the sāʿ for foodstuffs is five and one-third ratls, while the sāʿ for ritual purification is eight ratls, as transmitted reports support each of these measures.

Thus, the sāʿ used for zakat, expiations, and zakat al-fitr is two-thirds of the sāʿ used for ritual bathing and ablution. This is the position of a group of Ahmad’s followers and others who reconciled the transmitted reports on this subject, as anyone who carefully considers the relevant reports can see.

---

## Page 12492

Among the basic principles governing zakat is that Abū Hanīfah takes a broader view of its obligation than others. He requires it on grazing horses that include āthār (traces) (1), and on all forms of gold and silver, including permissible jewelry and other items. He includes minerals and other buried wealth within rikāz, requiring a one-fifth levy on it. However, apart from zakat al-fitr and the tithe, he requires zakat only from a legally accountable person. He permits the use of stratagems to avoid its obligation. His followers differed over whether this is disliked: Muhammad regarded it as disliked, whereas Abū Yūsuf did not.

Mālik and al-Shāfi'ī agreed that legal accountability is not a condition for zakat to be obligatory, given the many reports from the Companions on this matter. Neither required it on horses, permissible jewelry, or the earth’s produce except for what was mentioned earlier. Mālik prohibited using stratagems to avoid zakat and maintained that it remained obligatory despite such stratagems. Al-Shāfi'ī regarded using stratagems to avoid it as disliked.

Ahmad’s position on the scope of its obligation lies between those of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik, as explained earlier regarding produce subject to the tithe. He requires zakat on the wealth of both legally accountable persons and those who are not legally accountable. Different positions are reported from him concerning permissible jewelry, although the position upheld by his followers is that zakat is not obligatory on it. His position on stratagems is the same as Mālik’s: he prohibits using stratagems to evade its obligation

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 12493

and holds that it remains obligatory despite such stratagems, as indicated by Surah Nūn and other evidence. The four imams and the rest of the Muslim community—with only isolated exceptions—agree that zakat is obligatory on trade goods, whether the merchant is resident or travelling, and whether he holds stock—buying goods when prices are low and storing them until prices rise—or trades continuously, like shopkeepers. This applies whether the merchandise consists of new or used textiles; food, whether staples, fruit, accompaniments eaten with bread, or anything else; vessels, such as earthenware and the like; or living property, such as slaves, horses, mules, donkeys, fodder-fed sheep and goats, or anything else. Trade goods constitute most of the non-apparent wealth of town dwellers, just as livestock constitute most of the apparent wealth.

Section

Ownership is a prerequisite for zakat, but they disagreed over possession. They hold three views concerning zakat on property not in one’s possession, such as a debt:

The first is that zakat is obligatory on every debt and every tangible asset, even if it is not in the possession of

---

## Page 12494

its owner, such as property that has been wrongfully seized or lost, or a debt that is denied or owed by someone insolvent or delaying payment. Zakat must be paid without delay on property that can be recovered, such as a debt owed by a solvent person. This is one of al-Shāfiʿī’s two views, and the stronger of them.

Section

There are three views concerning payment of the equivalent value in zakat:

The first is that it is valid in all circumstances, as Abū Hanīfa held.

The second is that it is not valid under any circumstances, as al-Shāfiʿī held.

The third is that it is valid only when there is a need—for example, when someone owes a sheep or goat as zakat on his camels but does not have one, or when someone sells his grapes and fresh dates before they dry. This is Ahmad’s explicitly stated position: he prohibited payment of the equivalent value but allowed it in certain cases because of need. Some of his followers, however, transmitted from him that it was permissible. They therefore treated payment of the equivalent value as an issue on which two positions were reported from him, and preferred prohibition, since that was his well-known position, corresponding to al-Shāfiʿī’s view.

This view is the most balanced of the views, as we have explained in a similar discussion concerning prayer. The evidence requiring payment of the specified item itself, whether based on explicit texts or analogy, is like all other evidence establishing an obligation. It is well known that the benefit served by requiring the specified item may sometimes be outweighed by the greater benefit of paying its equivalent value and by the hardship involved in providing the item itself—a hardship that Islamic law removes.

---

## Page 12495

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman’s dower remains owed by her husband for successive years, but she cannot demand it for fear that doing so will cause them to separate. She then accepts real estate in lieu of her dower, or receives the dower after several years. Must she pay zakat for the past years, or is zakat not due until a year has elapsed from the time she received it?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah.

The scholars hold several views on this issue:

One view is that zakat must be paid for the past years, whether the husband is solvent or insolvent. This is one of the two views in the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad, and a group of their followers have defended it.

Another view is that it is due if he is solvent and she is able to collect it, but not if collection cannot be secured. This is the other view in their schools.

Another view is that zakat is due for one year only. This is Mālik’s view and one view in Ahmad’s school.

Another view is that it is not due under any circumstances. This is Abū Hanīfa’s view and one view in Ahmad’s school.

---

## Page 12496

The weakest of these views is that zakat is due for past years even when one is unable to collect the debt. This view is invalid: it is inconceivable under the Sharia that they should be entitled to collect anything when the owner has received nothing. Moreover, if enough time passes, the zakat would exceed the wealth itself.

Furthermore, if the qualifying wealth diminishes and zakat is held to be due from that wealth itself, the amount payable could only be determined through a lengthy calculation of a kind that the Sharia could not prescribe.

The most plausible views are that nothing is due in any circumstances until the wealth has been held for a year, or that a single year’s zakat is due upon receipt. Each of these views has merit. The former is Abū Hanīfa’s view, and the latter is Mālik’s; both have also been held within Ahmad’s school. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man owns camels and buys pasture for them during the grazing season. Is zakat due on them?

He answered:

If they graze for most of the year—for example, if he buys pasture for them for three or four months—he must pay zakat on them. This is the stronger of the scholars’ two views.

---

## Page 12497

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

If a flock numbers forty animals, whether young or adult, zakat is due on them once a year has elapsed. If they initially numbered fewer than forty but number forty at the end of the year, there is disagreement on this; the more cautious course is to pay zakat. And Allah knows best.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man owns a flock that has not reached the minimum threshold for zakat. Does zakat become due on it during the course of the year?

He answered:

The scholars hold two views on this issue, both reported from Ahmad:

The first is that the year begins when the flock reaches forty, as al-Shāfiʿī held.

The second is that the year begins when he acquired the dams, as Mālik held. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12498

He was also asked:

A village inhabited by farmers is divided into two halves. One farmer in one half owns enough sheep for zakat to be due, while the farmers in the other half do not own enough sheep for zakat to be due. The imam has required the village’s farmers to pay zakat on the sheep. Is it due from those whose flocks reach the minimum threshold? If it is due from them, may the imam collect it from those whose flocks do not reach that threshold?

He answered:

If the amount demanded is what Allah has prescribed for those liable for zakat, they alone must pay it. But if the amount demanded unjustly exceeds what is obligatory, all should share in paying it in proportion to their wealth. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12499

Chapter: Zakat on the Produce of the Land

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What tithe is due on grain, and in what amount? Is it payable by the owner, the farmer, or both?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

The minimum threshold is five wasqs. One wasq is sixty sāʿs according to the measure of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. When Baghdad was founded, the imams determined that the Prophet’s sāʿ equalled five and one-third Iraqi ratls as measured at that time. Thus, the threshold amounts to one thousand six hundred Iraqi ratls.

At that time, the Iraqi ratl was ninety mithqāls: one hundred and twenty-eight dirhams and four-sevenths of a dirham. Its weight was subsequently increased to one hundred and thirty dirhams, then increased again to one hundred and forty-four. Some later jurists mistakenly supposed that one or the other of these was the ratl the imams had used in determining that measure.

Accordingly, its amount in Damascene ratls, each equivalent to six hundred dirhams, is

---

## Page 12500

three hundred and forty-two ratls and six-sevenths of a ratl.

Six-sevenths of a ratl is four hundred and twenty-eight dirhams and four-sevenths of a dirham. This is two-thirds of a ratl and four-sevenths of an ūqiyya.

Those later jurists who assumed that the Baghdad ratl was one hundred and thirty dirhams added one mithqāl to each Baghdad ratl, equivalent to one dirham and three-sevenths of a dirham. This adds two thousand dirhams and five-sevenths of a dirham, making the minimum threshold, according to their calculation, three hundred and forty-six ratls plus three hundred and fourteen dirhams and two-sevenths of a dirham—the latter amount being half a ratl and two-sevenths of an ūqiyya.

The tithe is payable by whoever owns the crop. If a landowner enters into a sharecropping arrangement with a farmer, scholars hold two views concerning the validity of that arrangement. Whoever considers sharecropping permissible takes his share and gives the farmer his share, and each must pay zakat on his own share. Whoever considers sharecropping invalid assigns the entire crop to the owner of the seed. If the farmer owns the seed, he is entitled to the entire crop, while the landowner is entitled only to rent for the land. In that case, zakat is payable by the farmer.

No Muslim has ever said, “Sharecropping is permissible, but the entire tithe is payable by the farmer.” Rather, whoever said, “The tithe is payable by the farmer,” also said, “The landowner has no share in the crop, nor does the grantor, nor anyone else.” Thus, whoever thinks that the tithe is payable by the farmer

---

## Page 12501

while also holding crop-sharing to be permissible has contradicted the consensus of the Muslims. Muslim practice in the lands of Syria recognizes the permissibility of sharecropping, in accordance with the established Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) and that of his rightly guided caliphs. This applies whether the seed is supplied by the landowner or the farmer. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) made an agreement with the people of Khaybar for half of its yield of fruit and crops, on condition that they cultivate it at their own expense. Thus, they supplied the seed. This is what the Companions agreed upon, and it has been the practice of Muslims throughout most Muslim lands from the time of their Prophet until today.

Anyone who enters into a sharecropping arrangement must pay zakat on their share. Anyone who follows the opinion that such sharecropping is invalid, and holds that he is entitled to none of the crop, that the farmer owes him only rent, and that taking a share of the crop without the farmer’s willing consent would make him an oppressor consuming unlawful wealth, must hand the crop over to the farmer and inform him that he is entitled only to the customary rent. If, after this, the farmer willingly agrees to share the crop with him and pay the zakat, the farmer is then freely doing him a favor. It is well known that, if farmers knew this, most of them would not willingly agree to it. This is the true position on this issue according to the views of both groups. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12502

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

Section

As for the agricultural tithe, the majority of scholars—including Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and others—hold that it is payable by the person who owns the crop as it grows. Allah Most High says:

“O you who believe, spend from the good things you have earned and from what We have brought forth for you from the earth.”

The first part includes zakat on trade goods, while the second includes zakat on what Allah brings forth for us from the earth. Whoever Allah brings forth grain for must therefore pay its tithe. Thus, if someone rents land to cultivate it, the tithe is payable by the tenant according to all these scholars. This is also the view of Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad. Abū Hanīfah, however, holds that the tithe is payable by the landlord.

When land is sharecropped for half the yield, the landowner must pay the tithe on his share, and the farmer must pay the tithe on his share. Each must pay the tithe on what Allah has brought forth for him.

---

## Page 12503

Anyone who is lent land, granted it as an allotment, or designated as the beneficiary of an endowment of it, and then grows crops on it, must pay the tithe on those crops. If he leases it out, the tithe is payable by the tenant; if he has it cultivated under a sharecropping agreement, the tithe is apportioned between them.

The principle underlying these imams’ position is that the tithe is an obligation attached to the crop. They therefore hold that both the tithe and the land tax may be payable together: the tithe is an obligation attached to the crop, whose recipients are those entitled to zakat, whereas the land tax is an obligation attached to the land, whose recipients are those entitled to fay’ revenues. These are two obligations owed to different recipients on different grounds, so both apply. Likewise, someone who kills a Muslim by mistake owes blood money to the victim’s family and must perform an expiation as a duty owed to Allah. Similarly, someone who kills a privately owned game animal while in the consecrated state of pilgrimage must compensate its owner and pay the prescribed penalty as a duty owed to Allah.

Abū Hanīfah, however, holds that the tithe is an obligation attached to the land, and that two obligations cannot therefore be imposed upon it together. Among the arguments advanced by the majority is that land tax is payable on land capable of cultivation, whether it is cultivated or not, whereas the tithe is payable only on the crop.

The report attributed to the Prophet:

“The tithe and the land tax cannot be combined”

is a fabrication, by unanimous agreement of the hadith scholars.

---

## Page 12504

He was also asked:

Concerning someone who owns grapevines whose fruit cannot be made into raisins, and who does not leave the fruit until harvest time: how should he pay its tithe—in fresh or dried fruit? If he pays it in dried fruit, he will be paying it from produce other than that of his orchard.

He replied:

As for grapes that cannot be made into raisins, it is permissible—and preferable—to pay their tithe in raisins, in the amount that would have been due had those grapes been capable of becoming raisins. This undoubtedly discharges his obligation. The owner is not required to pay from the very property on which the obligation falls, either in this case or in any other. Rather, anyone who possesses gold, silver, trade goods, grain or fruit subject to the tithe, or livestock subject to zakat, and pays the expressly prescribed amount due from other property, thereby discharges his obligation. How much more so, then, in this case?

If he pays the tithe in grapes, there are two views within Ahmad’s school:

The first, which is Ahmad’s expressly stated position, is that this does not discharge his obligation.

The second is that it does discharge his obligation. This is the view of Judge Abū Ya‘lā and of most scholars, and it is the stronger view.

---

## Page 12505

As for grapes that can be made into raisins but which he harvests before they become raisins, he must undoubtedly pay in raisins. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) used to send his zakat collectors to estimate the yield of date palms and grapevines and require their owners to pay the zakat due in dried fruit, even though the owners ate much of their fruit fresh. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) would instruct those estimating the yield to leave one-third or one-quarter for the owners, on which no tithe was to be collected. He would say:

“When you estimate the yield, leave one-third; if you do not leave one-third, then leave one-quarter.” In another narration: “For the produce includes a share for gifts of palm fruit, visiting guests, and wayfarers.”

This means that the owner voluntarily gives others the fruit of certain date palms to eat. He also feeds guests who visit his orchard, as well as wayfarers—that is, travellers. This deduction is the position of Imam Ahmad and other hadith-oriented jurists. Scholars disagree concerning this issue, as they do concerning the first issue. As for the second, I know of no disagreement concerning it, for the entitlement of zakat beneficiaries is not forfeited merely because the owner chooses to harvest the fruit fresh, if it can be dried.

However, if he sells his grapes or fresh dates after they have begun to ripen, Ahmad explicitly stated that, in this case, paying one-tenth of the sale price discharges his obligation; he need not pay in grapes or raisins. There is disagreement within his school concerning payment of the equivalent value. His numerous recorded statements indicate that he permits this when there is a need, but not otherwise. The position commonly held by many of his followers is that it is impermissible without exception, while a view permitting it without restriction has also been extrapolated from his statements. His explicit statements, however, distinguish between the two circumstances.

---

## Page 12506

Such cases are common in his school, in that of al-Shāfiʿī, and in those of other imams. An imam may explicitly give different answers to two similar questions, and some of his followers may extend the answer to each question to the other, whereas the correct approach is to uphold his explicit rulings and the distinction between the two cases. For example, he explicitly ruled that a bequest to one’s killer is valid if made after the wounding, while also ruling that if a slave whose emancipation is stipulated to take effect upon his master’s death kills his master, that stipulation becomes void. Some of his followers accordingly derived two alternative positions from him on each question.

Others, however, said: Rather, if the killing occurs after the bequest is made, the bequest becomes void, just as an heir’s killing the person from whom he would inherit prevents him from inheriting. But if the bequest is made to the killer after the wounding, it is valid, for the testator consented to it after being wounded. There are many comparable cases.

He was asked:

“A holder of a land grant has a farmer cultivating it, and they divide the crop equally. Must he pay the tithe?”

He answered:

A person must pay the tithe on whatever grows on his property. Thus, when the crop from land held under a grant is divided equally, the farmer must pay the tithe on his half, and the grant holder must pay the tithe on his half. This is according to the correct view, which Muslims have followed in practice, both in earlier times and today: namely, the view that sharecropping is valid whether the seed is supplied by the owner or by the cultivator.

---

## Page 12507

As for those who hold that sharecropping is invalid, in their view the grant holder is entitled only to the fair rental value, while the entire crop belongs to the cultivator who supplied the seed. In that case, the cultivator must pay the entire tithe. If, however, the grant holder wishes to take half the yield as his share while making the person entitled to the other half pay the entire tithe, he may not do so, by scholarly consensus. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“A person holds a land grant from the ruler. Is the produce he receives from that grant subject to zakat or not?”

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, he must pay the tithe on whatever produce he owns, whether he holds the land by grant, lease, ownership, or loan. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

“Is the cultivator’s share of the farm’s produce subject to zakat?”

He answered:

As for zakat in orchard and crop sharecropping, this rests on a fundamental issue

---

## Page 12508

—namely, whether crop and orchard sharecropping are permissible. There are two well-known views:

The first is that they are not permissible. Those who hold this view regard them as a form of leasing or hiring in return for an unspecified payment. Some therefore invalidate them without exception, as Abū Hanīfa does. Others make exceptions where there is a need: they permit orchard sharecropping because trees, unlike land, cannot be leased. They also permit crop sharecropping on land containing trees as an adjunct to orchard sharecropping, either without restriction, as al-Shāfi'ī holds, or provided that the open ground constitutes no more than one-third, as Mālik holds. Some permit orchard sharecropping without restriction, as Mālik does and as al-Shāfi'ī did in his earlier position. In his later position, however, al-Shāfi'ī restricted its permissibility to date palms and grapevines.

The second view permits both orchard and crop sharecropping. Its proponents maintain that these are partnerships, a distinct category from leasing or hiring, in which the extent of the benefit and the payment must be specified. In these partnership contracts, the work is not itself the objective; rather, the objective is the yield that the two parties share.
