One contributes the use of his property, and the other contributes his physical labor. The same applies to capital-and-labor partnerships.

Accordingly, if the parties to these contracts part ways, the worker is entitled to the customary share of the profit for someone in his position—whether one-third or one-half—not to the customary wage for his work. This view is unquestionably correct, and the Companions unanimously agreed upon it.

---

## Page 12509

The view that orchard and crop sharecropping are permissible is held by the majority of the early generations—the Companions, the Successors, and others. It is also the position of al-Layth ibn Sa‘d, Ibn Abī Laylā, Abū Yūsuf, Muhammad, and the hadith jurists, such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, Muhammad ibn Ishāq ibn Khuzayma, Abū Bakr ibn al-Mundhir, al-Khattābī, and others.

The correct view is that crop sharecropping is more lawful than leasing for a specified rent, because it is closer to justice and further removed from risk. The contracts prohibited by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, include those that fall under usury, which is forbidden in the Quran, and those that fall under games of chance—that is, gambling. Sales involving contractual uncertainty are a form of gambling and games of chance. Thus, when rent or a purchase price involves uncertainty—for example, something that has neither been described nor seen, and whose kind is unknown—this constitutes contractual uncertainty and gambling.

It is well known that the tenant seeks to benefit from the land by obtaining a crop from it. Once he pays the specified rent, the landlord has certainly obtained what he sought. The tenant, however, does not know whether he will obtain a crop or not. Crop sharecropping is different: both parties share in the gain and in receiving no return. The same applies to capital-and-labor partnerships: if anything is gained, they share it; if nothing is gained, both receive no return. The loss of the benefit of one party’s property is thus matched by the loss of the benefit of the other party’s labor.

---

## Page 12510

For this reason, it is not permissible to stipulate a fixed amount of the returns for either party, whether in capital-and-labor partnerships, orchard sharecropping, or crop sharecropping, because this is contrary to justice: one party might receive something while the other receives nothing. This is what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited in the hadiths reporting “that he forbade mukhābara (sharecropping),” “leasing land,” or “crop sharecropping,” such as the hadith of Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj and others. These reports explain that people used to cultivate land on condition that the produce of a particular plot belonged to the owner.

For this reason, al-Layth ibn Sa‘d said: “The arrangement of this kind that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited is one that anyone knowledgeable about the lawful and unlawful would, upon examining it, recognize as impermissible.”

Crop sharecropping, however, is undoubtedly permissible, whether the seed comes from the owner, the cultivator, or both, and whether the contract is expressed in terms of leasing, crop sharecropping, or otherwise. This is the soundest position on this issue. The same applies to all arrangements of this kind, such as entrusting one’s animal or boat to someone who earns with it, with the profit shared between them; or entrusting one’s livestock or date palms to someone who tends them, with the wool, milk, offspring, and honey shared between them.

Once these two views on crop sharecropping are understood, those scholars who hold that

---

## Page 12511

crop sharecropping is invalid say that the entire crop belongs to the landowner if he supplied the seed, or to the cultivator if he supplied it. Whichever party is held to own the crop is liable for its tithe. As for those who hold that the landowner is entitled to an undivided share of the crop, he must pay the tithe on his share, by agreement of the imams. No Muslim scholar has said that the landowner shares the crop with the cultivator while the cultivator alone bears the entire tithe. Whoever says this has contradicted the consensus of the Muslims.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Regarding men wearing silver in the form of clasps, a ring, a belt, sword ornaments, and other silver adornments: is this forbidden, and is it impermissible to pray while wearing them?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. A silver ring is permissible by agreement of the imams, for it is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, wore a silver ring and that his Companions wore rings. A gold ring, by contrast, is forbidden by agreement of the four imams, for it is authentically established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade it.

---

## Page 12512

As for a sword, it is permissible to adorn it with a small amount of silver, for “the sword of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had silver on it.” The same applies to a small amount of gold, according to the correct view.

As for a belt containing a small amount of silver, it is permissible according to the sounder of the two views. Clasps needed to hold a turban in place, if comparable in weight to rings—a mithqāl or thereabouts—have an even stronger claim to permissibility than a ring, since a ring is worn for adornment, whereas these serve a need. Moreover, these are attached pieces involving only a small amount, rather than separate ornaments like a ring. A small amount of silver forming part of another object, where that kind of fitting is needed—such as a small fitting on a knife or a ring on a vessel—is permissible in vessels, even if direct contact with it during use is disliked. The rules concerning clothing allow greater latitude than those concerning (vessels), for gold and silver vessels are forbidden to both men and women.

As for clothing, wearing gold and silver is permissible for women by agreement, while men are permitted whatever they need of these materials. A small amount of silver is also permissible for adornment, as is a small amount of gold incorporated into something else, such as embroidered borders and the like, according to the sounder of the two views in the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited gold except in small pieces.

Thus, if the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted a small amount of silver for adornment, whether as a separate item or attached to something else, such as the ornaments of a sword or another object, how, then, can it be forbidden

---

## Page 12513

to wear a small amount of silver when needed?

All of this would hold even if a general prohibition against wearing silver had been reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, just as a general prohibition against men wearing gold and silk has been reported from him, when he said:

“These two are forbidden to the males of my community and lawful for its females.”

Likewise, a general prohibition against gold and silver vessels has been reported from him. Since the Prophet’s statements, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were general regarding gold and silver vessels and the wearing of gold and silk, exceptions were made for whatever the evidence of Islamic law specifically excluded, such as small amounts of silk, small amounts of silver in vessels when needed, and similar cases.

As for wearing silver, if there is no general statement prohibiting it, no one may prohibit any form of it except what the evidence of Islamic law establishes as forbidden. Thus, when the Sunnah permits a silver ring, this is evidence that it is permissible, as is whatever is equivalent to it and whatever has an even stronger claim to permissibility. Whatever does not fall into these categories requires examination to determine whether it is lawful or forbidden. And Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

---

## Page 12514

He was also asked about a soldier who said to a craftsman, “Make me a belt of gold or silver, and inscribe on it ‘In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.’” Is this permissible? It must then be put back into the fire to complete its manufacture. And is it permissible for anyone to wear a belt of gold or silver?

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

A gold belt is forbidden, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Gold and silk—these two are forbidden to the males of my community and lawful for its females.”

As for a silver belt, scholars differ concerning it. Al-Shāfi'ī permitted it, as did Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him.

As for inscribing the Quran on it, this resembles inscribing the Quran on dirhams and dinars. This case, however, differs in that the belt is put back into the fire after being inscribed. All of this is disliked, for it leads to the Quran being treated carelessly and disrespectfully and being taken into

---

## Page 12515

places where the Quran should not be taken. For belts, dirhams, dinars and the like are liable to be handled carelessly and disrespectfully. Although some scholars have permitted carrying dirhams inscribed with the Quran, this permission was granted because of the need to do so; they did not permit inscribing the Quran on them. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12516

Chapter: The End-of-Ramadan Alms

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about zakāt al-fitr:

“Should it be given as dates, raisins, wheat, barley, or flour? May one give it to relatives whom one is not obliged to support? Or is it permissible to give its equivalent value?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the people of a locality subsist on one of these foodstuffs, they may undoubtedly give it from their staple food. But may they give other foodstuffs on which they subsist? For example, if their staples are rice and millet, must they give wheat or barley, or will rice, millet, or dhurah (a cereal grain) suffice? There is a well-known disagreement on this matter.

Two positions are reported from Ahmad:

---

## Page 12517

According to one, only the foodstuffs specified in the texts may be given. According to the other, one may give one’s staple food, even if it is not among these categories. This is the view of most scholars, including al-Shāfiʿī and others, and it is the soundest opinion. For the basic principle governing charitable dues is that they are prescribed on the basis of equal treatment of the poor, as Allah Most High says:

“From the average of that which you feed your families.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prescribed zakāt al-fitr as a sāʿ of dates or a sāʿ of barley because these were the staple foods of the people of Medina. Had they subsisted on something else instead, he would not have required them to give food they did not ordinarily eat, just as Allah did not command this in the case of expiations. The end-of-Ramadan alms are comparable to expiations: both attach to the person, unlike zakat on wealth, which becomes obligatory because of one’s wealth and is given from the kind of wealth Allah has bestowed.

As for flour, giving it is permissible according to the schools of Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad, but not al-Shāfiʿī. It is given by weight, since grinding increases its bulk.

A relative who is eligible to receive these alms has a greater claim than an unrelated person if their needs are equal. For your charity to a needy person is charity, whereas charity to a relative is both charity and the maintenance of kinship ties. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12518

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Someone who is obliged to pay zakāt al-fitr knows that it is one sāʿ, but gives more, saying that the extra is voluntary charity. Is this disliked?”

He replied:

“All praise is due to Allah. Yes, this is permissible without being disliked according to most scholars, including al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others. Only Mālik is reported to have considered it disliked. Giving less than the obligatory amount, however, is impermissible by scholarly agreement. As for whether the obligatory amount is one sāʿ, half a sāʿ, or more, there are two opinions. And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 12519

Shaykh al-Islām was asked about zakāt al-fitr:

“Must it be distributed among all eight categories of recipients, or is giving it to one person sufficient? What are the scholars’ views on this?”

He replied:

“All praise is due to Allah. This subject involves two fundamental issues.

The first concerns zakāt on wealth, such as livestock, money, trade goods and agricultural produce subject to zakāt. Scholars hold two views on this.

The first view is that everyone paying zakāt must distribute it among all the categories whose members can be reached, giving to three people from each category. This is the well-known position of the school of al-Shāfiʿī, and it is also one view reported from Imam Ahmad.

The second view is that the obligation is simply not to distribute it outside the eight categories, not to give anyone more than suffices for their needs, and not to show favoritism by giving to one person while passing over

---

## Page 12520

someone more entitled than him, or equally entitled, when equitable distribution is possible. According to these scholars, a person fulfils his obligation if he gives all the zakāt on his wealth to a single recipient belonging to one category, provided that the recipient is entitled to that amount. For example, if someone owes a debt of one thousand dirhams that he cannot repay, giving him one’s entire zakāt of one thousand dirhams fulfils the obligation.

This is the view of the majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad, according to the well-known position reported from him. It is also the position transmitted from the Companions, such as Hudhayfa ibn al-Yamān and ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAbbās, and is attributed to ʿUmar himself. It is authentically reported in Sahīh Muslim that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Qabīsa ibn Mukhāriq al-Hilālī:

“Stay here, Qabīsa, until the zakāt reaches us, and we will order that it be given to you.”

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd and elsewhere, it is reported that he said to Salama ibn Sakhr al-Bayādī:

“Go to the zakāt collector of Banū Zurayq, and let him give you their zakāt.”

These two hadiths show that he gave a community’s zakāt to a single individual. However, it was the ruler who ordered this, and cases of this kind are disputed. There are arguments on both sides of the issue that this fatwa cannot accommodate.

Our concern here is the second fundamental issue: zakāt al-fitr. Is this charity treated like zakāt on wealth, or like charitable dues attached to the person, such as expiations? There are two views. Those who hold the first view, and also maintain that distribution among all the categories is obligatory, require such distribution for zakāt al-fitr as well.

---

## Page 12521

The position of al-Shāfiʿī—may Allah be pleased with him—mentioned by the questioner rests on these two principles. Those who hold that distribution among all the categories is not obligatory, as the majority of scholars maintain, permit zakāt al-fitr to be given to a single recipient, as has been the practice of Muslims, past and present.

Those who adopt the second view hold that zakāt al-fitr is treated like the expiations for breaking an oath, zihār, killing, intercourse during Ramadan, and violations committed during Hajj. Its basis is the person, not wealth. Thus, it is reported in the Sunan collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“prescribed zakāt al-fitr as purification for the fasting person from idle and obscene speech, and as food for the needy. Whoever gives it before the prayer, it is accepted zakāt; whoever gives it after the prayer, it is ordinary charity.”

Another hadith reports that he said:

“Spare them the need to beg on this day.”

For this reason, Allah prescribed it in the form of food, just as He prescribed food for expiation. According to this view, giving this food fulfils the obligation only when it is given to those entitled to receive food offered in expiation: those who receive it because of their own need. None of it is therefore given to those whose hearts are to be reconciled, or for freeing slaves, or to other such categories. This view has the stronger evidence.

The weakest view is that every Muslim must give

---

## Page 12522

his zakāt al-fitr to twelve, eighteen, twenty-four, thirty-two, twenty-eight, or a similar number of recipients. This runs contrary to the practice of Muslims during the time of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, his Rightly Guided Caliphs, and all his Companions. No Muslim practised this in their time. Rather, a Muslim would give his own zakāt al-fitr and that of his dependants to a single Muslim.

Had they seen someone dividing a sāʿ among more than ten people, giving each a handful, they would have condemned this in the strongest terms and considered it a reprehensible innovation and an objectionable practice. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed a sāʿ of dates or a sāʿ of barley, and either half a sāʿ or a full sāʿ of wheat—an amount sufficient to meet the needs of one needy person fully. He made it food for the needy on the day of Eid, sparing them the need to beg. If a needy person receives only a handful, it neither benefits him nor serves its intended purpose.

Likewise, if someone who is in debt and is a stranded traveller receives a handful of wheat, it does not benefit him in a way that fulfils its intended purpose to any extent that reasonable people would consider meaningful. Nevertheless, this may serve a purpose in some circumstances, as, for example, if there were a number of people in dire need

---

## Page 12523

and if a sāʿ were divided among them, they would survive, whereas if it were given exclusively to some of them, the rest would die. In this case, it should be divided among several people. But this requires that dividing it serve their welfare. The Sharia is above such objectionable practices, which reasonable people would not approve and which none of the early generations of this ummah or its leading scholars practised. Furthermore, the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“food for the needy,”

explicitly establish that this is the right of the needy. Likewise, Allah Most High says in the verse concerning zihār (declaring one’s wife unlawful by likening her to one’s mother):

“Then the feeding of sixty needy people.”

If that may not be distributed among the eight categories of recipients, then neither may this. For this reason, what is paid as zakāt on wealth must be of the same kind as the property constituting the zakāt threshold, and the required payment is something that can be retained and made productive. Thus, female livestock are required rather than males, except for a yearling male calf and a two-year-old male camel, because the purpose is milk and offspring, which only females provide.

With Eid sacrifices and pilgrimage offerings, however, the purpose is consumption, so a male is preferable to a female. When these offerings and sacrifices, or any portion of them, are given in charity, they are given to the needy who are in want, rather than distributed among all eight categories of recipients. Zakāt al-fitr was prescribed as food for consumption, not for generating further wealth. It is therefore clear that it belongs to the same category as expiatory offerings.

If it is said that His words,

“Charitable dues are only for the poor and the needy,”

explicitly encompass all forms of charity, the response is: This is mistaken for several reasons:

---

## Page 12524

First:

The definite article here merely identifies the particular charitable dues already mentioned in His words:

“Among them are those who criticize you concerning charitable dues; if they are given some of them, they are pleased.”

These, then, are charitable dues on wealth, not those due on persons, by agreement of the Muslims. Thus, when He says in the verse concerning compensation:

“Then compensation by fasting, charity, or sacrifice,”

this charity is not included in the verse in Surah Barā’ah. The leading scholars agree that compensation for an ailment need not be distributed among all eight categories. Likewise, voluntary charity is not included in the verse, by the consensus of the Muslims. The same applies to all other acts of kindness, for it is authentically reported through several chains that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every act of kindness is charity.”

Such charity is not restricted to the eight categories, by agreement of the Muslims.

This is the response of those who deny that this charity is included in the verse. Moreover, the verse encompasses all the poor, the needy, and those in debt throughout the east and west of the earth. No Muslim has said that every one of these people must receive a share. Rather, the most that has been said is that three people from each category must be given something. Yet this restricts the general wording concerning each category and then selects one poor person rather than another. Furthermore, no one has required equal distribution among the individuals within each category. Thus, in the view of

---

## Page 12525

the majority, the same principle applies to the categories—regarding comprehensive and equal distribution—as applies to the individuals within each category.

The second point is that His words:

“Charitable dues are only…”

express restriction. They affirm what is mentioned and leave everything else aside. The meaning is that charitable dues are not for anyone other than these recipients; rather, they are for these recipients. What is affirmed concerns the same matter as what is denied. Clearly, the intention is not to establish ownership, but to explain lawfulness: charitable dues are not lawful for anyone other than these recipients. The corresponding affirmation is therefore that they are lawful for them.

This is because the statement occurs in the context of censuring those who asked him for charitable dues without being entitled to them. A person is censured for requesting something unlawful for him, not for requesting something lawful for him, even though he does not yet own it. Otherwise, these recipients and everyone else would be censured for requesting charitable dues from the ruler before receiving them. If the censure applied universally, the restrictive wording would not single these people out for censure rather than others. Yet the context of the verse requires that they be censured. The particular grounds for their censure are that they requested something unlawful for them. This, then, is what is denied, while what is affirmed is that it is lawful for these recipients.

Making something lawful for the categories and their individual members does not entail comprehensive or equal distribution. This is like the preposition “for” in His words, Exalted is He:

“It is He who created for you all that is on the earth.”

And His words:

“He has subjected to you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth—all from Him.”

And the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“You and your wealth are for your father.”

There are similar examples in which the preposition “for” indicates permission. Thus, the assertion that He apportioned the charitable dues among them through the conjunction “and,” which denotes sharing

---

## Page 12526

and the preposition “for,” taken to denote ownership, is untenable for the reasons we have mentioned.

The third point is that Allah says concerning inheritance:

“Allah instructs you concerning your children: a male shall have the share of two females.”

And He says:

“You shall have half of what your wives leave,”

through to His words:

“And they shall have a quarter of what you leave.”

And He says:

“If there are brothers and sisters, a male shall have the share of two females.”

Since the preposition “for” here denotes ownership, all the categories mentioned must be covered, each category included, and equal shares allotted among them. Thus, if a man has four wives, four sons, daughters, sisters, or brothers, every individual within the relevant category must be included and receive an equal share, because each is entitled by virtue of kinship, in which they stand equally. In the case of charitable dues, however, the matter is different, so this is not obligatory.

It should not be objected that it is impossible to include every individual in a category, for the reply is that the same principle advanced regarding the categories must also be applied to their individual members. If it is said, “All categories must be included as far as possible, and whatever is beyond one’s ability is waived,” the same must be said regarding individuals.

Yet that is not the rule. Rather, one must strive to act justly as far as possible, as we have explained. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12527

Chapter: Paying Zakat

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

May a merchant pay his obligatory zakat in the form of goods of a kind that is needed?

If someone dies owing him a debt, may he give zakat to one of the deceased’s relatives, if that relative is eligible to receive it, and then recover the debt from him? If he distributes his zakat among the people of another town far enough away for prayers to be shortened, does this discharge his obligation?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If he gives the recipient dirhams, this undoubtedly discharges his obligation.

As for giving the equivalent value in another form, there is disagreement: is this permissible in all cases, impermissible in all cases, or permissible in certain circumstances because of a need or an overriding benefit? These are three views within the school of Ahmad and other schools. The last view is the most balanced.

If the zakat recipient wishes to use it to buy clothing, and the owner purchases

---

## Page 12528

clothing for him with it and gives it to him, he has done him a kindness. But if he values clothing already in his possession and gives it as zakat, he may value it above its market price. Moreover, someone who does not need the clothing may receive it and then sell it, incurring an auctioneer’s fee and perhaps selling at a loss. This would harm the poor.

For all the kinds of goods in which he trades, he may pay zakat in dirhams according to their value. If he has no dirhams and gives their equivalent value instead, the more evident view is that this is permissible, because he has assisted the poor by giving them property of the same kind as his own.

As for the debt owed by the deceased, it may be paid from zakat according to one of the two scholarly opinions. This is also one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, because Allah Most High said:

“and those in debt,”

not “and to those in debt.” Thus, it is not a condition that the debtor personally take ownership of the zakat. Accordingly, the debt may be settled on his behalf, or the zakat may be given into the possession of his heir or someone else. However, someone who owes a debt must not be given zakat in order for that debt to be recovered.

---

## Page 12529

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“When the ruler collects zakat, whether the tithe or other forms, and spends it wherever he wishes without giving it to the poor and needy, does this discharge the obligation or not?”

He replied:

As for the tithe, zakat on livestock and trade goods, and other such dues collected by Muslim rulers, paying them discharges the owner’s obligation, by scholarly consensus, if the ruler is just and distributes them through their prescribed lawful channels.

If, however, he is unjust and does not distribute them through their prescribed lawful channels, the owner should not pay zakat to him, but should distribute it himself to those entitled to it. If he is compelled to pay it to the unjust ruler, such that he would suffer harm if he withheld it, then this payment discharges his obligation in that situation according to most scholars.

In this case, the rulers have wronged those entitled to the zakat, just as an orphan’s guardian or an endowment’s administrator does when taking possession of the funds and spending them for purposes other than those prescribed.

---

## Page 12530

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is paying the equivalent value as zakat permissible or not, given that it is often more beneficial to the poor?”

He replied:

As for paying the equivalent value in zakat, expiation, and similar obligations, the well-known position in the schools of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī is that it is not permissible, whereas Abū Hanīfa permits it. Ahmad—may Allah have mercy on him—prohibited it in some cases and permitted it in others. Some of his followers upheld his explicit rulings as stated, while others treated them as two differing positions transmitted from him.

The stronger view on this matter is that paying the equivalent value without a need or an overriding benefit is prohibited. Thus, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, fixed the compensatory payment at two sheep or twenty dirhams, rather than prescribing payment of the equivalent value. Moreover, if paying the equivalent value is permitted without restriction, the owner may resort to inferior kinds of property, and valuation may cause harm. Furthermore, zakat is founded on sharing one’s wealth with those in need, and this principle applies both to the amount of wealth and to its kind.

As for paying the equivalent value because of a need, a benefit, or considerations of fairness, there is no

---

## Page 12531

objection to it. For example, if someone sells the fruit of his orchard or his crops for dirhams, paying one-tenth of those dirhams discharges his obligation. He is not required to purchase fruit or wheat, since he has placed the poor on an equal footing with himself. Ahmad explicitly stated that this is permissible.

Another example is someone who owes one sheep as zakat on five camels but has no one locally who will sell him a sheep. Paying its equivalent value is sufficient in this case; he is not required to travel to another town to purchase a sheep. Likewise, if those entitled to zakat ask him to give them its equivalent value because that would benefit them more, he may do so. The same applies if the zakat collector considers accepting its equivalent value more beneficial to the poor.

Thus, it is reported that Muʿādh ibn Jabal used to say to the people of Yemen:

“Bring me khamīs garments or worn clothing; that is easier for you and better for the Emigrants and Helpers in Medina.”

Some have said that he said this concerning zakat, while others have said that it concerned jizya.

---

## Page 12532

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“If someone forgives a debt owed by a person in financial hardship, may he count it toward his zakat?”

He replied:

As for forgiving a debt owed by someone in financial hardship, this does not discharge the zakat due on assets in hand, and there is no disagreement about this. However, if someone is owed a debt by a person eligible for zakat, may he forgive an amount equal to the zakat due on that debt and count this as its zakat? Scholars within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere hold two opinions on this. The stronger opinion is that it is permissible, because zakat is founded on sharing one’s wealth with those in need. Here, he has given something of the same kind as what he owns.

This differs from the case of someone whose wealth consists of assets in hand but who gives a debt owed to him instead. What he gives is inferior to what he owns, so this amounts to giving inferior wealth in place of good wealth, which is impermissible. As Allah Most High says:

“Do not deliberately choose the inferior part of it to give away”—to the end of the verse.

Thus, someone paying zakat must give from the same kind of wealth that he owns, not something inferior to it. If he owns good-quality fruit and wheat, he must not give inferior produce as zakat on them.

---

## Page 12533

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“If someone owes zakat and has relatives who are eligible for charity in a town far enough away to permit shortening the prayer when travelling there, may he give his zakat to them or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If they are in need, eligible for zakat, and do not receive enough to meet their needs from another source, he may give them his zakat, even if they live in a distant town. And Allah knows best.

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

“Regarding a needy person who needs zakat from crops: if the crop owner gives it to him in advance, before his crop ripens, does this discharge the owner’s obligation or not?”

He replied:

As for paying zakat in advance, before it becomes obligatory but after the basis for its obligation has arisen, this is permissible according to the majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad. Thus, it is permissible to

---

## Page 12534

pay zakat on livestock, gold and silver, and trade goods in advance if one possesses the minimum zakat threshold. It is also permissible to pay zakat on agricultural produce before it becomes obligatory, if the fruit has appeared but has not yet begun to ripen, and the crops have sprouted but the grain has not yet hardened. Once the grain hardens and the fruit begins to ripen, zakat becomes obligatory.

He was asked:

“A man possesses wealth exceeding the minimum zakat threshold and pays some of the obligatory zakat from it, thinking that a full year has elapsed, but then discovers that it has not. Another person pays zakat with the intention that, if the year has elapsed, it is zakat; otherwise, it is an advance payment towards what will become due. Does the payment discharge the obligation in both cases?”

He replied:

Yes, it discharges the obligation in both cases, provided that zakat becomes obligatory. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12535

He was asked:

“Is it permissible to give zakat to people affiliated with shaykhs, or not?”

He replied:

Section

As for zakat, one should carefully seek out those entitled to it among the poor, the needy, those in debt, and others who are religiously observant and follow the Sharia. Whoever openly displays religious innovation or wickedness deserves punishment through shunning and other measures, and should be called to repent. How, then, can he be helped in such conduct?

As for someone who takes zakat and spends it as he chooses, or spends it on his dependants despite being wealthy, it is not permissible to give it to him, nor does giving it to him discharge the giver’s obligation. Rather, zakat must be given only to someone entitled to it, or to someone who will deliver it to those entitled to it, such as someone with knowledge

---

## Page 12536

of those entitled to it, and who can be trusted to deliver it to them. As Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, Allah commands you to render trusts to whom they are due.”

If someone requests zakat whose need for it is unknown to the giver, while the giver knows that someone else is in need, giving to the person whose need he knows is preferable. Giving to a needy relative whom he is not obliged to support is preferable to giving to an unrelated person whose need is equal.

He was asked:

“A man owes zakat: may he give it to his needy relatives, or buy clothing or grain for them with it? If the ruler takes some of his sheep, does this discharge the zakat due on them? Must he distribute the zakat in the locality where there is scarcity and where his wealth is held, or not?

“If a poor person dies owing him money, may he count that debt towards his zakat? Or may he request the amount from someone else and receive it on the deceased’s behalf? May he give zakat to someone who does not pray, or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. He may distribute zakat to those entitled to it, even if they are relatives, provided they are not among his dependants. However, he should give them some of his wealth, and they may authorize someone to use it to purchase whatever they wish.

---

## Page 12537

Whatever the ruler takes as zakat without the owners’ authorization counts towards their zakat. Those living near the wealth have the strongest claim to its zakat. If they have no need of it, he should give it to those farther away. Giving it to the poor in another locality is permissible. If someone owes him a debt, whether that person is living or dead, he must not count it towards his zakat or resort to a stratagem to do so.

Anyone who does not pray should be instructed to pray. If he says, “I pray,” he is to be given zakat; otherwise, he is not.

He was asked (may Allah sanctify his soul):

“Is giving zakat to one’s needy relatives whom one is not obliged to support preferable to giving it to an unrelated person?”

He replied:

As for giving zakat to relatives: if a relative who is eligible to receive it is as needy as an unrelated person, the relative takes priority. But if the unrelated person is needier, he must not favour his relative in distributing it.

Ahmad reported from Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah that they used to say:

“One must not use it to favour a relative, ward off blame, or protect one’s own wealth.”

---

## Page 12538

He was asked (may Allah have mercy on him):

“Is it permissible to give zakat to one’s parents and children whom one is not obliged to support?”

He replied:

Those who receive zakat fall into two categories:

Those who receive it because of their own need, such as the poor and those who have incurred debts for their own purposes.

Those who receive it because of the needs of the Muslims, such as those fighting in Allah’s cause and those who have incurred debts to reconcile disputing parties. Such people may be given zakat even if they are one’s relatives.

As for giving zakat to one’s parents, if they are in debt or under contracts of manumission, there are two views; the more convincing view is that this is permissible. If, however, they are poor and one is unable to support them, the stronger view is that giving them zakat in this situation is permissible. This is because the grounds for entitlement are present and no impediment exists. These grounds must therefore be given effect, since no countervailing consideration of equal force opposes them.

---

## Page 12539

He was asked:

“A poor woman is in debt and has young grandchildren through her daughter who possess wealth and are under guardianship. Is it permissible for them to give their zakat to their grandmother? Does she take priority over others?”

He replied:

Giving her their zakat to repay her debt is permissible according to the stronger of the two scholarly views. This is one of the two views within the school of Ahmad and others. The same applies to giving zakat to other relatives to repay their debts.

As for giving it to cover her living expenses, if her needs are already met by their support or that of others, it is not to be given to her. If, however, she needs their zakat, it is to be given to her according to the stronger of the two scholarly views, and she has a greater right to it than those who are unrelated. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12540

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“May someone who owes a debt take from his father’s zakat to repay it?”

He replied:

If a son owes a debt and lacks the means to repay it, he may take from his father’s zakat according to the stronger of the two views within the school of Ahmad and others.

If, however, he needs support for his living expenses and his father lacks the means to support him, there is disagreement. The stronger view is that he may take from his father’s zakat. But if his needs are already met by his father’s support, he has no need of his zakat. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12541

He was asked:

“Can the charges imposed on a man by the authorities along the roads count towards his zakat?”

He replied:

Whatever the authorities collect under a designation other than zakat does not count towards zakat. And Allah Most High knows best.

He was asked about giving charity to needy relatives and others.

He replied:

If a person’s wealth is insufficient to provide for both relatives and others, he is obliged to support his relatives, so he must not give to others at his relatives’ expense. As for zakat and expiatory payments, he may give them to a relative whom he does not support. Relatives take precedence when the need is equal.

---

## Page 12542

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man is given some worldly wealth by a brother of his. Should he accept it or refuse it? The following has been reported: ‘Whoever receives something without asking and refuses it, it is as though he has refused something from Allah.’ Is this authentic or not?”

He replied:

It is authentically established that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) said to Umar:

“Whatever wealth comes to you without your asking for it or eagerly looking for it, accept it. Whatever does not, do not set your heart on it.”

It is also established in the Sahīh:

“Hakīm ibn Hizām asked him, and he gave him. Then he asked him again, and he gave him. Then he asked him again, and he gave him. Then he said: ‘O Hakīm, how often you ask! This wealth is green and sweet. Whoever takes it without covetousness will be blessed in it, but whoever takes it with a covetous heart will not be blessed in it and will be like someone who eats but is never satisfied.’ Hakīm said to him: ‘By the One who sent you with the truth, I will never take anything from anyone after you.’”

Thereafter, Abū Bakr and Umar would offer him gifts, but he would not accept them. These two hadiths make it clear that if a person asks verbally, or

---

## Page 12543

eagerly anticipates what he may be given, he should not accept it except where asking and such anticipation are permissible for him. But if it comes to him without his asking or eagerly anticipating it, he may accept it if the giver is giving him his due. Thus, the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) gave Umar money from the public treasury: he had performed work, and the Prophet paid him for it.

He may also decline—as Hakīm ibn Hizām did—what he has no right to claim from the giver. If he accepts it without having eagerly looked to the giver for it, he has done well. As for someone who is wealthy, he should repay the giver financially for his kindness, in accordance with the report:

“Whoever does you a kindness, repay him. If you find nothing with which to repay him, then pray for him until you know that you have repaid him.”

---

## Page 12544

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

Section: Accepting without Asking

The Sahīh collection contains the following report about Hakīm ibn Hizām:

“After Hakīm had asked the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) repeatedly, the Prophet said: ‘O Hakīm, this wealth is fresh and sweet. Whoever takes it without greed will be blessed in it, but whoever takes it with eager longing will not be blessed in it. He will be like someone who eats but is never satisfied. The upper hand is better than the lower hand.’

Hakīm said: ‘I said, “O Messenger of Allah, by Him who sent you with the truth, I will take nothing from anyone after you until I leave this world.”’

Abū Bakr (may Allah be pleased with him) would summon Hakīm to give him his stipend, but he would refuse to accept anything from him. Then Umar summoned him to give him his stipend, but he refused to accept it.

Umar said: ‘O Muslims’—or, in another version, ‘I call you to witness, O Muslims’—‘I am offering Hakīm his rightful share, which Allah has allotted him from this public revenue, but he refuses to take it.’ Thus Hakīm took nothing from anyone after the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him).”

---

## Page 12545

The words “he took nothing” mean that he did not diminish anyone’s wealth, not that he did not ask, as the context indicates. The report states that Hakīm told the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) that he would accept nothing from anyone, and the Prophet approved of this, as did the caliphs after him. This is evidence that it is permissible to decline a gift even when no request or eager longing is involved.

His saying,

“The upper hand is better than the lower hand,”

draws Hakīm’s attention to the fact that the recipient’s hand is the lower one. Ahmad was asked for evidence for this from the verse, but he knew of none. This, however, is good evidence.

Additional wordings have also been reported in this account, such as his saying:

“It is better for you not to accept anything from anyone.”

Its chain of transmission, however, needs to be examined. The wording explicitly indicates that not accepting anything is preferable in all cases.

---

## Page 12546

The Book of Fasting

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is fasting on a cloudy day obligatory? Is it a ‘day of doubt’ on which fasting is prohibited?”

He replied:

Section

As for fasting on a cloudy day, when clouds or dust haze prevent sighting the crescent, scholars hold several views, all represented within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

The first view is that fasting on that day is prohibited. There are then two opinions as to whether this prohibition means that it is unlawful or merely disliked. This is the well-known position in the schools of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, and is one of the positions reported from Ahmad. A group of his followers adopted this view, including Abū al-Khattāb

---

## Page 12547

and Ibn ʿAqīl, Abū al-Qāsim ibn Mandah al-Asfahānī, and others.

The second view is that fasting on that day is obligatory. This was the position adopted by al-Qādī, al-Khiraqī, and others among Ahmad’s followers. It is said to be the best-known position reported from Ahmad. Yet anyone familiar with Ahmad’s statements and wording knows that what is reliably established from him is that he considered fasting on a cloudy day recommended, following ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar and other Companions. ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar did not require people to fast on that day; rather, he himself fasted as a precaution.

Some Companions fasted on that day as a precaution. This is reported from ʿUmar, ʿAlī, Muʿāwiyah, Abū Hurayrah, Ibn ʿUmar, ʿĀʾishah, Asmāʾ, and others. Others did not fast on it, as was the case with many Companions, while some, such as ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir and others, prohibited fasting on it. Ahmad—may Allah be pleased with him—thus fasted on that day as a precaution. As for making fasting on it obligatory, this has no basis in Ahmad’s statements or those of any of his followers. Nevertheless, many of his followers believed that his position was that fasting on it was obligatory, and they defended that view.

The third view is that it is permissible either to fast on that day or not to fast. This is

---

## Page 12548

the position of Abū Hanīfah and others, and the position explicitly stated by Ahmad. It is also the position of many—or most—of the Companions and Successors.

This is comparable to abstaining from food and drink when an obstruction prevents dawn from being seen: one may abstain if one wishes, or continue eating until certain that dawn has broken. Likewise, someone who doubts whether his ablution has been invalidated may either renew it or refrain from doing so.

The same applies to someone who doubts whether the zakat year has elapsed, or whether the zakat he owes is one hundred or one hundred and twenty, and pays the additional amount. All the foundational principles of Islamic law establish that taking precautions is neither obligatory nor forbidden.

If he fasts that day with a general intention to fast, or with a conditional intention—“If this day is in Ramadan, this fast is for Ramadan; otherwise, it is not”—then this suffices according to Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad, in the sounder of the two reports from the latter. This is the report transmitted by al-Marwadhī and others, and the view chosen by al-Khiraqī in his commentary on al-Mukhtasar, as well as by Abū al-Barakāt and others.

The second view is that it does not suffice unless he intends to fast it as a day of Ramadan. This is one of the two reports from Ahmad, adopted by al-Qādī and a group of Ahmad’s followers.

The underlying question is whether one must specifically intend the fast to be for the month of Ramadan. There are three views on this within Ahmad’s school.

---

## Page 12549

The first view is that his fast does not suffice unless he intends it specifically for Ramadan. If he fasts with a general or conditional intention, or intends a voluntary fast or a fast in fulfilment of a vow, it does not suffice. This is the well-known position in al-Shāfi‘ī’s school and one of the reports from Ahmad.

The second view is that it suffices in all these cases, as in the school of Abū Hanīfah.

The third view is that it suffices with a general intention, but not with an intention specifically designating a fast other than Ramadan. This is the third report from Ahmad, and the view chosen by al-Khiraqī and Abū al-Barakāt.

The correct analysis of this issue is that intention follows knowledge. If a person knows that tomorrow is part of Ramadan, he must specifically intend to fast for Ramadan in this case. If he intends a voluntary fast or merely fasting in general, it does not suffice, because Allah has commanded him to intend to fulfil the obligation incumbent upon him: fasting the month of Ramadan, which he knows to be obligatory. If he does not fulfil that obligation, he has not discharged his liability.

If, however, he does not know that tomorrow is part of Ramadan, he is not required to specify his intention in this way. Whoever requires him to do so despite his lack of knowledge has imposed two mutually incompatible requirements. Thus, if fasting that day is deemed permissible, and he fasts in these circumstances with a general or conditional intention, his fast suffices. But if he intended to fast that day voluntarily, and it then became clear that it had been

---

## Page 12550

part of Ramadan, the more fitting view is that his fast also suffices. This is like someone who unknowingly holds a deposit belonging to another man and gives it to him as a gift, then discovers that it was his by right. He need not give it to him a second time; rather, he says, “What you received was something of yours that I was holding.” Allah knows the true nature of all things.

The report transmitted from Ahmad—that the people follow the imam in his intention—rests on the principle that fasting and breaking the fast depend on what people know, as related in the Sunan collections from the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), who said:

“Your day of fasting is the day you fast, your day of breaking the fast is the day you break the fast, and your day of sacrifice is the day you offer sacrifices.”

People have differed over the term “crescent”: does it denote what rises in the sky even if no one sees it, or is it not called a crescent until people announce its appearance and know of it? There are two views on this in the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. On this rests the disagreement over whether a day when the sky is completely overcast—or a cloudy day generally—is a day of doubt. There are three views on this in the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

The first is that it is not a day of doubt; rather, doubt arises when sighting the crescent is possible. This is the view of many followers of al-Shāfiʿī and others.

The second is that it is a day of doubt, because the crescent may have risen.

---

## Page 12551

The third is that it is legally deemed part of Ramadan and therefore is not a day of doubt. This is the view preferred by a group of Ahmad’s followers and others.

The jurists have disagreed about someone who alone sights the crescent marking the beginning or end of the month of fasting: should he begin and end the fast independently, do neither except with the people, or begin fasting independently but end it with the people? These are three well-known views in the school of Ahmad and elsewhere.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section: Whether Sighting the Crescent in Some Lands Counts as a Sighting for All

There is confusion concerning this question. Ibn ‘Abd al-Barr reported a consensus that the disagreement concerns places whose horizons for sighting the crescent may coincide. As for places such as al-Andalus and Khurāsān, there is no disagreement that a sighting in one is not taken into account for the other.

I say: On this question, Ahmad relied on the hadith concerning “the Bedouin who testified that the crescent had appeared the previous night, whereupon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed the people to act upon this sighting.” This was despite the sighting having occurred outside the town and its not being possible for it to have occurred beyond the distance permitting the shortening of prayers. He did not ask the man for further details. This reasoning does not contradict what Ibn ‘Abd al-Barr mentioned; but where is the boundary to be drawn?

---

## Page 12552

Among those who hold that a sighting does not count for all lands—such as most of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers—some set the boundary at the distance permitting the shortening of prayers, while others base it on differences in the horizons for sighting the crescent, as between the Hijāz and Syria, or Iraq and Khurāsān. Both criteria are weak: the distance permitting the shortening of prayers has no connection with the crescent. As for regional divisions, what determines their boundaries?

Moreover, both criteria are mistaken in two respects:

First, visibility varies according to how far east or west a location lies. Whenever the crescent is sighted in the east, it must also be visible in the west, but the converse does not hold. This is because the sun sets later in the west than in the east. If the crescent has already been sighted in the east, then by sunset in the west it will have grown brighter and moved farther from the sun and its rays, making it all the more visible. The same does not apply when it is sighted in the west, for its visibility there may be due to their later sunset, by which time it has gained in separation and brightness, whereas at sunset in the east it was still close to the sun. Furthermore, by the time it is sighted in the west, it has already set for those in the east.

This is an observable fact concerning the setting of the sun, the crescent, and all other celestial bodies. Thus, when the time for the Maghrib prayer begins in the west, it has already begun in the east, but the converse does not hold. The same applies to sunrise: when the sun has risen in the west, it has already risen in the east, but the converse does not hold. Thus celestial bodies rise and set earlier in the east.

---

## Page 12553

The crescent, however, appears and is sighted earlier in the west, for it emerges from the west, and nothing else in the sky emerges from the west. Its visibility results from its distance from the sun: the later the sun sets, the farther the crescent has moved away from it. Thus, whoever takes the distance between settlements as an absolute criterion has relied on neither a basis in Islamic law nor an observable fact.

Moreover, regarding the crescent marking the month of Hajj, Muslims have always relied on sightings reported by arriving pilgrims, even when the distance exceeded that permitting the shortening of prayers.

The second respect is this: if we adopt a boundary, such as the distance permitting the shortening of prayers or regional boundaries, then a man at the very edge of that distance or region must fast, end his fast, and perform the rites, while another man only a bowshot away does none of these things. This is not part of the religion of the Muslims. The correct view here—and Allah knows best—is that indicated by the saying:

“Your day of fasting is the day you all fast; your day of breaking the fast is the day you all break it; and your day of sacrifice is the day you all offer sacrifice.”

Thus, if a witness testifies on the night preceding the thirtieth of Shaʿbān that he sighted the crescent somewhere, whether near or far, fasting becomes obligatory. Likewise, if he testifies to the sighting during the day following that night, at any time until sunset, they must abstain from whatever invalidates the fast for the remainder of the day, whether one region or two are involved.

---

## Page 12554

And the determining factor is whether knowledge of the sighting reaches them in time to act upon it. If news of the sighting reaches them after sunset, fasting thereafter is obligatory in any case. But must the day already past be made up? They may learn during the month that the crescent was sighted in another region, although it was not sighted anywhere near them. The more likely correct view is that, if it was sighted nearby—meaning somewhere from which news could have reached them on the first day—this is like its having been sighted in their own town without the news reaching them.

If, however, it was sighted somewhere from which news could not have reached them until after the first day had passed, they need not make up that day. For the people’s day of fasting is the day on which they fast, and they can only fast on a day when sighting the crescent is possible for them. Since news of it could not have reached them that day, it was not their day of fasting. The same applies to ending the fast and performing the rites.

But if it becomes established for these people during the month that the crescent had been sighted, should they end their fast on the basis of that sighting? If the news reaches them through a single informant, however, they do not end their fast; for grounds on which to end their fast have become established for them during the month, and they do not make up the first day, so their fasting amounts to twenty-nine days. This accords with the view of those who take differences in lunar horizons into account: if someone begins fasting on the basis of a sighting in one place, then travels somewhere whose people sighted it earlier, he ends his fast with them and does not make up the first day. If their sighting was later, however, the reports transmitted from our scholars differ here: if they say that he should end his fast

---

## Page 12555

alone, then this is like his sighting the crescent while among them: according to the well-known position in our school, he would not end his fast alone. Yet if he fasts with them, he will have fasted thirty-one days. The more likely conclusion is that two positions can be derived within our school on this question, as in the case of someone who alone sights the crescent marking the end of fasting. For the objection in both cases is to a man ending his fast on his own. A sighting by the people of one town, but not others, is like a sighting by him and a group with him, but not others.

As for the crescent marking the end of fasting, if its sighting is established during the day, they act upon it. If this happens afterwards, it serves no purpose—for Eid is the day the people celebrate as Eid—but the date was transmitted (1).

The governing principle is that this matter depends on whether the report reaches people, in accordance with the Prophet’s words:

“Fast when you sight it.”

Whoever receives news that it has been sighted is bound by that sighting, with no distance limit whatsoever. This accords with what Ibn ‘Abd al-Barr mentioned: news between the two extremities of the inhabited world arrives only after a month, so it serves no purpose. This differs from places where the news arrives before the month ends, for there it is taken into account.

Reflect, then, on these four matters: the obligation to fast, abstention from what breaks the fast, the obligation to make up missed days, and the obligation to base Eid on that sighting; and on a distant sighting and the arrival of news after the act of worship has ended.

For this reason they said: if all the people err and stand at ‘Arafah on a day other than the Day of ‘Arafah

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 12556

that suffices for them, since account is taken of whether the information reaches them. But if a group among them mistake the day, their standing does not suffice, since the information could have reached them. Thus, what matters is whether the report reaches people, whether the issue is distance or small numbers. What I have stated agrees with what our fellow scholars have stated, except regarding the obligation to make up missed days when the report could not have reached them.

The evidence for this is that we know with certainty that, throughout the time of the Companions and their Successors, the crescent was sighted in some Muslim towns later than in others. This is an ordinary occurrence that does not change, and news of the sighting must have reached them during the month. Had they been obliged to make up missed days, they would have been as intent on investigating sightings throughout the Muslim lands as they were on investigating sightings in their own town. Making up missed days would also have been frequent in most Ramadans. Had this occurred, it would have been transmitted. Since it was not transmitted, this indicates that it has no basis. The hadith of Ibn ‘Abbās also supports this.

Our fellow scholars have answered that he refrained from ending his fast only because the sighting had been established for him through the testimony of just one person, on whose testimony he would not end his fast.

Nor can it be objected on behalf of our fellow scholars: “Likewise, it has not been transmitted that, when news of the crescent reached them during the month, they based their ending the fast upon it.”

We reply: This is not a matter people are intent on investigating, because it involves forgoing

---

## Page 12557

fasting for a day. If the report is established for them, they act upon it; otherwise, the cautious course is to fast, since that report may be weak. Nevertheless, this question requires further consideration.

There would be grounds for saying that, if the report reaches them during the month, they should rely only on their own sighting, unlike when it reaches them on the first day. Indeed, if a person learns of a sighting by only a few people during the month, it is debatable whether he must make up that day, even if he ends his fast on the basis of that sighting. For the statement,

“Your fasting is on the day you all fast,”

indicates that the earlier day was not a day of fasting for us. Moreover, religious obligation depends on knowledge; where there is neither knowledge nor apparent evidence, there is no obligation. The consistent implication of this is that, if the crescent sighting is established during the day, whether before or after people have eaten, they must complete the day fasting and abstain from whatever breaks the fast, without having to make it up. This is like a child reaching puberty or an insane person regaining sanity, according to the soundest of the three opinions. One opinion requires him both to abstain and to make up the day; another requires neither; and the third requires abstention but not making up the day.

The word hilāl (crescent) is derived from appearing and raising the voice. Thus, if it rises in the sky but does not become known on earth, it has no legal effect, either inwardly or outwardly. Its name is derived from human action: one says ahlalnā al-hilāl and istahlalnāhu. Thus, only a crescent whose sighting is announced constitutes a hilāl. If one or two people sight it but do not report it, it does not constitute a hilāl, and no ruling is established by it until they report it. Their report then constitutes ihlāl, which means raising the voice

---

## Page 12558

to announce it. Moreover, religious obligation depends on knowledge: if knowledge of it is not possible, fasting that day is not obligatory. Requiring a missed fast to be made up when its omission involved no negligence requires evidence.

Furthermore, if making up the fast were obligatory or recommended when news of a sighting in a distant place, or by a small number of people, arrived during the month, then fasting on the day of doubt would be recommended even under clear skies—indeed, fasting on the thirtieth day would be recommended in all circumstances. This is because a few people, or someone far away, might report during the month that they had sighted the crescent; fasting as a precaution would therefore be recommended. Whenever something may be obligatory under the Sharia, taking the precaution of performing it is prescribed. Since such precautionary observance is not prescribed here, we know conclusively that no obligation arises while the observer is distant or unknown. Rather, the observer must be nearby and publicly known, so that his sighting constitutes an announcement making the crescent’s appearance known.

Those who do not take precautions when the sky is overcast may invoke this argument. The response, however, is that its rising in this situation is an evident or comparable instance; it is only the obstruction that prevents sighting. This is like people being in a cave or an underground chamber on the thirtieth night, unable to look for the crescent.

Furthermore, the fundamental reasoning of those who do not require the intention to fast to be formed during the night is that fasting on the day of doubt suffices. News of a sighting often arrives before noon, as with the day of ʿĀshūrāʾ. Requiring that day to be made up entails hardship, because such situations occur frequently, while an obligation to make it up was not widely known among the early generations. The answer is that an obligation to abstain from whatever breaks the fast does not necessarily entail an obligation to make up the day.

---

## Page 12559

for the obligation arises only when the crescent is announced and sighted, not when it rises. Moreover, the consensus reported by Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr supports this: in the case he describes, if the report arrives only after the month has passed, its only practical consequence would be the obligation to make up missed fasts. It is therefore clear that a distant sighting does not oblige anyone to make up missed fasts at all.

To summarize: anyone who receives news of a crescent sighting in time to act upon it in fasting, ending the fast, or performing pilgrimage rites must unquestionably take it into account. The scriptural texts and reports from the early generations support this. Whoever restricts this to the distance permitting the shortening of prayers, or to a particular region, holds a position contrary to both reason and the Sharia.

If the news reaches someone only after an observance has been performed, and that observance cannot be made up—as with an Eid already celebrated or pilgrimage rites—then it has no effect. This is the subject of the consensus reported by Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr.

But if the news reaches someone during the period in question, does it affect the obligation to make up missed fasts, or the determination of when to end fasting on that basis? Likewise, does it affect other rulings, such as when a debt falls due, the period prescribed for an oath of abstinence from marital relations, the expiry of a woman’s waiting period, and similar matters? It appears to me that making up missed fasts is not obligatory; determining the end of fasting on that basis, however, requires further consideration.

This is a middle position on the issue; every other position entails abhorrent implications.

---

## Page 12560

especially those who hold that dates may differ from place to place. For if some or all of the delegations see the crescent and then arrive in Mecca, while it has not been sighted near Mecca, their position entails consequences for the pilgrimage rites that are manifestly contrary to Islam. Owing to the flaws we have mentioned, this position has taken various forms. What we have explained, however, achieves the unity prescribed by the Sharia: each community unites upon whatever it can agree upon. If someone differs from them without their knowing it, because he alone knows something they do not, this does no harm. The problem lies in awareness of division and disagreement.

To clarify this, one must understand the crescent moons. Allah says:

“They are measurements of time for the people and for Hajj.”

This indicates that He meant what is known through sight or hearing. Hence al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him, held that if the sky is clear and no one has sighted the crescent, it is not a day of doubt: there is no uncertainty concerning the crescent, even if there is uncertainty as to whether it has risen.

This can be explained in two ways:

First: the word hilāl (crescent) follows the pattern fiʿāl. In Arabic, this pattern denotes something by means of which an action is performed: izār is what one wraps around the waist; ridāʾ is what one wears over the upper body; rikāb is what is used for riding; wiʿāʾ is that in which, or by means of which, something is contained; simād is what is used to fertilize the soil; ʿisāb is what is used for binding; and sidād is what is used to plug an opening. This is a common and regular pattern in nouns.

---

## Page 12561

Thus hilāl (crescent) is the name for that which is announced aloud. Such an announcement can only follow awareness of it through sight or hearing. This is illustrated by the poet’s words:

Its riders hail al-Farqad,
As does the mounted pilgrim performing the lesser pilgrimage.

That is, they call out at al-Farqad. He thus describes them as making an announcement concerning it; hence the crescent is called hilāl. The same usage occurs in His words:

“And that which has been dedicated to other than Allah.”

That is, something over which an invocation has been uttered. Whether the voice is raised or lowered, it remains something spoken, declared and uttered for other than Allah.

Second: He made them measurements of time for people. They can only serve this purpose if people become aware of them through sight or hearing. Without such awareness, there is no determination of time, and so they are not crescent moons. This is the utmost that can be ascertained through sensory perception, since the place of their rising cannot be accurately determined by calculation at all. I have written something on this subject.

This question also rests on that point, for human beings cannot establish a precisely defined time and place for sighting the crescent. They can ascertain only what they perceive with their eyes or hear with their ears. Thus, if the obligation must depend on sighting for someone who sees it, it must depend on hearing the report for someone who does not. For someone who neither sees it nor hears of it, there is no proclamation of the crescent.

It is Allah whom we ask to perfect His favour upon us and upon the Muslims.

---

## Page 12562

He—may Allah sanctify his soul—was also asked:

A man sights the crescent alone and is certain of his sighting. May he end the fast on his own, or begin fasting on his own, or should he do so with the people generally?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If someone alone sights the crescent marking the beginning or the end of the fast, must he begin fasting or end the fast on the basis of his own sighting? Or should he neither begin nor end the fast except with the people?

There are three views on this, corresponding to three positions reported from Ahmad:

The first: He must begin fasting, and must end the fast privately. This is the view of al-Shāfiʿī.

The second: He begins fasting, but does not end the fast except with the people. This is the well-known position in the schools of Ahmad, Mālik and Abū Hanīfah.

The third: He begins fasting with the people and ends the fast with the people. This is the soundest of

---

## Page 12563

these views, because the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Your fasting is on the day you all fast, your breaking of the fast is on the day you all break the fast, and your sacrifice is on the day you all offer sacrifice.”

Al-Tirmidhī narrated it and graded it sound and singular. Abū Dāwūd and Ibn Mājah also narrated it, mentioning only the breaking of the fast and the sacrifice. Al-Tirmidhī narrated it through ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar, from ʿUthmān ibn Muhammad, from al-Maqburī, from Abū Hurayrah, that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Fasting is on the day you all fast, breaking the fast is on the day you all break the fast, and sacrifice is on the day you all offer sacrifice.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “This is a sound, singular hadith.” He added: “Some scholars explained this hadith as meaning that fasting and breaking the fast are observed with the community and the general body of the people.”

Abū Dāwūd narrated it through another chain. He said: Muhammad ibn ʿUbayd narrated to us; Hammād narrated to us, transmitting the report of Ayyūb, from Muhammad ibn al-Munkadir, from Abū Hurayrah, who mentioned the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in the report, with the words:

“Your breaking of the fast is on the day you all break the fast, and your sacrifice is on the day you all offer sacrifice. All of ʿArafah is a place for standing, all of Minā is a place for sacrifice, every mountain pass of Mecca is a place for sacrifice, and all of Jamʿ (Muzdalifah) is a place for standing.”

Moreover, if he alone saw the crescent marking the sacrifice, it would not thereby become publicly known. The term hilāl (crescent) denotes that which is publicly announced. Allah has made the crescent a means of determining times for people and for Hajj, and this is only achieved when people announce it publicly and the month is evident—even if it is neither a crescent nor a month.

The basis of this issue is that Allah, glorified and exalted, has made certain religious rulings dependent

---

## Page 12564

on what is designated by the terms hilāl (crescent) and shahr (month), as in fasting, breaking the fast, and offering sacrifice. Allah Most High says:

“They ask you about the crescents. Say, ‘They are markers of time for people and for Hajj.’”

Thus He, glorified is He, explained that the crescents are markers of time for people and for Hajj. Allah Most High says:

“Fasting has been prescribed for you,”

up to His words:

“The month of Ramadan in which the Quran was revealed as guidance for mankind.”

He has therefore made fasting the month of Ramadan obligatory, and this is agreed upon among Muslims. What people dispute, however, is whether “crescent” denotes what appears in the sky, even if people are unaware of it, with its appearance marking the beginning of the month; or whether “crescent” denotes what people publicly announce, and “month” denotes what becomes widely known among them. There are two views.

Those who hold the first view say that when someone alone sees the crescent, the appointed time for fasting has begun and Ramadan has commenced for him. That night is, in reality, part of Ramadan, even if no one else knows it. They also say that anyone who did not see it must make up the fast if it becomes clear to him that the crescent had appeared. By analogy, the same would apply to the month of breaking the fast and the month of sacrifice. Yet, regarding the month of sacrifice, I know of no one who has said that a person who alone sees its crescent should stand at ‘Arafah by himself, apart from the other pilgrims, and then, on the following day, offer his sacrifice, stone Jamrat al-‘Aqabah, and come out of ihrām independently of the other pilgrims.

Their disagreement concerns only breaking the fast. Most scholars treated it like offering sacrifice and said that he should break the fast only with the Muslims. Others said that breaking the fast is like fasting, and that Allah has not commanded His servants to fast thirty-one days. The inconsistency of

---

## Page 12565

these views shows that the correct position is the same as that concerning Dhū al-Hijjah.

Accordingly, for the crescent and the month to be recognized, the crescent must be widely known among people and its appearance publicly announced by them. Even if ten people see it, but the sighting does not become widely known among the town’s inhabitants—because their testimony is rejected or because they do not testify to it—the same ruling applies to them as to the rest of the Muslims. Just as they do not stand at ‘Arafah, offer sacrifice, or perform the Eid prayer except with the Muslims, so too they do not fast except with the Muslims. This is the meaning of his statement:

“Your fasting is on the day you fast; your breaking of the fast is on the day you break it; and your day of sacrifice is on the day you offer sacrifice.”

For this reason, Ahmad said, according to a report from him: “He fasts with the imam and the Muslim community, whether the sky is clear or cloudy.” Ahmad said: “Allah’s Hand is over the community.”

On this basis, the rulings concerning the month differ: has the month begun for all the inhabitants of the town, or has it not begun for them all? This is clarified by the words of Allah Most High:

“So whoever among you is present during the month, let him fast it.”

He commanded only those present during the month to fast. Such presence is possible only with a month that has become widely known among people, so that being present during it or absent from it is conceivable.

The Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—“When you see it, fast; and when you see it, break the fast; and fast from one crescent to the next”—and similar statements address the community. However, if someone is in a place where no one else is present, he fasts the month upon seeing the crescent, for he

---

## Page 12566

is the only person there. Accordingly, if he did not fast and it later became clear that the crescent had been sighted elsewhere, or the sighting was established at midday, he would not be required to make up that day. This is one of the two reports from Ahmad, for the month was established for them only when its arrival became evident and widely known.

From that point onward, they were obliged to abstain from whatever invalidates the fast, just like those who were commanded to fast on ‘Āshūrā’ during the course of the day and, according to the correct view, were not commanded to make it up. The hadith concerning making it up is weak. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12567

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

As for the third foundation, it is fasting. Scholars have differed over forming the intention to fast the night before, holding three views.

One group—including Abū Hanīfah—held that any fast, whether obligatory or voluntary, is valid if the intention is formed before solar noon. This is indicated by the hadith concerning ‘Āshūrā’ and the hadith in which the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) entered upon ‘Ā’ishah and, finding no food, said, “Then I am fasting.”
