In contrast, another group—including Mālik—held that no fast, whether obligatory or voluntary, is valid unless the intention is formed the night before, following the apparent meaning of the hadith of Hafsah

---

## Page 12568

and Ibn ‘Umar, which is transmitted both as a report traced back to the Prophet and as a statement of a Companion:

“No fast is valid for anyone who has not formed the intention to fast the night before.”

The third view is that an obligatory fast is valid only if the intention is formed the night before, as indicated by the hadith of Hafsah and Ibn ‘Umar. This is because fasting is required throughout the entire period, and an intention does not apply retroactively. A voluntary fast, however, is valid with an intention formed during the day, as indicated by the Prophet’s words:

“Then I am fasting.”

Likewise, obligatory prayers require certain essential elements—such as standing and remaining on the ground—that are not required in voluntary prayers. Thus Allah grants His servants greater latitude in the ways they may perform voluntary worship. Voluntary acts of worship always allow greater latitude than obligatory ones.

As for their fasting on the day of ‘Āshūrā’, if it was obligatory, it only became obligatory for them during the day, because they had not known of it beforehand. The report transmitted by some later writers on juristic disputation that this occurred in Ramadan is false and has no basis.

This is the most balanced view, and it is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. Their positions varied, however, on whether a voluntary fast is valid if the intention is formed after solar noon. The stronger view is that it is valid, as reported from the Companions.

Their followers differed concerning the reward: is it the reward for a full day, or only from the time

---

## Page 12569

he forms the intention?

Ahmad’s explicit statement is that the reward begins from the time the intention is formed. They also differed over specifying the type of fast in the intention. There are three views on this within Ahmad’s school and elsewhere:

The first is that one must specifically intend to fast Ramadan. Neither a general intention to fast nor an intention specifying a fast other than Ramadan suffices. This is the view of al-Shāfi‘ī and one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, adopted by many of his followers.

The second is that either a general intention to fast or an intention specifying another fast suffices. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and a position also reported from Ahmad.

The third is that a general intention to fast suffices, but not an intention to observe a voluntary fast, make up a missed fast, or fulfil a vow. This is a position reported from Ahmad and adopted by a group of his followers.

---

## Page 12570

Section

They differed over fasting on the day of obscured skies: when clouds or dust haze obstruct the view of the new crescent on the night preceding the thirtieth of Sha‘bān.

Some held that one must fast that day as a precaution, intending it as a fast of Ramadan. This is the position reported from Ahmad that most of his later followers adopted and attributed to most of their predecessors. They based this on their interpretation of the hadith and on the premise that Sha‘bān usually has twenty-nine days. The crescent is therefore more likely to have appeared, as is usually the case, and fasting becomes obligatory on the basis of predominant probability.

Another group held that it is not permissible to fast that day as part of Ramadan. This is a position reported from Ahmad and adopted by some of his followers, including Ibn ‘Aqīl and al-Halwānī. It is also the view of Abū Hanīfah, Mālik and al-Shāfi‘ī. They relied on the relevant hadiths and on the principle that an obligation cannot be established by doubt.

There is a third view: that it is permissible to fast that day as part of Ramadan, and it is permissible

---

## Page 12571

not to fast.

It is preferable, however, to fast beginning at dawn. It is known that if a person knew the time when dawn might break, he would be permitted either to abstain or to eat, even if he abstained at dawn. For there is no basis for regarding abstention as recommended, but … (1).

Most of Ahmad’s recorded statements support precisely this view: he recommended fasting that day and fasted it himself, but did not consider it obligatory. In this, he relied on reports from the Companions recorded in the collections of legal questions transmitted by his son ʿAbd Allāh, al-Fadl ibn Ziyād al-Qattān and others. He followed what was reported from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar and others like him.

What is reported from them is that they would fast when the sky was overcast, without considering fasting obligatory. Most people did not fast, and they did not censure them for refraining.

He did not recommend fasting when the sky was clear; rather, he prohibited it. This is because both the presumption and the apparent circumstances indicate that the crescent has not appeared. Fasting that day would therefore mean beginning Ramadan a day early, which the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prohibited.

Two different positions are reported from him on whether a day of overcast skies is called a “day of doubt.” His followers likewise differed on this matter.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 12572

As for a day of clear skies, in his view it is either a day of doubt or a day certainly belonging to Shaʿbān; fasting on it is prohibited without hesitation. The principles of Islamic law support this position more strongly than any other. When there is doubt about whether something is obligatory—as when someone doubts whether zakah, an expiation, a prayer, or anything else is due—it need not be performed, but neither is refraining from it recommended. Rather, performing it as a precaution is recommended. Thus, the principles of Islamic law neither prohibit taking precautions nor impose an obligation merely on account of doubt.

Furthermore, the beginning of the month is like the beginning of the day. If someone doubts whether dawn has broken, he is neither obliged to abstain nor prohibited from abstaining with the intention of fasting. Moreover, cloud cover at the beginning of the month is comparable to the obscurity arising from doubt. Rather, fasting on the day of doubt is prohibited for fear of adding to the obligatory fast.

This position reconciles most of the reports transmitted from the Companions on this subject. The groups among them who fasted—such as ʿUmar, ʿAlī, Muʿāwiyah, and others—did not explicitly declare it obligatory, while most of those who refrained did not explicitly declare it prohibited.

Perhaps those among them who disapproved of fasting only disapproved of it for someone who believed it obligatory, fearing that something not obligatory would be made obligatory. Similarly, some of them disapproved of cleansing oneself with water after relieving oneself when they feared that a person might come to believe it obligatory. Likewise, a group of them instructed someone who fasted while travelling to make up the fast because they thought he disapproved of breaking his fast while travelling. The disapproval would thus relate to the circumstances of the person performing the act, not to taking the precaution of fasting itself. For prohibiting fasting or making it obligatory

---

## Page 12573

are both far removed from the principles of Islamic law. When the transmitted hadiths on this subject are carefully considered, most explicitly require fasting only after the full count of days has been completed, while some indicate fasting before its completion. As for making fasting obligatory before the count is complete, whether either establishes this is open to question. This middle position is thus supported by most of Ahmad’s explicit statements.

If it were maintained that both fasting and refraining from fasting are permissible, with refraining being recommended, that position would be … (1) from declaring fasting prohibited or obligatory. It is reported from al-Siddīq—may Allah be pleased with him—that they used to eat while uncertain whether dawn had broken.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 12574

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Praise be to Allah, who sent down the Book to His servant and made it an explanation of all things and a reminder for people of understanding. He commanded us to hold fast to it, for it is His rope, the firmest of bonds. Through it He guided us to the paths of guidance and the ways of truth, and in it He told us that He:

“Made the sun a radiant light and the moon a reflected light, and determined phases for it, so that you may know the number of years and the reckoning.”

I bear witness that none is worthy of worship except Allah alone, without partner, the Lord of all lords. And I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger, sent with concise yet comprehensive speech, wisdom, and decisive discourse. May Allah bestow upon him and his family enduring blessings that last until the Day of Return.

To proceed:

Allah has perfected our religion for us, completed His favour upon us, and chosen Islam as our religion. He has commanded us to follow His straight path and not to follow other paths, lest they separate us from His path. He made this injunction the last of His ten commandments, which comprise the comprehensive precepts of the revealed laws and correspond to the words that Allah revealed to Moses in the

---

## Page 12575

Torah, although the words revealed to us are more complete and more eloquent.

For this reason, al-Rabīʿ ibn Khuthaym said:

“Whoever wishes to read the document of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—whose seal has remained unbroken since his passing, let him read the end of Sūrat al-Anʿām: ‘Say, “Come, I will recite what your Lord has forbidden to you”’—and the following verses.”

He commanded us not to be like those who became divided and differed after clear proofs had come to them. He informed His Messenger that he had nothing to do with those who divided their religion and became sects. He stated that He had placed him upon a prescribed religious way, and commanded him to follow it and not to follow the path of those who do not know.

The Most High said:

“And We have sent down to you the Book in truth, confirming the Scripture that came before it and as a guardian over it. So judge between them by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires, turning away from the truth that has come to you. To each of you We have prescribed a law and a way.”

“Had Allah willed, He would have made you one community, but He wished to test you through what He has given you. So compete in good deeds. To Allah you will all return, and He will inform you concerning that over which you differed.”

“And judge between them by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires. Beware of them, lest they tempt you away from some of what Allah has revealed to you.”

Thus, He commanded him not to turn away from the truth that had come to him by following their desires, even if that was a law or path prescribed for another prophet. For He appointed a way and a path for every prophet. He warned him lest they tempt him away from some of what Allah had revealed to him. If this applies to what was brought by another prophet’s revealed law, what, then, of something not known to have been brought by any revealed law, but which is instead the way of those who have no Scripture?

---

## Page 12576

He also commanded him and us in several passages to follow what has been revealed to us, rather than anything that contradicts it. He said:

“Alif-Lām-Mīm-Sād.”

“A Book has been sent down to you—so let there be no unease in your heart concerning it—that you may warn through it, and as a reminder to the believers.”

“Follow what has been sent down to you from your Lord, and do not follow any protectors besides Him. How little you take heed!”

He explained the condition of those who inherited the Scripture but went against it, and of those who held firmly to it, saying:

“Then they were succeeded by a generation who inherited the Scripture, taking the fleeting gains of this world and saying, ‘We will be forgiven’”—up to His words:

“As for those who hold firmly to the Scripture and establish prayer, We certainly do not let the reward of those who set things right go to waste.”

He also said:

“And this is a blessed Book which We have sent down, so follow it and fear Allah, that you may receive mercy.”

“Lest you say, ‘The Scripture was sent down only to two groups before us’”—and the following verses.

He said:

“O Prophet, fear Allah and do not obey the disbelievers and the hypocrites. Indeed, Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

“And follow what is revealed to you from your Lord. Indeed, Allah is fully aware of what you do.”

He also said:

“And hold firmly, all together, to the rope of Allah.”

The rope of Allah is His Book, as the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—explained. He also said:

“And follow what is revealed to you, and be patient until Allah judges.”

There are many other such texts in the Quran and Sunnah which the Muslims unanimously agree must be followed. This is something over which the Muslims have not differed as a general principle. Disputes may, however, arise over its details. Sometimes these occur among recognized scholars concerning “matters open to scholarly judgment”; at other times, those disputing concerning it are people ignorant of religion, or hypocrites

---

## Page 12577

or those who listen to the hypocrites.

Allah—glorified be He—has told us that among us are people who listen to the hypocrites and accept what they say:

“Had they gone out among you, they would have added nothing but disorder, and would have hurried about in your midst, seeking to sow discord among you; and among you are those who listen to them.”

The verb takes the preposition lām (“to”) because it implies acceptance and obedience, as Allah says through His servant:

“Allah hears whoever praises Him.”

This means that He responds to whoever praises Him. Likewise, “those who listen to them” means those who obey them. If there were people among the Companions who listened to the hypocrites, what, then, of others?

He has likewise told us about those who outwardly submit to the judgment of the Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—saying:

“Do not let those who rush into disbelief grieve you: those who say with their mouths, ‘We believe,’ but whose hearts do not believe, and those among the Jews who listen eagerly to falsehood and listen to other people who have not come to you”—up to His words:

“They listen eagerly to falsehood and devour unlawful gain.”

The correct view is that this lām introduces the object, as in His words, “devour unlawful gain.” That is, they profess falsehood and desire it, and they listen and obey other people besides you; they do not reserve their obedience exclusively for Allah and His Messenger.

Whoever says that the lām expresses purpose—meaning that they listen in order to lie for the sake of those people—is mistaken. The context indicates that the first meaning is intended. The truth is often lost between the ignorant and unlettered on the one hand, and those who distort words and possess a trait of hypocrisy on the other, as He—glorified be He—has told us concerning the People of the Book

---

## Page 12578

where He says:

“Do you then hope that they will believe you, when a group of them used to hear the words of Allah and then knowingly distort them after understanding them?”

—up to His words:

“And among them are unlettered people who know nothing of the Scripture except mere recitation,”

and the rest of the verse.

Since the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—foretold that this community would follow the ways of those before it as closely as one arrow-feather matches another—“even if they entered a lizard’s burrow, you would enter it too”—there must be people among them who distort words from their proper places, changing the meaning of the Quran and Sunnah concerning what Allah has declared or commanded. Among them are also unlettered people who do not understand the meanings of the Quran and Sunnah. Indeed, they may imagine that their mere recitation and superficial acquaintance with the words represent the utmost attainment in religion.

They may then debate those who distort the texts and other hypocrites or disbelievers, although their opponents know things unknown to these unlettered people. Either both groups go astray, and the words of the unlettered become a stumbling block for their opponents, who believe that what the unlettered say represents the utmost extent of religious knowledge, so that the two groups end up at opposite extremes; or those unlettered people follow those who distort the texts in some of their errors. This is one of the causes of the alteration of religions. This religion, however, is preserved, as the Most High says:

“Indeed, it is We who sent down the Reminder, and indeed, We will preserve it.”

There will always remain within it a group steadfastly upholding the truth and prevailing. Thus, it has not suffered the wholesale distortion of its scriptures and alteration of its laws that other religions have suffered, because of what He causes to be proclaimed

---

## Page 12579

by those who uphold Allah’s proof and clear signs, who bring the dead to life through Allah’s Book and give sight to the blind through its light. For the earth will never be without someone who upholds Allah’s proof, lest His proofs and clear signs be nullified.

What prompted me to offer this “introduction” was that I observed people, both during their month of fasting and at other times, listening to what certain ignorant practitioners of astronomical calculation say about whether the crescent can or cannot be seen. They base their position on this, either inwardly alone or both inwardly and outwardly.

I have even been told of judges who would reject the testimony of a number of upright witnesses because an ignorant, lying practitioner declared that the crescent could or could not be seen. Such a judge would thus be among those who reject the truth when it comes to them. He might also accept the testimony of an unreliable witness on that practitioner’s word, thereby becoming one of those who eagerly listen to falsehood. For the verse encompasses corrupt judges, as the context indicates where He says:

“They eagerly listen to falsehood and consume unlawful gains.”

Corrupt judges accept falsehood from informants or witnesses whose statements may not rightfully be accepted, and they consume unlawful gains through bribes and other means. How often these two go together!

There are also those who accept the astronomer’s statement neither inwardly nor outwardly, yet harbour resentment over the matter and serious misgivings because of their confidence in him: they question why the Sharia has paid no regard to this, especially if they have acquired some knowledge of calculations concerning the sun and moon

---

## Page 12580

and the conjunction of the two discs, their separation by a number of degrees, and the causes of the crescent’s appearance, the full moon, the moon’s disappearance, and solar and lunar eclipses. He thus accords the same standing to the judgment of the lying calculator who knows nothing of crescent sighting.

Now, those who give accurate accounts of astronomical calculations and the configuration and movements of the celestial spheres may encounter opposition from ignorant, unlettered people, whether professing faith or unbelief. Such a critic sees them contravening religion by relying on calculation to determine crescent visibility or by following astrological judgments concerning the stars’ beneficial and harmful influences. Seeing them engage in this—which religion forbids—he begins to reject everything they say on such matters. He fails to distinguish between the truth supported by revelation and reason and the falsehood that contradicts revelation and reason.

Yet this person is in a better religious condition than the first group, for he has denied some truth through mistaken interpretation and ignorance, without altering any of Islam’s foundations. The first group, however, may go so far as to alter Islam itself.

For it is necessarily known in the religion of Islam that, in matters of crescent sighting for fasting, Hajj, the waiting period, oaths of marital abstinence, or any other rulings tied to the crescent, it is impermissible to act on a calculator’s report that it can or cannot be seen. Numerous, widely transmitted texts from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, establish this. The Muslims have reached consensus on it. No early disagreement whatsoever is known on this matter, nor any later disagreement, except that

---

## Page 12581

some later students of jurisprudence, who emerged after the third century AH, claimed that when clouds obscure the crescent, a calculator may rely on computation for his own practice: if his calculations indicate that the crescent would be visible, he fasts; otherwise, he does not. Although this view is restricted to cloudy conditions and to the calculator himself, it is an anomalous opinion preceded by a consensus to the contrary. As for following such calculations under clear skies, or making the ruling applicable to everyone dependent upon them, no Muslim has advocated this.

This may approach the view of those Ismā'īlīs who rely on numerical reckoning rather than the crescent. Some of them attribute to Ja'far al-Sādiq a table to be followed, but it was fabricated in his name by 'Abd Allāh ibn Mu'āwiyah. These views lie outside the religion of Islam, and Allah has exonerated Ja'far and others of them. Undoubtedly, while the religion of Islam is manifest, no one can openly profess reliance on this. Nevertheless, someone may privately make it his basis for accepting or rejecting testimony, or harbour misgivings about the fact that the Sacred Law has not made the ruling dependent upon it.

Allah willing, I shall explain this and clarify what the Sacred Law has prescribed, presenting the evidence and its rationale in terms of both revelation and reason.

Allah Most High says:

“They ask you about the new moons. Say, ‘They are measurements of time for the people and for Hajj.’”

He thus states that they mark times for people, which encompasses all their affairs. He singles out Hajj for mention to distinguish it, because the angels and others attend it, and because it takes place in the last month of the year. It therefore serves as a marker for the year, just as the crescent

---

## Page 12582

marks the month. For this reason, they call a year a hijjah (pilgrimage), saying, “He is seventy hijjahs old,” and “We stayed for five hijjahs.” Allah has thus made the crescents markers of time for people in matters of worship established by the Sacred Law, whether directly or through a cause, and in rulings arising from a person’s stipulations. Whatever time-bound matter is established by revelation or stipulation, the crescent marks its appointed time. This includes fasting, Hajj, the period prescribed for an oath of abstention from one’s wife, the waiting period, and expiatory fasting. These five are mentioned in the Quran.

Allah Most High says:

“The month of Ramadan.”

And He Most High says:

“Hajj is during well-known months.”

And He Most High says:

“For those who swear to abstain from their wives is a waiting period of four months.”

And He Most High says:

“Then fasting for two consecutive months.”

Likewise, He says:

“So travel freely throughout the land for four months.”

The same applies to fasting undertaken by a vow and other such fasting. It also applies to stipulations concerning work connected with payment, debts arising from advance-payment sales, zakat, the poll tax, blood money, contractual options, oaths, deferred dowers, instalments under manumission contracts, settlements in lieu of retaliation, and all other debts, contracts and matters for which a term is set.

He Most High says:

“And the moon—We have determined phases for it, until it returns like an old date stalk.”

And He Most High says:

“It is He who made the sun a radiance and the moon a light, and determined phases for it, so that you may know the number of years and the reckoning. Allah did not create this except in truth.”

His words, “so that you may know,” are connected—and Allah knows best—to His words, “and determined phases for it,” not to “made.” For the fact that the former is

---

## Page 12583

a radiance and the latter a light has no bearing on knowing the number of years and the reckoning; rather, what matters for this is movement from one zodiacal sign to another. Moreover, our reckoning of months and years has not been tied to the sun, but to the crescent, as that verse indicates. He has also said:

“Indeed, the number of months with Allah is twelve months, in Allah’s decree on the day He created the heavens and the earth; four of them are sacred.”

He thus states that the months number twelve, and it is self-evident that a month is lunar. It follows that each is known by the crescent. I have also been informed that the religious laws preceding ours likewise tied their rulings to the crescents. Changes were introduced only by those among their followers who altered them. This is what the Jews do in using the conjunction of the solar and lunar discs and in fixing some of their festivals by the reckoning of the solar year. Likewise, in determining their fast, the Christians take account of the conjunction near the beginning of the solar year, while making their other festivals follow the solar year according to the events in the life of the Messiah.

The Sabians, Magians and other polytheists likewise follow conventions of their own. Some reckon solely by the solar year and have their own conventions concerning the number of its months; for although the year itself is natural, its division into months is numerical and conventional. Others reckon by the lunar year, but use the conjunction of the two discs.

What the Sacred Law has prescribed is the most complete, the best, the clearest, the soundest and the least susceptible to inconsistency.

---

## Page 12584

This is because the crescent is something observable, visible to the eyes. What is seen with the eyes is among the most certain things we know. Hence it is called hilāl (crescent), for this root conveys the meaning of becoming apparent and clear, whether to the ear or to the eye. Thus one says ahalla bi-l-‘umrah (he raised his voice proclaiming ‘umrah) and ahalla bi-l-dhabīhati li-ghayr Allāh (he raised his voice invoking someone other than Allah over the sacrificial animal). The sound of falling rain is called halal. One also says istahalla al-janīn when a newborn emerges crying, and tahallala wajhuhu when someone’s face becomes bright and radiant.

It has also been said that the root originally meant raising the voice. Since people would raise their voices upon sighting the crescent, they called it hilāl. Hence the verse:

Its riders raise their voices at al-Farqad,
As does the mounted pilgrim performing ‘umrah.

The expression tahallul al-wajh (the brightening of the face) is derived from the crescent’s radiance.

The point is that the appointed times have been determined by something manifest and clear, equally accessible to everyone. Nothing else shares this quality with the crescent. The conjunction of the sun and moon—their alignment before the crescent appears—is a hidden occurrence, known only through calculations mastered by certain people alone. These involve toil, the loss of much time, and distraction from what matters to people and from their indispensable needs. They may also be subject to error and disagreement.

---

## Page 12585

Likewise, the sun’s alignment with this or that sign of the zodiac cannot be perceived by sight. It can only be determined through obscure, specialized, difficult calculations that are liable to error. Through sensory perception, it can be known only approximately.

When winter ends and the season the Arabs call “summer,” which people call “spring,” begins, this is the time when the sun reaches the equinoctial point at the beginning of Aries. The same applies in autumn. What can be perceived through the senses, then, is winter and summer, and the two equinoxes between them, approximately. As for the sun’s passage from one sign to another, this can be known only through calculations that require effort and distract from other pursuits, despite their limited benefit. It is thus clear that the crescent alone provides an observable, universally recognizable boundary for determining appointed times.

The customs of nations in reckoning their months and years fall into all the logically possible categories: the month and year may both be defined numerically, or both by natural cycles; alternatively, the month may follow a natural cycle and the year be defined numerically, or vice versa.

Those who define both numerically include those who make the month thirty days and the year twelve months. Those who define both by natural cycles include those who make the month lunar and the year solar, adding at the end of the months the days accounting for the difference between

---

## Page 12586

the two years. The lunar year consists of three hundred and fifty-four days and a fraction of a day—a fifth or a sixth. It is spoken of as three hundred and sixty days only by rounding up, in keeping with customary practice—the Arab practice of rounding up incomplete units when reckoning days, months, and years.

The solar year, however, consists of three hundred and sixty-five days and a fraction of a day: one quarter. Thus, the difference between them is just under eleven days, amounting to a year every thirty-three and one-third years. Hence Allah, Exalted is He, says:

“And they remained in their cave for three hundred years and exceeded that by nine.”

It has been said that this means three hundred solar years, with “and exceeded that by nine” referring to reckoning by lunar years. Observing both these cycles is customary among many peoples who follow the two Scriptures, owing to the alterations they have made. I believe this was also the practice of the Magi.

As for those who define the year by a natural cycle and the month numerically, this is the reckoning of the Romans, Syrians, Copts, and others like them among the Sabians and polytheists. They reckon the month of Kānūn and similar months by a fixed number of days, while determining the solar year by the sun’s course.

The fourth category is to define the month by a natural cycle and the year numerically. This is the practice of the Muslims and those who follow the same approach. Now, those who define the year by a natural cycle do not rely

---

## Page 12587

on anything readily observable, as noted above; rather, they must resort to calculation and counting. Likewise, those who define the month by a natural cycle but rely on conjunction must resort to counting and calculation. Moreover, what they calculate is something obscure, known only to a few people, and involves effort, hardship, and the risk of error.

What our revealed Law has brought is therefore the most perfect arrangement: it determines the month by a natural, readily observable phenomenon, visible to everyone. Thus, no one goes astray from his religion, nor does attending to it distract him from anything beneficial, involve him in matters that do not concern him, or become a means of introducing confusion into Allah’s religion, as some scholars of other faiths do with their own religions.

As for the year, it has no readily observable boundary in the sky, so calculation and counting are unavoidable. Counting lunar months was therefore clearer and more accessible to everyone than reckoning by the sun’s course, while also making the year correspond to the months. Moreover, when years accumulate, all nations customarily have to count them, since a succession of years has no celestial boundary by which their number can be known. The number of months was thus made to correspond to the number of zodiacal signs: the year was set at twelve months, matching the number of signs through which the sun completes its circuit in one solar year. When the moon has circulated through them, it has completed its annual circuit.

All this makes clear the meaning of His words:

“and determined for it phases, so that you may know the number of years

---

## Page 12588

and the reckoning.” For counting the months of the year and the years in succession is fundamentally based on the moon’s appointed phases. The same applies to knowing the reckoning: months are counted by the crescent moon to determine the deadlines and other appointed times that fall within them. Likewise, the Most High says:

“Say, ‘They are markers of time for people and for the pilgrimage.’”

What we have explained thus makes clear that the crescent moon determines the timing of the month and the year, and that nothing whatsoever can take its place. It is readily visible, as is the count based upon it; this method is easy and accessible to everyone, and offers other benefits free of harmful consequences.

Whoever recognizes the confusion, hardship, and other harmful consequences that have affected the followers of the two Scriptures, the Sabians, the Magians, and others in their festivals, worship, calendars, and other affairs will become more grateful for the blessing of Islam. They themselves agree that the prophets prescribed none of this. Rather, it came to them through the Sabian philosophizers, who introduced these things into their religion and prescribed for them religious practices that Allah had not authorized.

We have therefore set out this discussion to safeguard this religion against the intrusions of those who would corrupt it, for this is among the matters whose alteration is to be feared. Indeed, in their pre-Islamic ignorance, the Arabs had altered the religion of Abraham through the intercalation they invented: they added a month to the year, making it an intercalary year, for purposes of their own.

---

## Page 12589

and through it they altered the appointed times of the pilgrimage and the sacred months, so that they would perform the pilgrimage sometimes in Muharram and sometimes in Safar, until it eventually returned to Dhū al-Hijjah. This continued until Allah sent the one who restored the religion of Abraham. When he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed his Farewell Pilgrimage, time had come full circle, and his pilgrimage fell in Dhū al-Hijjah. In his famous sermon, recorded in the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere, he said:

“Time has come full circle, returning to its state on the day Allah created the heavens and the earth. The year consists of twelve months, four of which are sacred: three consecutive months—Dhū al-Qa‘dah, Dhū al-Hijjah, and Muharram—and Rajab of Mudar, which falls between Jumādā and Sha‘bān.”

Before that, the pilgrimage had not fallen in Dhū al-Hijjah; even Abū Bakr’s pilgrimage in the year nine took place in Dhū al-Qa‘dah. This was one of the reasons why the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, delayed performing the pilgrimage.

Allah Most High revealed:

“Indeed, the number of months with Allah is twelve months, in the decree of Allah from the day He created the heavens and the earth; of these, four are sacred. That is the upright religion.”

Allah thus declared that this is the upright religion, making clear that alternatives involving intercalation and other customary practices of nations are not upright, because of the deviation and disorder they entail. The day and the week are analogous to the month and the year. The day is a natural unit, extending from the rising of

---

## Page 12590

the sun to its setting. The week, however, is a numerical unit, based on the six days in which Allah created the heavens and the earth, then rose over the Throne.

Thus, a balance is achieved between the sun and the moon: the day and the week are reckoned by the sun’s course, while the month and the year are reckoned by the moon’s course. Through both, the reckoning is complete. In light of this, His words “so that you may know” may be understood as referring back to “made,” meaning that He made the sun and the moon for all these purposes.

As for His words, Most High:

“And He made the night for rest, and the sun and the moon for reckoning,”

and His words:

“The sun and the moon follow a precise reckoning,”

some have said that the word husbān derives from calculation. Others have interpreted it as a turning like that of a millstone—that is, the rotation of the celestial sphere. This is a matter concerning which there is no disagreement. Indeed, the Quran and Sunnah indicate, and the scholars of the Muslim community unanimously affirm, the same conclusion maintained by specialists in astronomical calculation: that the celestial spheres are round, not flat.

---

## Page 12591

Section

Since what we have explained establishes that appointed times are reckoned by the new moons, all such times must be tied to them.

Muslims do not disagree that when a legally prescribed period begins at the start of a lunar month, all its months are reckoned by the new moons. Examples include beginning an expiatory fast at the start of Muharram; a woman’s husband dying at the start of Muharram; a man swearing to abstain from intercourse with his wife at the start of Muharram; or selling someone something at the start of Muharram with payment due in two or three months. All these months are reckoned by the new moons, even if some or all of them are short months.

If, however, the legally prescribed period begins partway through a month, one view holds that all the months are reckoned by counting days. Thus, if someone sells something with payment due in a year, beginning partway through Muharram, he counts 360 days; if payment is due in six months, he counts 180 days. If the period begins in the middle of Muharram, it ends on the twentieth of Muharram.

Another view holds that the first month is completed by counting days, while the remaining months are reckoned by the new moons. Both views have been transmitted from Ahmad and others. Some jurists make distinctions in certain rulings.

---

## Page 12592

This latter view, in turn, has two interpretations. The first is that the first month is reckoned as thirty days, while the remaining months are reckoned by the new moons. Thus, if an oath to abstain from intercourse with one’s wife is sworn in the middle of Muharram, the remaining days of that month are counted. If it is a short month, fourteen days are counted from it, and the month is completed with sixteen days from Jumādā al-Ūlā. This is the view of some scholars of our school and others.

The second interpretation is the correct one, followed by Muslims in practice both in earlier times and today: if the first month is full, thirty days are reckoned for it; if it is short, twenty-nine days are reckoned for it. Thus, whenever the oath is sworn in the middle of Muharram, the four months end in the middle of Jumādā al-Ūlā. The same applies to all other such calculations.

According to this view, the entire period is reckoned by the new moons, and there is no need to speak of counting days. Rather, we identify the day in the first month on which the period begins, and it ends on the corresponding day of the later month. If it begins on the first night of the first month, it ends at the same hour once the months have elapsed—that is, on the first night following those months. If it begins on the tenth day of Muharram, it ends on the tenth day of Muharram or another month, depending on the number of months being reckoned. This is the truth from which there is no departing.

This is indicated by His words:

“Say, ‘They are measures of time for people.’”

He thus made them measures of time for all people, although He—glory be to Him—knows that matters arising during the months far outnumber those arising at their beginnings. Thus, if they served as measures of time only for matters that

---

## Page 12593

arise at their beginnings, they would serve as measures of time for less than one-thirtieth of people’s affairs.

Moreover, if a month is the interval between two new moons, then that interval is exactly equal to the interval between the middle of one month and the middle of the next. This equality is self-evident, and distinguishing between them is wholly arbitrary.

Furthermore, who determined that a month reckoned by counting days must be thirty days, when the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“The month is like this, and this, and this,” folding in his thumb the third time.

We know that half the months of the year have thirty days and half have twenty-nine. Moreover, Muslims generally, in their worship and dealings, reckon an obligation deferred for a year as follows: if its term begins with the new moon of Muharram, it ends with the new moon of Muharram, at the close of Dhū al-Hijjah. If it begins on the tenth of Muharram, it likewise ends on the tenth of Muharram. Muslims know no other practice and base their reckoning solely upon it. Whoever proceeds to add a day because the first month was short has altered the familiar practice that comes naturally to them and introduced something alien that they do not recognize.

It is thus clear that this is an error on the part of those jurists who supposed it to be correct. We have drawn attention to it so that people may avoid falling into it and understand the true meaning of His words:

“Say, ‘They are measures of time for people.’”

This generality holds without exception and is of great significance: nothing is excluded from it.

---

## Page 12594

Likewise, His words:

“It is He who made the sun a radiance and the moon a light, and ordained its phases so that you may know the number of years and the reckoning.”

And likewise, His words:

“We made the night and the day two signs. We effaced the sign of the night and made the sign of the day illuminating, so that you may seek bounty from your Lord and know the number of years and the reckoning.”

He thereby makes clear that all reckoning of years and calculation depends upon His ordaining its phases.

Section

There is no doubt about what we have stated: rulings such as fasting Ramadan are tied to the new moons. However, the means of knowing that the crescent has appeared is sighting it, and nothing else. This is established by revelation and reason.

As for revelation, several people informed us, including our teacher, Imam Abū Muhammad ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Muhammad al-Maqdisī, Abū al-Ghanā’im al-Muslim ibn ‘Uthmān al-Qaysī, and others. They said: We were informed by Hanbal ibn ‘Abd Allāh, the muezzin, who said: We were informed by Abū al-Qāsim ‘Abd Allāh ibn Muhammad ibn al-Husayn, who said: We were informed by Abū ‘Alī ibn al-Mudhhib, who said: We were informed by Abū Bakr

---

## Page 12595

Ahmad ibn Ja‘far ibn Hamdān, who said: We were informed by Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān ‘Abd Allāh ibn Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Hanbal, who said: My father informed us, saying: Muhammad ibn Ja‘far, Ghundar, narrated to us, saying: Shu‘bah narrated to us from al-Aswad ibn Qays, who said: I heard Sa‘īd ibn ‘Umar ibn Sa‘īd relate that he heard Ibn ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with them both, narrate that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“We are an unlettered nation: we neither write nor calculate. The month is like this, this, and this”—and he folded his thumb on the third gesture—“and the month is like this, this, and this,” meaning a full thirty days.

Ahmad also said: ‘Abd al-Rahmān narrated to us from Sufyān; and Ishāq—that is, al-Azraq—narrated to us, saying: Sufyān informed us, from al-Aswad ibn Qays, from Sa‘īd ibn ‘Umar, from Ibn ‘Umar, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“We are an unlettered nation: we neither write nor calculate. The month is like this, this, and this,” meaning that he indicated twenty-nine. Ishāq said: He brought his hands together three times, folding his thumb on the third occasion.

Al-Bukhārī recorded it from Ādam, from Shu‘bah, with the wording:

“We are an unlettered nation: we neither write nor calculate. The month is like this, this, and this,” meaning sometimes twenty-nine and sometimes thirty.

Abū Dāwūd likewise narrated it from Sulaymān ibn Harb, from Shu‘bah, with the wording:

“We are an unlettered nation: we neither write nor calculate. The month is like this, this, and this”—and Sulaymān folded his finger on the third gesture—meaning twenty-nine and thirty.

It was narrated by

---

## Page 12596

al-Nasā’ī through ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Mahdī, from Sufyān, as we have mentioned, and also through Ghundar, from Shu‘bah, as we have related. At the end, he said, “a full thirty,” without saying “meaning.”

Its transmission through the Musnad, as we have related it, is the most distinguished and highly regarded of its routes. Ghundar is more eminent than all the others who narrated it from Shu‘bah and more precise in transmitting his hadith, while Imam Ahmad is the most eminent of those who narrated it from Ghundar, from Shu‘bah. This narration with its chain of transmission, recorded by al-Bukhārī, Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasā’ī through Shu‘bah, explains al-Thawrī’s narration and the other narrations from Ibn ‘Umar whose lack of detail may lead to a misunderstanding of Ibn ‘Umar’s meaning.

One example is what we have narrated through the aforementioned route: Ahmad said: Muhammad ibn Ja‘far and Bahz narrated to us, saying: Shu‘bah narrated to us from Jabalah—he specified to us, “Ibn Suhaym.” Bahz said: Jabalah ibn Suhaym informed me: I heard Ibn ‘Umar say:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, ‘The month is like this,’ and he brought his fingers together twice, folding his thumb on the third occasion.”

Muhammad ibn Ja‘far added in his narration: “Meaning twenty-nine.”

Al-Bukhārī and al-Nasā’ī likewise narrated it through Shu‘bah, with the wording:

“The month is like this and this”—and he folded his thumb on the third occasion.

Another example is what Nāfi‘ narrated from Ibn ‘Umar, as we have related through the previously mentioned chain to Ahmad: Ismā‘īl narrated to us, saying: Ayyūb informed us, from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The month is only twenty-nine days. Do not begin fasting until you see it, and do not end your fasting until you see it. If it is obscured from you, then reckon it.”

Nāfi‘ said: When twenty-nine days of Sha‘bān had passed, ‘Abd Allāh would send someone to look for it

---

## Page 12597

, and if it was sighted, that settled the matter. If it was not sighted and neither clouds nor dust haze obstructed the view, he would not fast the following morning. But if clouds or dust haze obstructed the view, he would fast the following morning.

We have also related it from the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd through Hammād ibn Zayd, who said: Ayyūb informed us—with precisely the same wording. Its wording is:

“The month is twenty-nine days.”

At the end, he said: When twenty-nine days of Sha‘bān had passed, someone would look for the crescent on Ibn ‘Umar’s behalf. If it was sighted, that settled the matter. If it was not sighted and neither clouds nor dust haze obstructed the view, he would not fast the following morning. But if clouds or dust haze obstructed the view, he would fast the following morning. He said: Ibn ‘Umar would end his fasting with the people and would not follow this reckoning.

‘Abd al-Razzāq also narrated it with the first wording in his Musannaf, from Ma‘mar, from Ayyūb, from Nāfi‘, from Ibn ‘Umar, from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“The month is only twenty-nine days.”

Through the same chain, he related that Ibn ‘Umar would fast the following morning if there were clouds, but would not fast if there were no clouds.

He said: Ma‘mar also informed us, from Ibn Tāwūs, from his father, of the same practice.

‘Ubayd Allāh ibn ‘Umar likewise narrated it from Nāfi‘, as we have related through the previously mentioned chain to Ahmad: Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd narrated to us, from ‘Ubayd Allāh: Nāfi‘ narrated to me, from Ibn ‘Umar, that when it was the twenty-ninth night and there were clouds or dust haze in the sky, he would fast the following morning.

Al-Nasā’ī narrated it from ‘Amr ibn ‘Alī, from Yahyā. His wording is:

“Do not begin fasting until you see the crescent, and do not end your fasting until you see it,

---

## Page 12598

and if it is obscured from you, make your reckoning.” He mentioned that Muhammad ibn Bishr narrated from ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn ‘Amr, from Abū al-Zinād, from al-A‘raj, from Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, mentioned the crescent and said: ‘When you see it, begin fasting, and when you see it, end your fasting. If it is obscured from you, count thirty days.’”

He regarded this as a discrepancy in the transmissions from ‘Ubayd Allāh. Such a discrepancy does not undermine a report unless there is some further indication warranting this. Masters of hadith such as al-Zuhrī, ‘Ubayd Allāh, and others may possess a hadith through two, three, or more routes. Sometimes they narrate it through one route, and sometimes through another. This occurs frequently in the two Sahīh collections and elsewhere. It becomes evident when a narrator distinguishes between two teachers or relates both reports together.

Al-Bukhārī narrated it through Nāfi‘, from Mālik ibn Anas’s narration from him, with the following wording:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, mentioned the month of Ramadan and said: ‘Do not begin fasting until you see the crescent, and do not end your fasting until you see it. If it is obscured from you, make your reckoning.’”

He did not mention at its beginning the words:

“The month is twenty-nine days.”

Nor did he mention the additional passage, in keeping with his practice of frequently refraining from narrating what he himself did not act upon.

As for the words:

“The month is twenty-nine days,”

Mālik narrated them through ‘Abd Allāh ibn Dīnār, from Ibn ‘Umar. Al-Bukhārī also narrated them through Mālik

---

## Page 12599

from ‘Abd Allāh ibn Maslamah, namely al-Qa‘nabī, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The month is twenty-nine nights, so do not begin fasting until you see it. If it is obscured from you, complete the count as thirty.”

This wording appears in abridged form in al-Bukhārī, who narrated it from al-Qa‘nabī, from Mālik. It is incomplete, for the wording in al-Muwatta’ is “days.” Al-Qa‘nabī’s wording is that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The month is twenty-nine days, so do not begin fasting until you see the crescent, and do not end your fasting until you see it. If it is obscured from you, make your reckoning.”

Thus, he included the words “and do not end your fasting until you see it,” and used the wording “make your reckoning,” not “complete the count.” Likewise, in all the other transmissions of al-Muwatta’, the two clauses precede it, and the wording is “reckoning.” Indeed, Abū ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-Barr said: “There is no disagreement in the transmissions from Nāfi‘ of this hadith regarding the words ‘make your reckoning.’” He added: “Sālim likewise narrated it from Ibn ‘Umar.”

He also related the hadith transmitted by Mālik and others from ‘Abd Allāh ibn Dīnār, from Ibn ‘Umar, and said: “Al-Darāwardī narrated it from ‘Abd Allāh ibn Dīnār with the wording: ‘If it is obscured from you, count the days.’”

This, then—and Allah knows best—is an omission and a narration by meaning in Mālik’s hadith as recorded in al-Bukhārī, just as Abū Bakr al-Ismā‘īlī and others mentioned that something similar occurred in this chapter in the wording of Abū Hurayrah’s hadith. A similar wording, suggesting exclusivity, occurs in what we have also narrated through the previously mentioned chain of transmission to

---

## Page 12600

Ahmad:

Hasan ibn Mūsā narrated to us: Shaybān narrated to us, from Yahyā, who said: Abū Salamah informed me, saying: I heard Ibn ‘Umar say: I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say:

“The month is twenty-nine days.”

Al-Nasā’ī also narrated it with this wording through Mu‘āwiyah, from Yahyā.

He also related it through ‘Alī, from Yahyā, from Abū Salamah, who reported that Abū Hurayrah said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The month may be twenty-nine days or thirty. When you see the crescent, end your fasting; if it is obscured from you, complete the count.”

Al-Nasā’ī regarded this as a discrepancy in the reports transmitted from Yahyā, from Abū Salamah. The correct view, however, is that both reports are reliably preserved from Yahyā, from Abū Salamah; there is no discrepancy in the wording.

Ahmad said: Muhammad ibn Ja‘far narrated to us: Shu‘bah narrated to us, from ‘Uqbah ibn Hurayth, who said: I heard Ibn ‘Umar say: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The month is twenty-nine days.”

Shu‘bah brought his hands together three times, folding his thumb on the third. ‘Uqbah said: I think he also said, “The month is thirty days,” bringing his palms together three times.

Al-Nasā’ī narrated it through Ibn al-Muthannā, from Ghundar, but his wording was only:

“The month is twenty-nine days.”

He added nothing further. Ahmad’s narration is therefore more complete and better presented, as noted earlier. The explanatory narration makes clear that all the other reports from Ibn ‘Umar containing the words “The month is twenty-nine days” mean one of two things: either that the month

---

## Page 12601

may be twenty-nine days, refuting those who suppose that a month, without qualification, consists of thirty days. Some earlier scholars held this mistaken view, and some jurists followed them regarding a month reckoned by a fixed number of days, invariably making it thirty days. Others opposed them, saying:

“The month is twenty-nine days, and the final day is an addition.”

This meaning was explicitly stated by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when he said:

“The month is like this, like this, and like this; and the month is like this and like this.”

That is, sometimes thirty days and sometimes twenty-nine. Whoever categorically declares it to be thirty or twenty-nine days is therefore mistaken.

The second meaning is that he intended the constant, invariable number of days in a month to be twenty-nine. The additional day is merely a possibility: it occurs in some months but not in others. The point is that these twenty-nine days must always be counted and taken into account, in every circumstance. Thus, fasting may under no circumstances begin until twenty-nine days of Sha‘bān have elapsed, and twenty-nine days must be fasted in Ramadan; under no circumstances may fewer days be fasted.

This is the meaning by which Ayyūb’s narration from Nāfi‘ is to be explained:

“The month is only twenty-nine days. So do not begin fasting until you see it, and do not end fasting until you see it.”

That is, the constant, invariable, obligatory count of the month is twenty-nine days. Nor is it possible to interpret

---

## Page 12602

this wording in the first sense, because it expresses exclusivity.

It has also been said that this may refer to a particular month rather than to months in general: that is, “That month is only twenty-nine days,” as though he meant the month in which he swore to abstain from his wives. However, this is ruled out by his words immediately afterward:

“So do not begin fasting until you see it, and do not end fasting until you see it. If it is obscured from you, then reckon it.”

This shows that he said this to explain the general religious ruling concerning months, not a particular month, since he made clear that he mentioned it in connection with fasting.

Had he meant a particular month which he knew to have twenty-nine days, then, knowing that it had twenty-nine days, there would have been no difference between its being obscured and its being visible. Nor would he have said:

“So do not begin fasting until you see it.”

Moreover, this could not be known unless the crescent marking the beginning of the fast had already been sighted. In that case, it would not be said:

“If it is obscured from you.”

For this reason, the leading scholars, such as Imam Ahmad, understood his unqualified statement as referring to months in general, not to a particular month, and based religious rulings upon it.

Hanbal ibn Ishāq said:

Abū ‘Abd Allāh related to me: Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd related to us, from Humayd ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān—Abū ‘Abd Allāh said: I asked Yahyā, “Those they call al-Mulā’ī?” He replied, “Yes”—from al-Walīd ibn ‘Uqbah, who said:

“We fasted during the time of ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, for eight

---

## Page 12603

and twenty days, and ‘Alī instructed us to complete it by fasting one more day.”

Abū ‘Abd Allāh, may Allah have mercy on him, said:

“The ruling is based on this length of month, for ‘like this, like this, and like this’ amounts to twenty-nine. Whoever has fasted in this manner must make up one day, but owes no expiation.”

What we have explained also clarifies the answer to the report from ‘Ā’ishah concerning this matter. She said:

“May Allah have mercy on Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān. Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, withdrew for a month, then came down after twenty-nine days. When questioned about this, he replied, ‘A month may be twenty-nine days.’”

‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, was rejecting what others had conveyed to her from Ibn ‘Umar, or what she herself had understood: that a month can only be twenty-nine days. Ibn ‘Umar did not mean this. Rather, we have already cited authentic reports from him stating that a month is sometimes twenty-nine days and sometimes thirty. This establishes that Ibn ‘Umar reported that a month may be either length.

What he narrated either agrees with what ‘Ā’ishah also narrated—that a month may be twenty-nine days—or means that what is invariably required and obligatory for a month is twenty-nine days.

In Arabic and other languages, people negate something, whether through expressions of exclusivity or otherwise, sometimes because the thing itself is absent, and sometimes because its benefit and intended purpose are absent. Likewise, they restrict one thing to another sometimes because every instance of that category is confined to it, and sometimes because only its beneficial or complete instances are confined to it. Furthermore, they sometimes

---

## Page 12604

direct the negation at what the name denotes, and sometimes at the name itself—even though it applies linguistically—when what is truly intended by that name is absent from it but present in something else. An example is His saying:

“O People of the Scripture, you have no ground to stand on until you uphold the Torah, the Gospel, and what has been revealed to you from your Lord.”

He thus denied that what they stood upon amounted to “anything,” although the word “thing” originally encompasses everything that exists, whether true or false. This is because whatever offers no benefit ultimately amounts to falsehood, which is nothingness, and so becomes like something nonexistent. Indeed, when something has an intended purpose but fails to fulfil it, its nonexistence would be even more fitting than that of something that has never existed, since it may cause harm. Thus, whoever speaks falsehood has said nothing, and whoever does nothing beneficial has done nothing.

Another example is the Prophet’s saying, when asked about soothsayers, “They are of no account.” In the two Sahīh collections, ‘Ā’ishah reported:

“Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked about some soothsayers, and he said, ‘They are of no account.’”

Hadith scholars likewise say of certain narrators, “He is of no account,” or of certain hadiths, “It is of no account,” when they are of no use in transmission because of evident falsehood, whether deliberate or inadvertent.

It is also said of someone whose character and similar qualities depart from what humanity requires: “This is no son of Adam, no human being. He has neither humanity nor decency. He is a donkey—or a dog.” Such language is likewise used of someone endowed with qualities that are

---

## Page 12605

beyond the bounds of humanity. As the women said of Joseph:

“This is no human being; this is none other than a noble angel.”

Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The needy person is not the one who goes around begging and is sent away with a morsel or two, or a date or two. Rather, the needy person is one who lacks sufficient means, whose need goes unnoticed so that he might be given charity, and who does not beg people insistently.”

He also said:

“Whom do you consider bankrupt among you?”

They replied, “Someone who has neither a dirham nor a dinar.”

He said, “That is not the truly bankrupt person. Rather, the bankrupt person is the one who comes on the Day of Resurrection…”—and the hadith continues.

He also said:

“Whom do you consider bereft of children?”—and the hadith continues.

Here, what is denied is the true applicability of the name in light of the meaning that ought to be considered. The terms “bereft of children” and “bankrupt” are applied because of the absence of children and wealth, which causes people distress. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, therefore explained that someone who lacks these things where their absence harms him is more deserving of the name than someone who lacks them where their absence may cause him no significant harm.

An example is saying to someone experiencing slight pain, “This is no pain; real pain is such-and-such,” or to someone who thinks himself wealthy, “You are not truly wealthy; so-and-so is truly wealthy.” Similar expressions are used concerning scholars and ascetics, as when people say, “The true scholar is only the one who fears Allah Most High.”

---

## Page 12606

Another example is the saying of Mālik ibn Dīnār:

“People say, ‘Mālik is an ascetic.’ The true ascetic is ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, to whom worldly riches came, yet he renounced them.”

Similar expressions are used when people attach great importance, in their beliefs and aims, to something bearing a particular name. They desire either its presence or its absence, believing that it truly deserves that name. It is then explained to them that the reality of that meaning belongs to something else, not to it, and that their beliefs and aims ought instead to be directed toward that other thing.

An example of this is the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The Muslim is one from whose tongue and hand Muslims are safe. The emigrant is one who abandons what Allah has forbidden. The believer is one whom people trust with their lives and property. And the one who strives is one who personally strives for Allah’s sake.”

Another example is His saying, Most High:

“The believers are only those whose hearts tremble when Allah is mentioned,”

through to His words:

“Those are the believers in truth.”

These are the ones who truly deserve this name by fulfilling its obligatory requirements.

Other examples include the sayings, “There is no knowledge except that which benefits,” and “There is no city without sovereign authority.” Another is the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“There is no usury except in deferred exchange,”

or:

“Usury is only in deferred exchange.”

For usury in its general form, encompassing both exchanges between two different kinds and exchanges within a single kind whose qualities are identical, occurs only with deferment. As for usury involving unequal amounts, it occurs only within a single kind, and no one engages in it unless the qualities differ—as when exchanging coined metal for uncoined metal, or good quality for poor quality. But when the qualities are identical,

---

## Page 12607

no one sells one dirham for two. This is why lending is prescribed here, for it is an act of generosity.

Since the prevalent form of usury—the one first addressed by the Quran and commonly practised by people—is usury involving deferment, it was said:

“Usury is only in deferred exchange.”

Moreover, usury involving unequal amounts was prohibited only because it leads to usury involving deferment. Thus, the usury primarily intended by the prohibition is usury involving deferment, so there is no usury except in it. Usury is most clearly evident in exchanges within a single kind of identical quality: when someone sells a hundred dirhams for a hundred and twenty, it is clear that the additional amount is in return for the period of deferment, which provides no benefit and is accepted only out of need. For this reason, periods of deferment are not compensable on grounds of either possession or destruction. Thus, if the property remained in someone’s possession, or the sum remained a debt he owed, for a period of time, he would not be liable to compensate for that period. This differs from an enhancement in quality, which is compensable in cases of destruction and wrongful seizure, and in a sale when exchanged for something of a different kind.

This is a broad subject. A declarative statement is either an affirmation or a negation. Just as people, when affirming, apply a name to something in which the intended meaning of that name is realized—even if the outward form of what it designates is absent—so too when negating. Negating particles indicate that a name does not apply because what it designates is absent. This may be because it does not exist at all; or because the essential reality intended by the designation is absent; or because that reality is incomplete; or because what is designated is not something that ought to be sought as an end, but rather something else should be sought.

---

## Page 12608

or for other reasons. All of this becomes clear only from the context and the accompanying verbal indicators, which, according to the majority, do not make the expression any less literal, since the composite expression has come to signify that meaning by convention; or from circumstantial indicators, which, according to the majority, make it figurative. When a statement is uttered without either kind of indicator, however, it conveys unrestricted negation, which occurs frequently in speech.

The same applies to the Prophet’s statements, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The month is only twenty-nine days,”

and:

“The month is twenty-nine days,”

when intended as restrictive statements about the category. Allah Most High attached rulings to the month, as in His words:

“The month of Ramadan,”

“Hajj is during well-known months,”

and:

“two consecutive months,”

and similar passages. Some minds might immediately assume that a month, without qualification, means thirty days. Perhaps someone who has not counted the days of the months might imagine that the year consists of three hundred and sixty days and that every month has thirty days. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was saying that the fixed, indispensable duration of a month is twenty-nine days. The additional day may or may not be included.

This is like saying that Islam is the testimony that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. This is what is indispensable. Anything beyond that may become obligatory upon a person, but he may die before speaking, in which case his Islam consists only of what he has uttered.

---

## Page 12609

And in view of what is established from Ibn ‘Umar, he must have heard both reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Alternatively, what he heard from him was:

“A month may have twenty-nine days and may have thirty,”

as explicitly reported, while the wording heard from Ibn ‘Umar:

“The month is only twenty-nine days,”

was narrated according to the meaning contained in the former report. This, however, is unlikely in Ibn ‘Umar’s case, for he did not narrate reports by meaning.

All three meanings have been reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: his statement,

“The month is twenty-nine days,”

referred to a particular month; he is also reported to have said,

“It may be,”

and he is reported to have said,

“The month is only”.

Numerous reports from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, agree with the first interpretation of Ibn ‘Umar’s hadith. One example is what al-Bukhārī narrated through Ibn Jurayj, from Yahyā ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn Sayfī, from ‘Ikrima ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān, from Umm Salama:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, swore to stay away from his wives for a month. When twenty-nine days had passed, he went to them in the morning or evening. He was told, ‘You swore not to come in for a month.’ He replied, ‘A month may have twenty-nine days.’”

This indicates that a month is reckoned complete on that basis without qualification, unless the oath was taken at the beginning of the month, which is contrary to the apparent meaning. If the oath was taken during the month, then this is explicit evidence concerning the disputed issue. Al-Bukhārī also narrated through Sulaymān ibn Bilāl

---

## Page 12610

from Humayd, from Anas, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, swore to stay away from his wives. His leg had been dislocated, so he remained in an upper room for twenty-nine nights, then came down. They said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, you swore to stay away for a month.’ He replied, ‘A month may have twenty-nine days.’”

As for a particular month, al-Nasā’ī narrated through Shu‘ba, from Salama, from Abū al-Hakam, from Ibn ‘Abbās, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Gabriel came to me and said, ‘The month is complete after twenty-nine days.’”

This is how Bahz narrated it from him. He also transmitted it through Ghundar. He transmitted it through Ghundar from him with the wording:

“The month is twenty-nine days.”

This narration shows that the Prophet’s oath to stay away from his wives was taken between two new moons. When twenty-nine days had passed, Gabriel informed him that the month was complete after twenty-nine days, because the month in which he had taken the oath had twenty-nine days. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had thought that he was required to complete thirty days. Gabriel therefore informed him that the month of his oath was complete after twenty-nine days.

Had the oath been taken at the first appearance of the crescent, he would not have needed Gabriel to inform him of this. For when the crescent is sighted after twenty-nine days, it is known that the month is complete. This is plainly evident, with no uncertainty requiring Gabriel to inform him of it. Moreover, had the oath covered the interval between two new moons, the Companions would have known that this

---

## Page 12611

was a month, for neither they nor anyone else doubted that a month is the interval between two new moons and that the numerical count is the criterion. But because the oath was taken during the month, they thought that thirty days had to be completed. Gabriel therefore informed him that the month of his oath was complete after twenty-nine days. He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told his Companions:

“The month is twenty-nine days”—meaning the month of his oath—“and a month may have twenty-nine days.”

There is also the statement of ‘Ā’isha, may Allah be pleased with her: “I was counting them.” Had the oath begun at the first appearance of the crescent, she would not have needed to count them, just as the days of Ramadan were not counted when they began fasting on the basis of sighting the crescent.

Indeed, Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās narrated from him a report whose wording appears to limit the month to that length. Through the previously mentioned chain leading to Ahmad:

Muhammad ibn Bishr narrated to us: Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Khālid narrated to us, from Muhammad ibn Sa‘d ibn Abī Waqqās, from his father, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, came out to us, striking one hand against the other and saying, ‘The month is like this and like this,’ then folding down one finger on the third gesture.”

Ahmad also said: Mu‘āwiya ibn ‘Umar narrated to us: Zā’ida narrated to us, from Ismā‘īl ibn Muhammad ibn Sa‘d, from his father, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“The month is like this and like this: ten twice, and nine once.”

Al-Nasā’ī narrated it through Muhammad ibn Bishr, as we have mentioned. Both he and Ahmad also narrated it through Ibn al-Mubārak, from Ismā‘īl, with a connected chain back to the Prophet, as noted earlier.

---

## Page 12612

and Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd, Wakī‘, and Muhammad ibn ‘Ubayd narrated it from Ismā‘īl, from Muhammad, omitting the Companion from the chain.

In his narration, Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd said: “I asked Ismā‘īl, ‘From his father?’ He replied, ‘No.’”

Ahmad, however, judged the version with a connected chain authentic. Concerning Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Khālid’s narration of Sa‘d’s hadith, “The month is like this and like this,” he related that Yahyā al-Qattān said: “We wanted him to say, ‘From his father,’ but he refused.”

Ahmad said: “This was how Ismā‘īl narrated it: sometimes he gave it a connected chain, and sometimes he did not. Zā’ida narrated it with ‘from his father.’”

He was asked: “But Wakī‘ has narrated it, and Yahyā says what he says?” He replied: “Zā’ida has narrated it.”

He also said: “‘Abd Allāh narrated it with ‘from his father,’ as did Ibn Bishr, Zā’ida, and others.”

His statement makes clear that this addition comes from these trustworthy narrators and is therefore acceptable. It also shows that, when narrating to those who transmitted from him, he sometimes included it and sometimes omitted it.

A report has also been transmitted that explains it. Abū Bakr al-Khallāl and his companion narrated through Wakī‘, from Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Khālid, from Muhammad ibn Sa‘d, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The month is like this, like this, and like this; and the month is like this, like this, and like this.”

Wakī‘ held up all ten fingers twice, then tucked in one—the thumb—on the third gesture.

These widely transmitted and accepted hadiths establish several points. The first is that his statement,

“We are an unlettered nation: we neither write nor calculate,”

is a declarative statement

---

## Page 12613

that entails a prohibition. For he stated that the community which followed him—the community of the middle way—is unlettered: it neither writes nor calculates. Thus, whoever writes or calculates, or does not belong to this community with respect to this ruling, will instead have followed a path other than that of the believers who constitute this community. He will therefore have done something that is not part of its religion. Departing from it is forbidden and prohibited; hence, the writing and calculation mentioned are forbidden and prohibited.

This is like his saying:

“The Muslim is one from whose tongue and hand other Muslims are safe.”

That is, this is the Muslim’s defining characteristic. Whoever departs from it departs from Islam, and whoever departs from some aspect of it departs from Islam in that respect. The same applies to his saying:

“The believer is one whom people trust with their lives and property.”

If it is asked: Why not say that its wording is declarative but its meaning is prescriptive, as in His words:

“Divorced women shall observe a waiting period,”

and:

“Mothers shall breastfeed,”

and similar statements? The meaning would then be that whoever belongs to this community should neither write nor calculate. He prohibited him from doing so, lest this be a statement contradicted by the reality it describes, since some members of the community have written or calculated.

The reply is: This meaning is valid in itself, but it is not the apparent meaning of the wording. Its apparent meaning is declarative, and departing from the apparent meaning requires evidence that makes such a departure necessary. There is no need for that here, as we have explained.

---

## Page 12614

Moreover, his statement:

“We are an unlettered community”

is not a command, for they were unlettered before the revealed law came, as Allah Most High says:

“It is He who has sent among the unlettered a Messenger from themselves.”

And He says:

“And say to those who were given the Scripture and to the unlettered, ‘Have you submitted yourselves?’”

Since this was already their condition before the Prophet’s mission, they were not being commanded to adopt it. They might, however, be commanded to retain some of its associated rulings, for we shall explain that they were not commanded to remain in their former condition in every respect.

If it is asked: Why could this not be a purely factual statement that they do not engage in writing and calculation and are not obliged to do so, since they have another method available to them? It would then provide no evidence that writing and calculation are prohibited, but only that they are not obligatory. Being unlettered is, after all, a deficiency rather than a perfection, so someone in that condition is more deserving of being excused than praised.

The reply is: This interpretation is untenable, because the community to which Allah sent him included many who could read and write, as was the case among his Companions, and some who could calculate. He (peace and blessings be upon him) was sent with the laws of inheritance, with all the calculations they entail. It is also authentically reported from him (peace and blessings be upon him) that when his zakat collector, Ibn al-Lutbiyya, returned, he examined his accounts. He also had several scribes—such as Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, Zayd and Muʿāwiya—who wrote down the revelation, drew up agreements, and wrote his letters to people—to those to whom Allah had sent him

---

## Page 12615

among the kings of the earth and the leaders of various communities—and to his agents, governors, zakat collectors, and others.

Allah Most High says in two verses of His Book:

“So that you may know the number of years and the reckoning.”

He thus states that He did this so that calculation might be known. The word ummī (“unlettered”) is originally derived from ummah (“community”), the collective body of unlettered people. It denotes someone who is not distinguished from that body by a particular skill, such as reading or writing. Similarly, someone who belongs to the general public, without being distinguished from them by the knowledge particular to others, is called an ʿāmmī (“layperson”).
