It has also been said that the word is derived from umm (“mother”): that is, someone who remains at the level of knowledge, learning and the like to which his mother accustomed him.

The distinction by which a person rises above general unletteredness to acquire a particular skill may itself constitute excellence and perfection, as when someone is distinguished by reciting the Quran and understanding its meanings. Alternatively, it may provide a means of attaining excellence and perfection, as when someone is distinguished by writing and reading written texts. This is praiseworthy in someone who uses it to attain perfection, and blameworthy in someone who leaves it unused or employs it for evil. Someone who can dispense with it through something more beneficial to him is more accomplished and superior. For such a person, foregoing it while still achieving its intended purpose is more perfect and preferable.

Once it is clear that there are two ways of distinguishing oneself from the unlettered, “the community among whom was sent

---

## Page 12616

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was made up first and foremost of the Arabs. Through them, the call reached the other nations, for he was sent speaking their language. They were generally unlettered, possessing no distinction in learning, scripture, or anything else, although their innate dispositions were more fully prepared to receive knowledge than those of other nations. They were like arable land ready for cultivation but with no one to tend it. They had neither a scripture revealed by Allah to read, as the People of the Book did, nor sciences derived through reasoning, as the Sabians and others did. Writing was exceedingly rare among them. They possessed such knowledge as is accessible through innate disposition, which does not take a person beyond general al-umūma: knowledge of the Creator, glorified is He; esteem for noble character; and knowledge of the stars’ seasonal weather patterns, genealogy, and poetry. Thus, they merited the designation of unlettered in every respect.

As He said of them:

“It is He who sent among the unlettered a Messenger from themselves.”

And Allah Most High said:

“And say to those who were given the Scripture and to the unlettered, ‘Have you submitted yourselves?’ If they submit, they are rightly guided; but if they turn away, your duty is only to convey the message.”

He thus contrasted the unlettered with the People of the Book: someone belonging to the People of the Book is distinct from someone who is unlettered. When the Prophet was sent among them, they became obliged to follow the Book he brought, to reflect upon it, understand it, and act upon it—Allah having made it an explanation of everything, and their Prophet having taught them everything, even how to relieve themselves. They therefore became people of scripture and knowledge. Indeed, they became the most knowledgeable of all people

---

## Page 12617

and the foremost in beneficial knowledge. Their blameworthy and deficient state of being unlettered—that is, lacking knowledge and revealed scripture—fell away as they learned the Book and wisdom and were made heirs to the Book.

As He said concerning them:

“It is He who sent among the unlettered a Messenger from themselves, reciting His verses to them, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom, although before that they were clearly astray.”

They had thus been unlettered in every respect. Once He had taught them the Book and wisdom, He said concerning them:

“Then We made those whom We chose from among Our servants heirs to the Book. Among them are those who wrong themselves, those who follow a middle course, and those who, by Allah’s permission, are foremost in good deeds.”

Allah Most High also said:

“And this is a blessed Book which We have sent down, so follow it and fear Allah, that you may receive mercy.”

“Lest you say, ‘The Scripture was sent down only to two groups before us, and we were unaware of their studies.’”

“Or say, ‘Had the Scripture been sent down to us, we would have been better guided than they.’”

The supplication of Allah’s intimate friend was answered concerning them when he said:

“Our Lord, send among them a Messenger from themselves who will recite Your verses to them, teach them the Book and wisdom, and purify them. Indeed, You are the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

And He said:

“Allah certainly bestowed a great favour upon the believers when He sent among them a Messenger from themselves, reciting His verses to them, purifying them, and teaching them the Book and wisdom.”

Thus, some forms of this unlettered condition became prohibited, others disliked, and still others a deficiency and a failure to attain what is better. Anyone who does not recite al-Fātiha or does not recite any of the Quran is termed “unlettered” by the jurists in their chapters on prayer. They contrast him with one who can recite.

---

## Page 12618

and thus they say: “It is not valid for someone who can recite to follow an unlettered person in prayer, but an unlettered person may follow another unlettered person,” and discuss similar rulings. By “unlettered” here, they mean someone who cannot perform the obligatory recitation, regardless of whether he can write or do arithmetic. Some forms of this unlettered condition constitute neglect of an obligation for which a person is liable to punishment—if he is able to learn but neglects to do so.

Other forms are blameworthy, such as the condition Allah, Almighty and Majestic, described among the People of the Book when He said:

“Among them are unlettered people who know the Scripture only through mere recitation, and do nothing but conjecture.”

This describes someone who neither understands Allah’s words nor acts upon them, but confines himself to merely reciting them. As al-Hasan al-Basrī said: “The Quran was revealed to be acted upon, but they made reciting it their action.” The unlettered person here may recite the words of the Quran or other writings without understanding them. Indeed, he may speak on matters of knowledge on the basis of superficial statements and conjecture. Such a person is also blameworthily unlettered, as Allah condemned him, because he lacks obligatory knowledge, whether it is an individual or a collective obligation.

Still other forms involve [forgoing] what is better and more complete, as with someone who recites only part of the Quran, understands only what concerns him, and understands of the Sharia only what is obligatory

---

## Page 12619

for him. Such a person, too, is called unlettered; someone else who has been granted knowledge of the Quran and acts upon it is superior to him and more accomplished.

As for the distinguishing qualities that are themselves virtues and perfections, lacking them means lacking something individually obligatory, collectively obligatory, or recommended. These qualities are attributed without qualification to Allah and His prophets. For Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise, possessing knowledge, beneficial speech—both directives and statements—and will. His prophets likewise possess these qualities, and our Prophet is the foremost of the learned and the wise.

As for distinguishing skills that serve as means and avenues to virtue, but which can be dispensed with through other means, these include writing and arithmetic. If someone lacks them while his own excellence cannot be attained without them, their absence is a deficiency. If he acquires them and uses them to advance his perfection and excellence—as when someone learns to read and write, then reads the Quran and beneficial scholarly books, or writes things that benefit people—this constitutes a virtue and a perfection for him.

But if he uses these skills to acquire something harmful to himself or others—as when someone reads books of misguidance or writes things that harm people, such as forging the handwriting of rulers, judges, and witnesses—this is harmful to him, a misdeed, and a deficiency. For this reason, Umar forbade teaching women to write.

---

## Page 12620

And if these skills can be dispensed with altogether, so that a person attains complete knowledge and teaches to perfection without them, that is better and more perfect for him. Such was the state of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, of whom Allah said:

“Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, whom they find mentioned in the Torah and the Gospel that are with them.”

His being unlettered did not mean that he lacked knowledge or the ability to recite from memory, for in these matters he was the foremost of all masters. Rather, it meant that he neither wrote nor read written texts, as Allah said of him:

“You did not recite any scripture before it, nor did you write one with your right hand.”

People have differed over whether he wrote with his own hand on the day of al-Hudaybiyyah as a miracle granted to him, or whether he did not write. His inability to write, despite attaining the fullest purposes of writing while being barred from that means, was among his greatest virtues and most magnificent miracles. For Allah taught him without the intermediary of a book, as a miracle granted to him and because of the distortion and alteration that had entered books. He himself, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught his community the Book and wisdom without needing to write with his own hand.

As for the other eminent Companions, such as the four caliphs and others, most of their leading figures could write because they needed to do so, since none of them was given the revelation that he was given. Thus, his distinctive unlettered state was a perfection in his case, because he could dispense with writing through something better and more perfect. For others, however, it was a deficiency, because they lacked virtues that could only be fully attained through writing.

---

## Page 12621

Once this is clear, recording and calculating the days of the month falls into this same category, as we explained earlier. If someone records the movements of the sun and moon using abjad letters or similar notation, and calculates how far they have travelled, when they come into conjunction on the night when the moon is hidden, when they stand opposite one another on the night of the full moon, and so forth, such writing and calculation serve only to establish the times people need for dating events, scheduling activities, and similar purposes.

Other nations have done this, establishing their times through writing and calculation, using tables or letters assigned numerical values. They likewise calculate the movements of the sun and moon, making adjustments and corrections using their mean motion, until they ascertain the time of the moon’s disappearance, the full moon, and other such occurrences.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained that we, the unlettered community, do not engage in this kind of writing or perform this kind of calculation. Thus, what his words deny is the use of writing and calculation concerning the days of the month to determine when the crescent disappears and appears. We have already explained that although an unrestricted negation is general in scope, when something in the context clarifies the intended meaning, it shows whether that meaning is specific or general.

When he coupled this with his statements, “The month is thirty days” and “The month is twenty-nine days,” he made it clear that what he meant was that, regarding the crescent, we need neither writing nor

---

## Page 12622

calculation, for the month is sometimes one length and sometimes the other. The only criterion distinguishing the two is sighting the crescent; there is no other distinction established through writing or calculation, as we shall explain. Those versed in writing and calculation cannot predict sightings with consistent precision. They can only approximate them, sometimes getting them right and sometimes getting them wrong.

This shows that the unlettered character mentioned here is a praiseworthy quality and a mark of perfection in several respects:

First, writing and calculation are rendered unnecessary by something clearer and more evident: the crescent itself. Second, writing and calculation in this matter are subject to error. Third, they involve considerable effort without benefit, distracting people from beneficial pursuits, since determining the month is a means to an end, not an end in itself.

Since writing and calculation are denied of this community because something better renders them unnecessary, and because of the harm they involve, employing writing and calculation in this matter is a deficiency and a fault—indeed, a misdeed and a sin. Whoever engages in it has departed from the unlettered community in an aspect of its perfection and excellence that is free from harm, and has entered into a deficient practice that leads him to corruption and confusion.

Moreover, he made this a characteristic of the community, just as Allah described it as justly balanced in His words, Exalted is He:

“We have made you a justly balanced community.”

To depart from that is therefore to follow a path other than that of the believers.

---

## Page 12623

Moreover, when something characterizes the community because it is more beneficial than its alternatives, and because those alternatives involve harm, it must be maintained and may not be replaced by something else, for two reasons: the harm involved in the alternative, and the obligation to preserve the community’s quality of excellence. Even if an individual is not personally obliged to perform recommended acts, everything prescribed for the community as a whole becomes part of its religion. Preserving the religion in its entirety is obligatory upon the community, whether as an individual or a collective obligation. Thus, the community as a whole must preserve the entire Book and all the Sunnah teachings concerning recommended and encouraged acts, even though this is not obligatory upon each of its members. Likewise, the community is required to uphold generally recommended practices that individuals are not required to observe for themselves.

For example, someone who leads people in prayer may not habitually do everything that a person praying alone is permitted to do. Rather, he must neither prolong the prayer so much that it causes hardship to those behind him, nor fall short of its established Sunnah practices, such as reciting a surah in each of the first two units, completing the bowing and prostration, and similar matters. Indeed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed the Companions to remove an imam from leading the prayer because he had spat in the direction of the mosque’s qiblah. He also said:

“The people should be led in prayer by the one most proficient in reciting the Book of Allah. If they are equal in recitation, then by the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah. If they are equal in their knowledge of the Sunnah…”—and the hadith continues.

He also said:

“When a man leads people in prayer while someone better than him is among them, they will remain in decline.”

For this reason, the jurists state that the imam who leads the people in their Hajj must ensure

---

## Page 12624

that the Hajj is performed in its fullest form, by delaying departure from Minā until the third day rather than leaving early with the first departure, and by observing similar recommended practices of Hajj. An individual would not sin by omitting these, but the imam may not omit them, because it is in the interest of all the pilgrims that their Hajj be performed fully and completely. For this reason:

“When Eid and Friday coincided during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he attended the Eid prayer, then granted a concession regarding the Friday prayer, saying, ‘We will hold the Friday prayer.’”

According to the well-known position reported from Ahmad, he and other scholars therefore held that the imam must hold the Friday prayer for them, so that those who attend both may attain the full benefit, even though individuals are permitted to leave. There are many similar examples requiring that the completeness of the community’s religion be preserved in its collective affairs concerning times, places and acts—the religion concerning which Allah said:

“This day I have perfected for you your religion, completed My favour upon you, and approved Islam as your religion.”

Thus, whenever the completeness of the community’s religion would be diminished—even by omitting a recommended practice in a way that leads to its abandonment altogether—ensuring its observance becomes a collective obligation, whether upon the leaders or upon others. The completeness and merit attained through sighting the crescent rather than calculation would therefore be lost through reliance on calculation, even if calculation involved no harm.

The second argument is what the hadiths indicate, as in his words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Do not begin fasting until you see it, and do not end the fast until you see it.”

This is authentically reported from him in the hadith of Ibn ʿUmar. He thus prohibited fasting before sighting it, and ending the fast before

---

## Page 12625

sighting it.

The prohibition must mean one of two things: either it applies to fasting generally—whether obligatory, voluntary, in fulfilment of a vow, or to make up a missed fast—or it means, “Do not fast Ramadan until you sight it.” On either interpretation, he prohibited fasting Ramadan before sighting the crescent. Sighting means perceiving it and seeing it with the eyes. If the Muslims have not sighted it, how can it be said, “Someone has reported that it is visible”? And if it has been sighted, how can it be said, “Someone has reported that it is not visible”?

It is well known that his words,

“Do not begin fasting until you see it, and do not end the fast until you see it,”

do not mean that no one may fast until he has seen it personally. Rather, no one may fast until either he or someone else has seen it.

In short, this is a universal negation, not merely a negation of universality: no one may fast until he sees it, knows that it has been sighted, or its sighting has been established.

Thus, when the early generations and those who followed them differed over fasting the day of doubt as part of Ramadan, some fasted it as a precaution regardless of whether the skies were clear or cloudy. Others disapproved of fasting it in either case, out of aversion to adding a day to the month. Still others distinguished between clear and cloudy skies, because its absence is apparent under clear skies but not under cloudy skies. Those who fasted it as a precaution did so only because someone else might have sighted it, in which case they would reduce the subsequent count accordingly.

Had they known that no one had sighted it, however, no one in the Muslim community would have considered it permissible to fast that day on the grounds that calculations indicated that the crescent would appear, even though it had not been sighted, just as

---

## Page 12626

the majority who disapproved of fasting it likewise paid no heed to this argument, since the ruling remains in force until the crescent is actually sighted, not merely until sighting it becomes possible.

They differed over fasting that day without intending it as a Ramadan fast, when it does not coincide with one’s customary fasting practice. There are four opinions: it is permissible, disliked, prohibited, or recommended. Some permit or recommend it, understanding the prohibition to concern fasting it as part of Ramadan. Others regard it as disliked or prohibited because the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade anticipating Ramadan by fasting, because of the fear of adding to the month, and for other reasons.

If someone fasts that day without intending it as a Ramadan fast, or with the disapproved intention of fasting it as part of Ramadan, does it suffice if the day is subsequently shown to have been part of Ramadan? Or does it not suffice, so that he must make it up? There are two opinions within the Muslim community. If it only becomes known during the day that the crescent had been sighted, is forming the intention during the day sufficient? On this too, there are two opinions within the Muslim community.

If it becomes clear that the crescent was sighted elsewhere, must the fast be made up, or is making it up not required at all? Or is it required if the distance is less than that which permits shortening the prayers? Or if the sighting occurred within the same region? Or if both places fall under the same administration? Is a sighting established by the testimony of one person, or are two required in all circumstances? Or, when the sky is clear, must there be a large number of witnesses?

These are matters over which Muslims have differed. These, then, are the disputed questions concerning the thirtieth day, from which further questions have arisen because of the universal need to address this

---

## Page 12627

matter, and because of what they understood from the words of Allah and His Messenger, what they discerned in the principles of His law, and what had reached them from the earliest generation. These belong to the kinds of questions over which those qualified to exercise independent juristic judgment have differed. This is unlike the approach of those who depart from this and resort to calculation or writing, such as tables and computations of positions and corrections derived from the motions of the sun and moon, and other such methods which the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, expressly stated were not the practice of his community and prohibited.

For this reason, scholars have consistently regarded those who resort to such methods as having introduced into Islam something that does not belong to it. They therefore reject these views with the same condemnation they direct towards the people of religious innovation. Those who have introduced innovations in this matter resembling those of the People of the Book and the Sabians fall into several groups:

One group consists of people affiliated with the Shia, including the Ismā‘īlīs and others, who rely on numerical reckoning rather than sighting the crescent. This innovation first arose in Kufa. Some of them rely on a table which they claim Ja‘far al-Sādiq handed down to them, although it was brought forward only by ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mu‘āwiya. Knowledgeable Shia and non-Shia alike agree that this is a fabrication falsely attributed to Ja‘far by this ‘Abd Allāh. Reliable transmission from Ja‘far and the general body of the imams of the Prophet’s household has established their adherence to the position held by the Muslims. This is also the view of most discerning Shia.

---

## Page 12628

Some of them rely on the assumption that the first of Ramadan falls on the same weekday as the fourth of Rajab, or as the fifth of Ramadan in the preceding year.

Some of them attribute to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, a hadith unknown in any of the books of Islam and never transmitted by any scholar, claiming that he said:

“The weekday on which you begin fasting is the weekday on which you offer the sacrifice.”

Most of these people insist that Ramadan must be a full thirty days and rule out its having twenty-nine days.

Some rely on sighting the moon in the east before it disappears from view. They insist that it must remain hidden for two nights and say: “The first day, at whose beginning it is seen, belongs to the preceding month. The day in question is the day at neither end of which it is seen. Then the day at whose end it is seen is the first day of the next month.” They thus place the beginning of the month before the sighting of the crescent, despite knowing that the crescent sometimes remains hidden for one night, sometimes for two, and may remain hidden for three nights.

As for those who rely on calculating and adjusting the months, they use the preceding Ramadan or Rajab as their reference, or draw up a table on which they rely. Although they contradict his words, peace and blessings be upon him,

“We neither write nor calculate,”

their sole basis is

---

## Page 12629

averaging the motions of the sun and moon. This averaging consists in taking their highest and lowest rates of motion, finding the mean, and adding it up.

Since the usual pattern among the months of the year is for the first to have thirty days and the second twenty-nine, all these forms of calculation and tabulation assume that the first month has thirty days, the second twenty-nine, and the year 354 days. Every few years, they must record an additional day, making that year 355 days; they add it to Dhū al-Hijjah, for example. This is the basis of their reckoning.

This reckoning agrees with reality most of the time, since this is the predominant pattern of the months. It is not, however, an invariable rule: two, three, or more consecutive months may each have thirty days, and two, three, or more consecutive months may each have twenty-nine days. Their tables and calculations are thus disproved, and their religion, which is not upright, is undermined. This is one of the reasons why tabulation and calculation must not be relied upon in determining the lunar crescents.

Such is the method of these renegade innovators who have departed from the law of Islam: they calculate the month in question from the months preceding it, either in every year or in some years, and record the results.

As for the second group, they are certain jurists of Basra who held that his words:

---

## Page 12630

“Estimate it” meant calculation based on the moon’s stations. It is reported that Muhammad ibn Sīrīn said: “I went out on the day of doubt, and everyone I visited from whom knowledge was taken was eating, except one man who performed calculations and relied on them. He would have been better off not knowing how to do so.”

It has been said that this man was Mutarrif ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Shikhkhīr, a man of great standing. Yet if this is authentically reported from him, it is one of the lapses of scholars. This view has also been attributed to Abū al-ʿAbbās ibn Surayj. Some Mālikī scholars attributed to al-Shāfiʿī the view that someone whose method was to draw inferences from the stars and the moon’s stations, who had not established from the stars that the crescent would appear that night, and for whom it was obscured, could resolve to fast, form that intention during the night, and have his fast count as valid. This attribution to al-Shāfiʿī is false and has no basis. Rather, what is reliably transmitted from him is the opposite, in agreement with the scholars generally. It was Ibn Surayj—one of al-Shāfiʿī’s leading followers—who had reported its attribution to him, being the one who championed his school.

These people’s use of Ibn ʿUmar’s hadith as evidence is utterly unsound, especially since Ibn ʿUmar himself narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“We are an unlettered nation: we neither write nor calculate.”

How, then, could his hadith entail reliance on calculation? These people calculate the moon’s course during that month and its nights. None of them has any consistently reliable method whatsoever. Rather, whatever method they follow is also subject to error. Allah, glorified is He, has not made the crescent’s appearance subject to a uniform calculation; indeed, for sighting it there can be no

---

## Page 12631

consistently reliable method other than sighting itself. They have pursued various methods, as did the earlier practitioners, some of whom could determine its course only by averaging—a procedure which those who calculate agree is not consistently reliable.

It is merely an approximation, such as saying: “If it is seen on the morning of the twenty-eighth, the month is full; if it is not seen on the morning of the eighth, the month is short.” This assumes that the moon remains invisible for two nights, which is incorrect: sometimes it remains invisible for one night, and at other times for three. Their statement rests on the assumption that each night it spends exactly six-sevenths of an hour in its lunar station, neither more nor less. Thus, it sets at midnight on the seventh night; on the fourteenth night, it rises at the beginning of the night and remains up until sunrise; on the twenty-first night, it rises at midnight; and if it is invisible on the twenty-eighth night, the month is short, otherwise it is full. This is its usual course, but it may move faster or slower.

As for rational considerations, know that all the meticulous specialists in astronomical calculation agree that sighting cannot be determined by calculation in such a way as to establish, with consistent reliability, that the crescent will certainly be seen or will certainly not be seen. Rather, sighting may occur at certain times, or it may be impossible. For this reason, those devoted to this science among the various nations—the Romans, Indians, Persians, Arabs, and others, such as Ptolemy, their foremost authority, and those who followed them before and after Islam—have not attributed to him a single statement concerning sighting, nor have they defined it as they defined the conjunction of the two discs. Only some of them among the people of Islam discussed it, such as

---

## Page 12632

Kūshyār al-Daylamī. Those among them who discuss sighting rely on him and others like him. Yet their accomplished specialists, such as Abū ʿAlī al-Marwadhī al-Qattān and others, rejected his claims, saying that he sought thereby to win favour among the Muslims; otherwise, this cannot be determined precisely. Perhaps anyone among them who ventured into this was suspected of hypocrisy—for hypocrisy is hardly unlikely among such people—or sought thereby to gain favour with certain ignorant kings who think highly of astronomical calculation while professing Islam.

The reason this cannot be determined precisely is as follows:

Astronomical calculation merely estimates this by determining the positions of the sun’s and moon’s discs and their courses: that they will align at such-and-such an hour, in such-and-such a zodiacal sign in the sky, above such-and-such a place on earth, whether the conjunction occurs by night or by day. This conjunction occurs after the moon disappears and before the crescent appears. The moon travels through its twenty-eight stations, as Allah has appointed its stations, then draws near the sun and disappears for one or two nights because of its alignment with it. When it emerges from beneath it, Allah places light in it. Its light then increases as it moves farther from the sun, until it stands opposite it on the night of the full moon. Then its light diminishes as it draws nearer the sun, until it reaches conjunction with it.

This is why they speak of conjunction and opposition. They cannot say, “At the time of separation, the crescent will be in such-and-such a state.” They say, “Conjunction occurs at the time of disappearance, and opposition at the time of the full moon.”

---

## Page 12633

And calculation can determine the moon’s disappearance and fullness, corresponding to conjunction and opposition. People describe these phenomena in terms of what is visible: the crescent’s disappearance at the end of the month, its appearance at the beginning, and the fullness of its light in the middle. Astronomical calculators, however, describe them in terms of what is hidden: the conjunction of the two discs, which occurs when the moon disappears, and the opposition of the sun and moon, which occurs at the full moon. These can be determined precisely by calculation.

As for the crescent’s first appearance, they have no precise way of determining it by calculation. It cannot be determined through a known calculation as the times of solar and lunar eclipses can. According to the established order Allah has appointed for the sun, a solar eclipse occurs only when the moon is hidden, when it comes between the sun and people’s sight in a precise alignment. Likewise, a lunar eclipse occurs only on the nights of the full moon, in a precise alignment, when the earth comes between the moon and the sun. For someone whose calculations are correct, knowing when solar and lunar eclipses will occur is like anyone’s knowing that the crescent must appear on the thirty-first night of the month; uncertainty arises only on the thirtieth night.

We therefore say: the most an astronomical calculator can determine, provided his calculations are correct, is, for example, that the two discs came into conjunction at a particular hour and that, by sunset, the moon will have moved away from the sun by, say, ten degrees, or less or more. A degree is one of the three hundred and sixty divisions of the celestial sphere.

---

## Page 12634

For they divided it into twelve parts, which they called “al-dākhil,” each zodiacal sign comprising twelve degrees. This is the extent of his knowledge: your determination of the distance between the two at a particular time and place. This is what he can establish by calculation. Whether the crescent can actually be seen, however, is a matter of sensory perception and natural conditions, not mathematical calculation.

The most he can say is: “Through repeated observation, we have established that when its separation is such-and-such a number of degrees, it is certainly visible, or certainly invisible.” This is ignorance and error, for the matter does not follow a single, unvarying rule governing both visibility and invisibility. Rather, if its separation is twenty degrees, for example, it will be visible unless something obstructs the view; if it is one degree, it will not be visible. Around ten degrees, however, visibility varies with several factors.

First, visibility varies according to the sharpness or weakness of eyesight. When the crescent is very fine, someone with keen eyesight can see it, whereas someone with weak eyesight cannot. When it is of moderate size, most people can see it. People’s eyesight is not confined between two fixed limits. Nor can the matter be decided by saying that most people can see it or that most cannot: if two people see it, the Lawgiver makes the ruling dependent upon their sighting, by unanimous agreement, even if the majority have not seen it. Thus, if he declares it invisible on that basis, his judgment is mistaken under Islamic law. If, on the other hand, he declares it visible, meaning that someone with keen eyesight can see it, there may happen to be no one with keen eyesight among those looking out for it.

---

## Page 12635

so no account is taken of whether someone who is not present could see it.

The second factor:

Visibility varies with the number of observers. When they are numerous, it is more likely that one of them will see it because of his keen eyesight, his familiarity with where it rises, and his intent gaze in that direction. When they are few, this may not occur. Thus, when someone supposes that it will be visible, the observers may be few and unable to see it. Conversely, when he says that it will not be visible, there may be many observers, including someone capable of perceiving what others cannot.

The third factor:

Visibility varies according to the observation point. Someone at a higher elevation—in a minaret, on a high rooftop, or on a mountaintop—is not in the same position as someone on level ground or down in a valley. Likewise, one observer may have a building, mountain, or similar object in front of him that usually still allows him to see it, though it sometimes obstructs his view; another may have nothing in front of him.

Thus, if it is said without qualification that the crescent will be visible, someone at a lower elevation or in a similar position may not see it. If it is said that it will not be visible, someone at a higher elevation or in a similar position may see it. These circumstances plainly affect visibility.

The fourth factor:

Visibility varies according to the time of observation. Those who perform the calculations customarily report its separation from the sun at sunset, and at that

---

## Page 12636

time, it is close to the sun, so its light is faint, and the redness of the sun’s rays partly impedes its visibility. As it descends towards the horizon, it moves farther from the sun, strengthening the condition that favours visibility, while the impediment remains. Its light thus increases, and it moves farther from the sun’s rays. If someone supposes that it cannot be seen at sunset or immediately afterwards, it may nevertheless be seen later, even as it descends in the west.

If he claims that its state can be precisely determined from the time the sun sets until the crescent itself sets, he can only determine the number of those degrees, since it remains above the horizon by the measure of the distance between them. As for the amount of light it gains and the extent to which the rays impeding its visibility diminish—for these are what make sighting possible—he can never determine these according to a single fixed rule under which sighting is always possible or always impossible. Nor is this, in itself, something subject to precise determination, especially when the sun is … (1).

The fifth factor:

The clarity or haziness of the atmosphere. I do not mean the presence of something that prevents sighting, such as clouds or the haze rising from smoke and vapours. Rather, I mean that, when atmospheric conditions permit it to be seen, some conditions make sighting easier than others: when the atmosphere is entirely clear of haze, as it is in winter after rainfall in open country where there is no vapour, unlike when the atmosphere contains vapour such that it is not possible

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 12637

to see it, as happens in summer because of vapours and smoke; under such conditions it cannot be seen as it can in clear air. As for facing it correctly, knowing where it appears, and similar matters, these are things that an observer can learn or endeavour to determine.

It may therefore be said that these are prerequisites for sighting, like gazing intently westward behind the sun. We have thus not included them among the causes of differences in sighting. We have mentioned only those factors that observers cannot fully encompass: the quality of their eyesight, their numbers, the place and time of observation, and the clarity or haziness of the atmosphere. If visibility is an outcome jointly determined by these factors, none of which enters into the calculator’s calculations, how can he nevertheless make a universal claim that no one can possibly see the crescent when he finds it at seven, eight, or nine degrees? Or how can he state categorically that it will be seen when it is at nine or ten degrees, for example?

This is why you find them disagreeing about the arc of visibility: how high must it be? Some say nine and a half, while others say … (1). They also need to distinguish between summer

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 12638

and winter: when the sun is high in the northern signs of the zodiac or low in the southern signs. This explanation makes clear that their predictions of visibility are of the same kind as their astrological predictions, and weaker still. For suppose it has been established that celestial motions cause terrestrial events.

A Muslim cannot categorically deny this much, since Allah, glorified is He, has made some created things—their very entities, attributes, and movements—causes affecting others. Neither revelation nor reason rules this out. Muslims, however, fall into two groups on this matter.

Some say that there is no evidence establishing this, so it is not permissible to assert it, for that would be speaking without knowledge.

Others say that, on the contrary, it is established in general terms, because some instances of it have become known through experience, and because the revealed law indicates this through the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The sun and the moon do not eclipse because of anyone’s death or birth. Rather, they are two of Allah’s signs through which He instils fear in His servants.”

Fear is only aroused when there is a cause for fear. Thus, it is known that their eclipses may cause something fearful. His words,

“They do not eclipse because of anyone’s death or birth,”

refute what some people had imagined. The sun was eclipsed on the day of Ibrāhīm’s death, and some people believed that it had been eclipsed because of his death, in honour of his passing, and that his death had caused its eclipse. The Prophet therefore explained

---

## Page 12639

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—that the sun does not eclipse because someone has died or been born. Similarly, the two Sahīh collections report from Ibn Abbās, who said:

“Men of the Ansār told me that they were with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, when a shooting star was hurled and shone brightly. He asked, ‘What would you say about this in pre-Islamic times?’ They replied, ‘We would say: A great man has been born tonight, or a great man has died.’ He said, ‘They are not hurled because anyone has died or been born. Rather, when Allah decrees a matter, the bearers of the Throne glorify Him.’”

The hadith continues. Thus, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained that the hurling of meteors does not result from an event on earth, but from something that has occurred in heaven, and that they are hurled to drive away the devils who eavesdrop. Likewise, the sun and the moon are two of Allah’s signs through which He instils fear in His servants. As Allah says:

“We send the signs only as a warning.”

It is therefore known that these heavenly signs may be a cause of punishment. For this reason, whenever a cause for fear arose, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed righteous deeds to avert it. He commanded the eclipse prayer—a lengthy prayer—and commanded the freeing of slaves, charity, supplication, and seeking forgiveness. As he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Indeed, affliction and supplication

---

## Page 12640

meet and contend between heaven and earth.” Thus, supplication and similar deeds repel affliction descending from heaven.

If you say:

Among ordinary people—even those associated with scholarship—are some who categorically assert that celestial movements do not cause anything whatsoever to occur. They may even believe that admitting this possibility or affirming it constitutes the forbidden astrology concerning which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever acquires a branch of astrology has acquired a branch of sorcery; the more he learns of the former, the more he acquires of the latter.”

This was reported by Abū Dāwūd and others. Some may also appeal to their understanding of his words:

“They do not eclipse because of anyone’s death or birth.”

They believe that the cause meant here is a final cause—that is, that they do not eclipse in order for death or life to result?

I reply:

This assertion is ignorance, because it is a statement made without knowledge. Allah has forbidden a person to deny what he has no knowledge of, and has forbidden him to say about Allah what he does not know. He has also declared that it is Satan who commands people to speak without knowledge. He says:

“Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge.”

And He says:

“He only commands you to evil and indecency, and to say about Allah what you do not know.”

And He says:

“Say: My Lord has only forbidden shameful deeds, whether open or secret, sin, unjust aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.”

For this is not stated in the Book of Allah or the Sunnah of His Messenger, nor has any scholar asserted it

---

## Page 12641

nor does reason, or anything known through reason, provide grounds for denying it.

Such a groundless categorical denial is merely like the denial by some ignorant people that the celestial spheres are round. Some categorically deny this, while others deny that anyone can be certain of it. Both positions are ignorance. What basis does a person have for denying this, or for denying that anyone in creation knows it, when he has no evidence beyond what he may grasp through his own deficient understanding?

Yet the Book, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the scholars of the Muslim community establish that the celestial spheres are round. Allah Most High says:

“Among His signs are the night and the day, and the sun and the moon.”

He also says:

“It is He who created the night and the day, and the sun and the moon, each floating in an orbit.”

And He Most High says:

“It is not for the sun to overtake the moon, nor does the night outstrip the day; each floats in an orbit.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said: “In a circular form, like the whorl of a spindle.” Such is also its meaning in the Arabic language: a falak is something round. Hence the expression tafallaka thadyu al-jāriyah, meaning that a young woman’s breast has become round.

He Most High says:

“He wraps the night around the day and wraps the day around the night.”

Tak wīr means making something round. Hence the expressions kāra al-‘imāmah and kawwarahā, meaning that someone wound a turban around. From this, too, a ball is called a kurah: it is a round body. This is why the celestial spheres are described as spherical in shape. The original form of kurah is kawarah: the wāw bore a vowel and was preceded by an a-vowel, so it was changed into an alif. Likewise, kawwartu al-kārah means “I made the bundle round.” Related to this is the hadith:

“Indeed, the sun and the moon will be rolled up on the Day of Resurrection

---

## Page 12642

as though they were two bulls in the fire of Hell.” Allah Most High says:

“The sun and the moon move by precise reckoning,”

like the pivot of a millstone. He also says:

“You will see no disparity in the creation of the Most Merciful.”

Among bodily shapes, this holds only for rounded forms, not polygons, whether triangular, square, or otherwise. A polygon has disparities, since its corners differ from its straight sides, whereas a rounded body is uniform on every side and in every direction, with no part differing from another.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed the Bedouin who said, “We seek your intercession with Allah, and we seek Allah’s intercession with you.” He replied:

“Woe to you! Allah is not to be sought as an intercessor with any of His creation. His majesty is far greater than that. His Throne is above His heavens like this”—and he made a dome-like gesture with his hand—“and it creaks beneath Him as a new saddle creaks beneath its rider.”

Abū Dāwūd and others narrated this from Jubayr ibn Mut‘im, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayrah reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When you ask Allah for Paradise, ask Him for al-Firdaws, for it is the highest and most central part of Paradise, and its roof is the Throne of the Most Merciful.”

He thus stated that al-Firdaws is both the highest and the most central part. This is possible only in a rounded form. In a square or a similar shape, its middle is not its highest point; rather, it is all level.

As for the consensus of the scholars, Iyās ibn Mu‘āwiyah—the renowned imam and judge of

---

## Page 12643

Basra, one of the Successors—said: “The sky is like a dome over the earth.”

Imam Abū al-Husayn Ahmad ibn Ja‘far ibn al-Munādī, an eminent scholar renowned for his knowledge of transmitted reports and his major writings in the various religious sciences, and belonging to the second generation of Ahmad’s followers, said:

“There is no disagreement among the scholars that the sky is spherical and revolves, together with all the celestial bodies within it, like a sphere turning upon two fixed, motionless poles: one to the north and the other to the south.”

He said:

“This is indicated by the fact that all the celestial bodies revolve from the east, shifting little by little in a uniform order in their movements and the measures of their segments, until they reach the middle of the sky. They then descend in that same order, as though fixed within a sphere that turns them all together in a single revolution.”

He said:

“They likewise unanimously agreed that the earth, with all its movements, on land and at sea, is like a sphere.”

He said:

“This is indicated by the fact that the sun, moon, and celestial bodies do not rise and set at the same time for everyone throughout the earth. Rather, they do so in the east before the west.”

He said:

“The terrestrial sphere is fixed at the centre of the celestial sphere, like a point within a circle. This is indicated by the fact that each celestial body appears the same size in every part of the sky. This therefore indicates that the distance between heaven and earth in all

---

## Page 12644

directions is the same; the earth must therefore be at the centre of the sky.”

Some people may think that the prophetic reports—which state that the Throne is the roof of Paradise and that Allah is upon His Throne, while also indicating that the celestial spheres are spherical—are contradictory, or imply that Allah is beneath some of His creation. Some of the Jahmiyyah have similarly argued against Allah’s being above the Throne on the grounds that the celestial spheres are spherical, claiming that this necessarily places the Lord below. This stems from their mistaken conception of the matter.

Anyone who knows that the celestial spheres are spherical, that the encompassing sphere which forms the roof is the highest of the high, and that the centre within it, at the earth’s deepest point, is “Sijjīn” and “the lowest of the low,” will understand this from Allah’s contrast between the highest of the high and Sijjīn. The evident contrast is between height and depth, or between spaciousness and constriction, because height entails spaciousness, while constriction entails lowness. He will also know that the sky is above the earth in an absolute sense: it can never conceivably be beneath it, even though it is spherical and encompasses it. Likewise, the higher something is, the more elevated and encompassing it is.

He will also know that directions fall into two categories:

An intrinsic category, consisting solely of above and below.

A relative category, defined in relation to a living creature according to its movement: thus, whatever is in front of it

---

## Page 12645

is called “in front”; whatever is behind it is called “behind”; whatever is to its right is called “right”; whatever is to its left is called “left”; whatever is above its head is called “above”; and whatever is beneath its feet is called “below.” These are relative designations. Consider: if a man were suspended with his feet towards the sky and his head towards the earth, would the sky not still be above him, even though his feet faced it? Likewise, if an ant or another creature walked beneath a ceiling with its feet towards it and its back towards the earth, the upward direction would lie towards its feet, although still above it. The lowest of the low ultimately lies in the depths of the earth.

As for the stars in the sky, although some are aligned with our heads and others are in the other half of the celestial sphere, none of them is beneath anything; rather, they are all above us in the sky. Now, when a person envisages this, relative lowness immediately comes to mind. This is the argument advanced by the Jahmī who denied Allah’s being above His Throne and misled those unfamiliar with the matter into imagining that anyone who says, “Allah is above the Throne,” has thereby placed Him beneath half of creation or made Him another celestial sphere. Exalted is Allah above what the ignorant person says!

If anyone supposes that the position of the people of Islam entails things unbefitting Allah, these are not, in fact, necessary implications. Rather, this is corroborated by the hadith narrated by Ahmad in his Musnad, through al-Hasan from Abū Hurayrah, and also narrated by al-Tirmidhī in the hadith concerning lowering;

---

## Page 12646

for the hadith indicates that Allah is above the Throne, while the fact that the Throne is the roof of creation indicates that it encompasses creation.

Anyone who interprets it as meaning, “It descended upon Allah’s knowledge,” as al-Tirmidhī did, has not understood the actual state of affairs. However, since he belonged to Ahl al-Sunnah and knew that Allah is above the Throne, but did not understand the configuration of created things, he feared that a Jahmī might interpret it to mean that Allah is intermingled with creation, and so he explained it in this way. Otherwise, everything the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said is true, with its parts confirming one another. Sound knowledge established through reason likewise confirms and bears witness to what the Messenger brought.

We therefore say: once it becomes clear that we know what has been established concerning the roundness of the celestial spheres, it is evident that anyone who denies this contradicts all the evidence. However, someone who suspends judgment before the matter is clarified has done what is required. The same applies to someone whose information on the matter has always come from a source he does not trust. For the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When the People of the Book relate something to you, neither affirm nor deny what they say.”

That some celestial motions cause certain events is not to be denied; rather, this should either be accepted or left unchallenged. Nevertheless, astrological prognostication is false on rational grounds and forbidden by religious law. This is because the motion of a celestial sphere, even if it has an effect, does not act independently; rather, the influence of spirits and others

---

## Page 12647

among the angels is greater than its influence. The same is true of the influence of natural bodies on earth. Likewise, the influence of human hearts through supplication and other means is among the greatest influences, as Muslims agree, as do the Sabians who practise astrology and people of other nations. Thus, in the general course of events, it is only one component of the cause.

Even if we suppose that it is an independent cause, or that it necessarily entails the complete cause, knowledge of it is impossible because of the rapidity of its motion. Even supposing that it could be known, the location of its influence cannot be precisely determined, for there is no reason why a solar eclipse should affect one region rather than another. And even if we suppose that it is an independent cause, that all its conditions have been fulfilled, and that it is known, there is no doubt that even small righteous deeds—prayer, zakah, fasting, pilgrimage, maintaining family ties, and other acts prescribed by religious law—counteract what that cause would otherwise bring about.

For this reason, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, commanded us to pray, supplicate, seek forgiveness, free slaves, and give charity when an eclipse occurs. He also informed us that supplication and affliction meet and struggle with one another between heaven and earth. Astrologers acknowledge this, to the point that their leading authority, Ptolemy, said:

“The clamour of voices in temples of worship, offering manifold supplications in every tongue, unravels what the revolving celestial spheres have bound.”

Thus, if a cause of good arises, the acts prescribed by religious law—prayer and zakah—strengthen and support it; and if a cause of evil arises, such deeds repel it. The same applies to a servant’s seeking his Lord’s guidance when considering a matter, as

---

## Page 12648

the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, commanded when he said:

“When any one of you intends to undertake something, let him pray two units of prayer”—to the end of the hadith.

Seeking guidance from Allah—the All-Knowing, the All-Powerful, the Creator of causes and their effects—is better than consulting the ascendant regarding what one intends to do. For such a choice, if valid, can at best secure one of the causes of success. Seeking Allah’s guidance, however, pursues success through all its avenues, for Allah knows what is best. He may therefore put a person’s heart at ease and facilitate the means, or make them difficult and turn him away from that undertaking.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, also said:

“Whoever visits a fortune-teller and questions him”—to the end of the hadith.

Muslim narrated it through Safiyya bint Abī ʿUbayd, from one of the wives of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. The term “fortune-teller” includes the astrologer and others, either in its wording or in its meaning. He, peace and blessings be upon him, also said:

“Whoever acquires a branch of astrology has acquired a branch of magic; the more he acquires of the former, the more he acquires of the latter.”

Abū Dāwūd and Ibn Mājah narrated it. Thus, the prohibition of relying on astrological judgments, whether in theory or in practice, has become clear from the perspective of religious law. We have also explained, on rational grounds, that this is generally unattainable: the causes of events, their conditions, and their impediments cannot be determined by precisely tracking the movements of certain things. A prediction happens to be correct only when the remaining causes are present and the impediments are absent—not because it rests on an indication that holds consistently, either invariably or in most cases. Expert astrologers agree with this, and they know that the ascendant of a country does not

---

## Page 12649

generally provide a sound basis for judgment, because it is counteracted by the ascendant of the time and other impediments. They say that astrological judgments rest on intuition and conjecture.

We therefore explain to them that their claims concerning the sighting of the crescent and their astrological judgments belong to the same category. Rational evidence establishes that these matters cannot be determined precisely, while scriptural evidence establishes that such practices are forbidden. The Book and wisdom with which Allah sent Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, make us independent of whatever benefit we suppose these practices might offer.

This is why it has been said that their discourse consists either of true knowledge that brings no benefit—and we seek refuge in Allah from knowledge that brings no benefit—or of false conjectures that cannot be trusted; and some conjecture is sinful. Whoever said this spoke the truth. When someone engaged in calculation wears himself out calculating minutes and seconds, the most he achieves is something of no benefit. They undertake this toil only for the sake of astrological judgments, which are false conjectures.

As for discourse concerning matters of religious law, if it yields certain knowledge, it brings benefit in this world and the Hereafter. If it yields a probable judgment—such as ruling on the testimony of two witnesses or acting upon preponderant probabilistic evidence—then this is action based on knowledge. It is a probable judgment for which one is rewarded in this world and the Hereafter.

“Praise be to Allah, who has guided us to this. We would never have been guided had Allah not guided us. The messengers of our Lord certainly came with the truth.”

This is the end of what was found. May Allah bless Muhammad and his family and grant them peace.

---

## Page 12650

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Some inhabitants of a city sighted the crescent of Dhū al-Hijjah, but the sighting was not established before the city’s judge. May they fast on the day outwardly reckoned as the ninth, even if it is actually the tenth?

He replied:

Yes, they should fast on the day outwardly recognized by the community as the ninth, even if it is in fact the tenth, and even supposing that sighting were established.

In the Sunan collections, Abū Hurayrah reports that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Your fasting is on the day you all fast, your breaking of the fast is on the day you all break the fast, and your sacrifice is on the day you all offer sacrifices.”

It was recorded by Abū Dāwūd, Ibn Mājah and al-Tirmidhī, who graded it authentic. ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—also reported that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The day of breaking the fast is the day people break their fast, and the day of sacrifice is the day people offer their sacrifices.”

This was narrated by al-Tirmidhī, and this is the practice followed by all the imams of the Muslims. For if people mistakenly stood at ‘Arafah on the tenth day, their standing there would be valid

---

## Page 12651

by unanimous agreement, and that day would count as the Day of ‘Arafah for them. If they mistakenly stood there on the eighth, there is disagreement over whether their standing would be valid. The stronger view is that it would also be valid. This is one of the two views in the schools of Mālik, Ahmad and others. ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—said:

“‘Arafah is only the day that people recognize as such.”

The underlying principle is that Allah, glorified and exalted be He, has made the ruling dependent on the crescent and the month. He, exalted be He, says:

“They ask you about the crescent moons. Say, ‘They are measurements of time for the people and for Hajj.’”

The term hilāl (crescent) refers to that whose appearance is publicly proclaimed and announced. If it appears in the sky but people neither know of it nor proclaim it, it is not a hilāl. Similarly, shahr (month) is derived from shuhrah (being widely known). If it has not become widely known among people, the month has not begun.

Many people err in such matters because they suppose that, whenever the crescent appears in the sky, that night marks the beginning of the month, whether or not people become aware of it and announce its appearance. That is not so: people must become aware of it and announce its appearance. This is why the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Your fasting is on the day you all fast, your breaking of the fast is on the day you all break the fast, and your sacrifice is on the day you all offer sacrifices.”

That is, it is the day you recognize as the time for fasting, breaking the fast or offering sacrifices. If you do not recognize it, no ruling follows from it.

Fasting on a day about which there is doubt as to whether it is the ninth or the tenth of Dhū al-Hijjah is permissible, without any disagreement among the scholars;

---

## Page 12652

because the presumption is that the tenth has not yet begun. Likewise, if, on the night preceding the thirtieth of Ramadan, they are unsure whether the crescent has appeared, they fast that doubtful day, by agreement of the imams. The “day of doubt” concerning which disapproval has been reported refers only to doubt about the beginning of Ramadan, because the presumption is that Shaʿbān continues.

Only two questions give rise to uncertainty in this matter:

The first: If someone alone sees the crescent of Shawwāl, or a group whom he knows to be truthful informs him of its sighting, should he break his fast or not?

The second: If someone sees the crescent of Dhū al-Hijjah, or a group whom he knows to be truthful informs him of its sighting, are the Day of ʿArafah and the Day of Sacrifice, for him, the ninth and tenth as determined by this sighting, which has not become publicly known? Or are they the ninth and tenth as publicly recognized?

As for the first question, the scholars agree that someone who alone sees the crescent of Shawwāl must not break his fast publicly, unless he has an excuse permitting him to do so, such as illness or travel. As to whether he should break his fast privately, the scholars hold two views. The stronger is that he should not do so. This is the well-known position in the schools of Mālik and Ahmad. There is also a view within both schools that he should break his fast privately, as is the well-known position in the school of Abū Hanīfah

---

## Page 12653

and al-Shāfiʿī. It is reported that, during the time of ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—two men saw the crescent of Shawwāl. One broke his fast, while the other did not. When ʿUmar heard of this, he said to the man who had broken his fast, “Were it not for your companion, I would have given you a painful beating.”

The reason is that the day of breaking the fast is the day when the people break their fast: that is the day of Eid. The day fasted by someone who alone saw the crescent is not the day of Eid on which the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prohibited fasting. He prohibited fasting on the Day of Breaking the Fast and the Day of Sacrifice, saying:

“One is the day when you end your fasting, and the other is the day when you eat from your sacrificial animals.”

Thus, the days on which he prohibited fasting are those on which the Muslims break their fast and offer their sacrifices.

This becomes clear from the second question. If someone alone saw the crescent of Dhū al-Hijjah, he would not be permitted to stand at ʿArafah before the people, on what is publicly reckoned as the eighth, even if it is the ninth according to his own sighting. This is because standing at ʿArafah and slaughtering the sacrifice alone entail the same departure from the community as publicly breaking the fast on his own.

As for fasting on the ninth in the case of someone who saw the crescent, or was informed by two trustworthy men that they had seen it—when it is the tenth according to that reckoning, although this has not been established among the public, and is the tenth according to the private sighting—the ruling on this follows from the preceding discussion.

---

## Page 12654

Thus, whoever instructs him to fast on the thirtieth day—which, according to the private sighting, falls in Shawwāl—and does not instruct him to break his fast privately, permits and recommends fasting on the day in question. For this is the Day of ʿArafah, just as that other day belongs to Ramadan. This is the correct view, supported by the Sunnah and sound reasoning. Whoever instructs him to break his fast privately on account of his sighting would, according to that view, forbid him to fast on this day, just as in the case of the crescent of Shawwāl that he alone sighted.

If it is said, “The authority entrusted with establishing the crescent sighting may be negligent in rejecting the testimony of upright witnesses: he may have failed to investigate their uprightness, rejected their testimony because of enmity between him and them or for other reasons not sanctioned by Islamic law, or relied on the statement of an astronomer who claimed that it would not be visible.”

The answer is:

The applicable ruling does not differ according to whether the person followed in establishing the crescent sighting exercises his judgment correctly, errs, or is negligent. For if the crescent has not appeared and become widely known, such that people can seek to ascertain it—

It is authentically established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said concerning prayer leaders:

“They lead you in prayer. If they do so correctly, you and they will be rewarded; if they err, you will be rewarded, while they will bear the blame.”

Thus, his error and negligence are his responsibility, not that of the Muslims who have neither been negligent nor erred.

---

## Page 12655

There is no doubt that the authentic Sunnah and the agreement of the Companions establish that it is impermissible to rely on astronomical calculations. As authentically reported from the Prophet in the two Sahīh collections, he said:

“We are an unlettered nation: we neither write nor calculate. Begin fasting upon sighting the crescent, and end fasting upon sighting it.”

Whoever relies on calculations to establish the crescent is not only astray in matters of Islamic law and an innovator in religion, but also mistaken in his reasoning and understanding of calculation. Astronomers know that visibility cannot be precisely determined by calculation. At most, their calculations, when accurate, can establish how many degrees separate the crescent from the sun at sunset, for example. Visibility, however, is not governed by fixed numbers of degrees. It varies according to the sharpness or weakness of eyesight, the elevation of the place from which the crescent is sought, and the clarity or murkiness of the atmosphere. One person may see it at a separation of eight degrees, while another may fail to see it at twelve degrees.

For this reason, those versed in calculation have disagreed widely and inconsistently over the arc of visibility. Their leading authorities, such as Ptolemy, did not say a single word on the subject, because it cannot be established by mathematical proof. Only some of their later scholars, such as Kūshyār al-Daylamī and others like him, discussed it. When they saw that Islamic law made its rulings contingent on the crescent, they regarded calculation as a method by which to determine precisely

---

## Page 12656

the crescent’s visibility. Yet this method is neither sound nor consistent; rather, it frequently produces errors, as experience has shown. They disagree greatly over whether the crescent will be visible or not.

The reason is that they have sought to determine by calculation what cannot be known through calculation, and have thus missed the correct approach. I have discussed this at length elsewhere and explained that what authentic revelation teaches accords with sound reason. I have also discussed what marks the beginning of day, explaining that it cannot be precisely determined by calculation, because daylight appears through rising vapours. Anyone seeking to derive the duration of evening twilight from that of morning twilight would be correct only if the appearance and disappearance of light depended solely on the sun’s position relative to the horizon, which can be calculated. But if vapours affect this—and there is more vapour in winter and over damp ground than in summer and over dry ground—and this cannot be precisely determined by calculation, then the method of mathematical inference is invalid.

This is why morning twilight lasts longer in winter than in summer. This puzzles anyone relying solely on mathematical inference, since, in his view, the duration of morning twilight varies with the length of the day. This too has been discussed at length in its proper place. Allah, glorified be He, knows best. May Allah bless Muhammad.

---

## Page 12657

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A traveler who fasts during Ramadan is reproached, accused of ignorance, and told that not fasting is better. What travel distance permits shortening the prayers? If someone sets out on a journey during the day, may he break his fast? May travelers such as those who hire out mounts, merchants, camel drivers, sailors, and passengers at sea refrain from fasting? What is the difference between journeys undertaken in obedience to Allah and those undertaken for a sinful purpose?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Muslims agree that a traveler may refrain from fasting, whether traveling for Hajj, jihad, trade, or any similar purpose that Allah and His Messenger do not disapprove of. As for journeys undertaken for a sinful purpose, such as traveling to commit highway robbery and the like, there are two well-known scholarly opinions, just as there are concerning shortening the prayers on such journeys. As for a journey on which prayers may be shortened, it is permissible to refrain from fasting and make up the missed days

---

## Page 12658

by agreement of the imams. The Muslim community unanimously agrees that a traveler may refrain from fasting, whether he is able to fast or not, and whether fasting causes him hardship or not. Even if he travels with shade and water available and someone to serve him, he may refrain from fasting and shorten his prayers.

Anyone who says that refraining from fasting is permissible only for someone unable to fast must be called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is to be put to death. Likewise, anyone who censures a traveler for not fasting must be called upon to repent of that.

Anyone who says that a traveler who does not fast incurs sin must also be called upon to repent of that, for such positions contradict the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and the consensus of the Muslim community.

Likewise, the Sunnah for a traveler is to offer four-unit prayers as two units. Shortening them is better for him than performing all four, according to the four imams: Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad, and al-Shāfi‘ī according to the sounder of his two opinions.

The Muslim community has not disputed the permissibility of a traveler’s refraining from fasting. Rather, they have disputed whether a traveler may fast. A group of earlier and later scholars held that one who fasts on

---

## Page 12659

a journey is like one who does not fast while resident, and that if he fasts, his fast does not discharge his obligation; rather, he must make it up. This view is reported from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf, Abū Hurayrah, and other early Muslims, and it is the position of the Zāhirī school.

The two Sahīh collections report that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“It is not righteousness to fast while traveling.”

However, the position of the four imams is that a traveler may either fast or refrain from fasting. As reported in the two Sahīh collections, Anas said:

“We used to travel with the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—during Ramadan. Some of us fasted and others did not. Those who fasted did not criticize those who did not, nor did those who did not fast criticize those who did.”

Allah Most High says:

“And whoever is ill or on a journey must make up an equal number of days later. Allah intends ease for you and does not intend hardship for you.”

The Musnad reports that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Allah loves His concessions to be taken, just as He dislikes being disobeyed.”

The Sahīh reports that a man said to the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—“I am a man who fasts frequently. Should I fast while traveling?” He replied, “If you refrain from fasting, that is good; and if you fast, there is no harm.”

Another hadith states:

“The best among you are those who shorten their prayers and refrain from fasting while traveling.”

As for the length of the journey that permits shortening the prayers and refraining from fasting, the position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad is that it is two days’ travel at a moderate pace by camel or on foot. This amounts to sixteen parasangs, such as the distance between Mecca and ‘Usfān, or between Mecca and Jeddah.

Abū Hanīfah said: A journey of

---

## Page 12660

three days.

A group of earlier and later scholars held that one may shorten the prayers and refrain from fasting on a journey of less than two days. This is a strong view, for it is established that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—shortened his prayers at ‘Arafah, Muzdalifah, and Minā, while the people of Mecca and others prayed behind him. He did not instruct any of them to perform the prayers in full.

If someone sets out on a journey during the day, may he break his fast? There are two well-known scholarly opinions on this, both reported from Ahmad. The better-supported view is that he may do so. It is established in the Sunan that some Companions would break their fast when they set out during the day, stating that this was the Sunnah of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. It is also established in the Sahīh:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—intended to fast while traveling, but then called for water and broke his fast as the people watched him.”

As for the second day, he may undoubtedly refrain from fasting, even if his journey lasts only two days, according to the majority of the imams and the Muslim community.

If a traveler returns during the day, there is a well-known disagreement among scholars over whether he must abstain for the remainder of that day. However, he must make up that day whether he abstains or not.

---

## Page 12661

Those who travel habitually may also refrain from fasting, provided they have a town to which they return. This includes merchants who bring in food and other goods; those who hire out their animals to such merchants and others; couriers who travel on the Muslims’ business; and others like them. The same applies to a sailor who has a home on land. However, someone whose wife and all his necessities are aboard his ship, and who is constantly traveling, may neither shorten his prayers nor refrain from fasting.

As for nomadic peoples—such as the nomads among the Arabs, Kurds, Turks, and others who spend the winter in one place and the summer in another—they may shorten their prayers while traveling from their winter quarters to their summer quarters, or from their summer quarters to their winter quarters. Once they settle in their winter or summer quarters, however, they may neither refrain from fasting nor shorten their prayers, even if they move about in search of pasture. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Which is better for someone traveling during Ramadan who experiences neither hunger, thirst, nor fatigue: to fast or to refrain from fasting?”

---

## Page 12662

He replied:

A traveler may refrain from fasting by agreement of the Muslims, even if he experiences no hardship, and refraining from fasting is better for him. If he fasts, however, this is permissible according to most scholars. Some hold that his fast does not discharge his obligation.

He was asked:

“An imam of a mosque congregation who follows the Hanafī school told his congregation that he has a book stating that a person receives no reward for fasting in Ramadan unless he forms the intention to fast before or after the ‘Ishā’ prayer, or at the time of the predawn meal. Is this correct or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Every Muslim who believes that fasting is obligatory for him and wishes to fast the month of Ramadan must have the intention to do so. If he knows that tomorrow is part of Ramadan, he necessarily intends to fast, for the intention resides in the heart, and everyone who knows what he wishes to do necessarily intends it. Expressing the intention verbally is not obligatory, by agreement of the Muslims. Muslims generally fast with this intention, and their fasting is valid without any disagreement among the scholars. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12663

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

“What does our master say about someone fasting in Ramadan: does he need an intention each day or not?”

He replied:
