Anyone who knows that tomorrow is part of Ramadan and wishes to fast that day has already intended to fast, whether or not he expresses that intention verbally. This is the practice of Muslims generally: they all intend to fast.

He was asked about sunset:

“May a fasting person break his fast as soon as the sun sets?”

He replied:

Once the entire disk of the sun has disappeared, the fasting person may break his fast. The intense redness remaining on the horizon has no bearing on this.

---

## Page 12664

And when the entire disk has disappeared, darkness appears in the east, as the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“When night approaches from this direction, day retreats from that direction, and the sun has set, the time has come for the fasting person to break his fast.”

He was asked:

“What if someone eats after the dawn call to prayer during Ramadan?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the caller gives the call to prayer before dawn—as Bilāl used to do during the Prophet’s lifetime (peace and blessings be upon him), and as callers in Damascus and elsewhere do—there is no harm in eating and drinking for a short time afterward.

If someone is unsure whether dawn has broken, he may eat and drink until it becomes clear that it has. If he later learns that he ate after dawn had broken, there is disagreement over whether he must make up the fast. The stronger view is that he need not make it up. This is the position reliably reported from ‘Umar and adopted by a group

---

## Page 12665

among the early and later generations. The well-known position in the schools of the four jurists, however, is that the fast must be made up. Allah knows best.

He was asked about a man who, whenever he intends to fast, loses consciousness, foams at the mouth, and thrashes about. He remains unconscious for days, to the point that insanity is suspected, though this has not been established.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If fasting causes him this kind of illness, he should refrain from fasting and make up the missed fasts. If this happens whenever he fasts, regardless of the time, then he is unable to fast and should feed one poor person for each day. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a pregnant woman who noticed something resembling menstrual bleeding, and whose bleeding persists. The midwives said that she should refrain from fasting for the benefit of the fetus, although she was not in any pain. Is she

---

## Page 12666

permitted to refrain from fasting, or not?

He replied:

If a pregnant woman fears for her unborn child, she should refrain from fasting, make up one day for each day missed, and, for each day, feed one poor person one ritl (a unit of weight) of bread with something to accompany it. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12667

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

All praise belongs to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help and ask His forgiveness. We seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without any partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and grant him abundant peace.

Section: What Invalidates the Fast and What Does Not

This falls into two categories. One comprises those things that invalidate the fast according to explicit scriptural text and scholarly consensus: eating, drinking and sexual intercourse. Allah Most High says:

“So now have relations with them and seek what Allah has decreed for you

---

## Page 12668

and eat and drink until the white thread of dawn becomes distinct to you from the black thread. Then complete the fast until nightfall.”

He thus permitted sexual relations, from which it was understood that fasting meant abstaining from sexual relations, eating and drinking. When He first said:

“Fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you,”

they understood that fasting meant refraining from eating, drinking and sexual intercourse. They knew and used the word “fasting” before Islam, as related in the two Sahīh collections from ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her:

“‘Āshūrā’ was a day on which Quraysh used to fast during the pre-Islamic era.”

It is authentically reported through more than one source that, before fasting the month of Ramadan was made obligatory, the Prophet commanded that the day of ‘Āshūrā’ be fasted and sent a caller to proclaim this. Thus, what this term denoted was already known to them.

It is likewise established by the Sunnah and the consensus of the Muslims that menstrual bleeding is incompatible with fasting: a menstruating woman does not fast, but makes up the missed fasts.

It is also established in the Sunnah, through the hadith of Laqīt ibn Sabirah, that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said to him:

“And draw water deeply into your nose, unless you are fasting.”

This indicates that water passing down through the nose invalidates the fast. This is the view of the vast majority of scholars.

---

## Page 12669

The Sunan collections also contain two hadiths:

One is the hadith transmitted by Hishām ibn Hassān, from Muhammad ibn Sīrīn, from Abū Hurayrah—may Allah be pleased with him—who reported that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever is overcome by vomiting while fasting need not make up the fast. But if he induces vomiting, he must make it up.”

Some scholars did not consider this hadith established; rather, they held it to be a statement of Abū Hurayrah. Abū Dāwūd said: “I heard Ahmad ibn Hanbal say, ‘There is nothing sound in this.’”

Al-Khattābī said: “He means that the hadith has not been reliably preserved.” Al-Tirmidhī said: “I asked Muhammad ibn Ismā‘īl al-Bukhārī about it, but he knew it only through ‘Īsā ibn Yūnus. He said, ‘I do not consider it reliably preserved.’”

He said: “Yahyā ibn Kathīr narrated from ‘Umar ibn al-Hakam that Abū Hurayrah did not regard vomiting as invalidating the fast.”

Al-Khattābī said: “Abū Dāwūd mentioned that Hafs ibn Ghiyāth narrated it from Hishām just as ‘Īsā ibn Yūnus did.”

He said: “I know of no disagreement among the scholars that someone overcome by vomiting need not make up the fast, or that someone who deliberately induces vomiting must make it up. They differed, however, over expiation. The majority of scholars said that he need only make up the fast. ‘Atā’ said that he must both make it up and offer expiation. This was also reported from al-Awzā‘ī, and it is the view of Abū Thawr.”

---

## Page 12670

I say: This is also implied by one of the two reports from Ahmad, in which he requires expiation of someone who undergoes cupping. For if he requires expiation of someone who undergoes cupping, then someone who deliberately induces vomiting has an even stronger claim to that ruling. However, his established position is that expiation is required only for sexual intercourse, as al-Shāfi‘ī also holds.

Those who did not consider this hadith established had not received it through a route they regarded as reliable. They identified its defect as ‘Īsā ibn Yūnus being alone in transmitting it. Yet it has been established that he was not alone: Hafs ibn Ghiyāth also transmitted it as he did. The other hadith corroborates it: the report transmitted by Ahmad and the compilers of the Sunan, such as al-Tirmidhī, from Abū al-Dardā’:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—vomited and broke his fast. I mentioned this to Thawbān, who said, ‘He spoke the truth; I poured water for his ablution.’”

Ahmad’s wording, however, is:

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—vomited and performed ablution.”

Ahmad transmitted it from Husayn al-Mu‘allim.

Al-Athram said: “I said to Ahmad, ‘They have differed considerably in transmitting this hadith.’ He replied, ‘Husayn al-Mu‘allim transmits it well.’” Al-Tirmidhī said: “Husayn’s report is the strongest on this subject.”

This has been cited as evidence that ablution is obligatory after vomiting, but it does not establish that. For if what is meant by ablution is ritual ablution, the report tells us only that he performed it. An action alone does not establish an obligation; rather, it indicates that ablution in such a case is prescribed. If it is held to be recommended, this constitutes acting upon the hadith.

---

## Page 12671

Likewise, none of the reports from some of the Companions about performing ablution after bleeding establishes that it is obligatory; rather, they indicate that it is recommended.

There is nothing in the scriptural evidence to establish that this is obligatory, as has been explained at length elsewhere. Indeed, al-Dāraqutnī and others narrated from Humayd, from Anas, who said:

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—underwent cupping and did not perform ablution; he did no more than wash the sites where he had been cupped.”

Ibn al-Jawzī also narrated it in “The Opponent’s Argument” without declaring it weak, although his usual practice is to raise whatever criticism he can.

As for the hadith narrated with the wording:

“Three things do not break the fast: vomiting, cupping, and a wet dream,”

and, in another wording:

“Neither one who vomits, nor one who has a wet dream, nor one who undergoes cupping breaks his fast,”

its established chain of transmission is that narrated by al-Thawrī and others from Zayd ibn Aslam, from one of his associates, from one of the Companions of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—who said: “The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said…” This is how Abū Dāwūd narrated it, and this man is unknown.

‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam also narrated it from his father, from ‘Atā’, from Abū Sa‘īd, from the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. However, ‘Abd al-Rahmān is considered weak by the scholars of narrator criticism.

(I say: The report’s transmission from Zayd through two routes, traced back to the Prophet, does not contradict its transmission

---

## Page 12672

with the Companion omitted; rather, it strengthens it. The hadith is established as a report from Zayd ibn Aslam, but this version contains the wording, “If vomiting overcomes him.” As for the report concerning cupping, it is either abrogated or, in turn, abrogates Ibn ‘Abbās’s report that “he underwent cupping while in a state of ritual consecration and fasting.” Perhaps its provision concerning vomiting, if it includes deliberately inducing vomiting, is also abrogated.

This supports the view that the prohibition of cupping came later. When two texts conflict—one departing from the presumed original state and the other maintaining it—the former is more likely to be the abrogating text. The abrogation of one provision strengthens the case for the abrogation of the provision paired with it. More than one narrator transmitted it from Zayd ibn Aslam with the Companion omitted. Yahyā ibn Ma‘īn said: “Zayd ibn Aslam’s hadith is of no weight.”

Even assuming its authenticity, it would refer to someone overcome by vomiting, since vomiting is mentioned alongside wet dreams. Someone who experiences this involuntarily, as when asleep, does not break his fast, by unanimous agreement. By contrast, someone who masturbates and ejaculates does break his fast. The expression “wet dream” applies only to someone who experiences it in his sleep.

Some have supposed that analogy requires that nothing leaving the body should invalidate the fast, and that someone who deliberately induces vomiting breaks his fast only because some of the food is liable to return. They have also said that a menstruating woman’s fast being invalidated is contrary to analogy. We have explained at length in our discussion of legal theory that there is nothing in Islamic law

---

## Page 12673

that runs contrary to sound analogy.

If it is objected:

You have stated that whoever deliberately breaks his fast without an excuse commits a major sin. Likewise, whoever deliberately leaves a daytime prayer unperformed until nightfall without an excuse commits a major sin, and that prayer is no longer accepted from him, according to the stronger of the two scholarly opinions—as with someone who misses Friday prayer, the stoning of the pillars, or other time-bound acts of worship. Yet the person who induces vomiting was instructed to make up his fast. It is also reported in the hadith concerning the man who had intercourse during Ramadan that he was instructed to make up his fast.

The reply is:

He was instructed to make up his fast only because a person induces vomiting for a valid excuse: for example, a sick person who uses vomiting as a treatment, or someone who induces vomiting because he has eaten something of doubtful lawfulness, as Abū Bakr vomited what he had consumed from a soothsayer’s earnings. When someone who induces vomiting has a valid excuse, his action is permissible, and he falls within the category of sick people who must make up their fasts. He is not among those who commit major sins by breaking their fasts without an excuse.

As for the report that the Prophet instructed the man who had intercourse to make up his fast, it is weak; more than one leading hadith scholar has graded it weak. This hadith is authentically transmitted through several routes in the two Sahīh collections, on the authority of both Abū Hurayrah and ‘Ā’ishah, yet none of the narrators mentions that he instructed him to make up his fast. Had he instructed him to do so, they would not all have omitted it, since this is a ruling of Islamic law that must be explained. And since he did not

---

## Page 12674

instruct him to do so, this indicates that making up the fast would no longer be accepted from him. This also indicates that he had deliberately broken his fast, rather than acting forgetfully or out of ignorance.

Concerning someone who has intercourse forgetfully, there are three opinions within the school of Ahmad and among other scholars. Three positions are also reported from Ahmad:

The first: he is required neither to make up the fast nor to offer expiation. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī, Abū Hanīfah, and the majority of scholars.

The second: he must make up the fast but need not offer expiation. This is Mālik’s position.

The third: he must do both. This is the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

The first opinion is stronger, as has been explained at length elsewhere. The evidence of the Quran and Sunnah establishes that Allah does not hold a person accountable for a prohibited act committed by mistake or forgetfully. Such a person is therefore in the same position as someone who did not commit it, and incurs no sin. Someone who incurs no sin is neither disobedient nor guilty of committing what he was forbidden to do. He has therefore done what he was commanded to do and has not done what he was forbidden to do. Such conduct does not invalidate his worship. Worship is invalidated only when a person fails to do what he was commanded to do or does what he was forbidden to do.

The consistent implication of this principle is that Hajj is not invalidated by any prohibited act committed forgetfully or by mistake, whether intercourse or anything else. This is the stronger of al-Shāfi‘ī’s two opinions.

---

## Page 12675

As for expiation and compensation, these are required because they serve as restitution for what has been destroyed. They belong to the category of liability requiring an equivalent replacement for what has been destroyed: even if a child, an insane person, or a sleeping person destroyed it, they would still be liable to replace it. If compensation for killing game is required of someone who acted forgetfully or by mistake, it falls into this category, like the blood money payable for someone killed by mistake and the expiation required for killing that person by mistake, as established by the explicit text of the Quran and the consensus of the Muslims.

The other prohibited acts, however, do not fall into this category. Trimming the nails, clipping the moustache, and enjoying comforts incompatible with remaining unkempt are comparable to using perfume and wearing clothing. Thus, the compensation required for them is of the same kind as that required for other prohibited acts, unlike game, for which an equivalent replacement is due.

The strongest opinion concerning someone who commits a prohibited act forgetfully or by mistake is therefore that no compensation is required except for killing game. Scholars hold several opinions on this matter. This is one of them, and it is the position of the literalists.

The second is that compensation is required for all such acts, even when committed forgetfully. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and one of the positions reported from Ahmad; it was preferred by al-Qādī and his followers.

The third distinguishes between acts involving destruction, such as killing game, shaving the hair, and trimming the nails, and acts involving no destruction, such as using perfume and wearing clothing. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and of Ahmad according to the second report from him, which a group of his followers preferred. This opinion is better than

---

## Page 12676

the others; yet removing hair and trimming nails should be classed with wearing clothing and using perfume, not with killing game. This is the sounder view.

The fourth opinion is that someone who kills game by mistake is not liable for it. This is one report from Ahmad, and scholars extended this ruling to hair and nails with even greater reason. Consistently applying this principle also means that a fasting person who eats, drinks, or has intercourse forgetfully or by mistake is not required to make up the fast. This is the position of a group of earlier and later scholars.

Some scholars, such as Mālik, hold that both forgetfulness and mistake invalidate the fast. Abū Hanīfah said that this is what analogy dictates, but he departed from it in cases of forgetfulness because of the hadith of Abū Hurayrah. Others hold that forgetfulness does not invalidate the fast, whereas mistake does. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad. Abū Hanīfah thus treated forgetfulness as a case warranting juristic preference. The followers of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad, however, said that forgetfulness does not invalidate the fast because it cannot be guarded against, unlike mistake: a person can refrain from breaking the fast until certain that the sun has set, and can abstain from eating and drinking when unsure whether dawn has broken.

This distinction is weak; indeed, the reverse is true. The Sunnah for a fasting person is to hasten the breaking of the fast and delay the predawn meal. When the sky is completely overcast, certainty admitting of no doubt cannot be attained until such a long time has elapsed that the Maghrib prayer is missed

---

## Page 12677

and the opportunity to break the fast promptly is also lost. Yet the worshipper is commanded to perform Maghrib and to do so promptly. If, when he considers it most likely that the sun has set, he is required to delay Maghrib until he is certain, he may delay it until the twilight disappears while still lacking certainty that the sun has set.

It is reported from Ibrāhīm al-Nakha‘ī and other early scholars—and this is also the position of Abū Hanīfah—that, in overcast weather, they recommended delaying Maghrib, praying ‘Ishā’ early, delaying Zuhr, and praying ‘Asr early. Ahmad and others explicitly stated this as well. Some of his followers explained this as a precaution to ensure that the prayer time had begun. But that is not the case, for this runs contrary to such precaution regarding the times of ‘Asr and ‘Ishā’. Rather, this practice was prescribed because these prayers may be combined when there is a valid excuse, and overcast weather constitutes such an excuse. The first prayer in the pair is therefore delayed and the second brought forward for two benefits.

The first is to ease the burden on people, allowing them to perform the prayers on a single occasion because rain is feared, just as they are combined when it is raining. The second is to attain certainty that the time for Maghrib has begun. Zuhr and ‘Asr may likewise be combined according to the sounder of the two opinions, which is also one of the two reports from Ahmad. The two prayers may also be combined because of deep mud, a strong, cold wind, and similar conditions, according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions. This is Mālik’s position and the sounder of the two opinions within Ahmad’s school.

---

## Page 12678

Second, an error in bringing ‘Asr and ‘Ishā’ forward is preferable to an error in bringing Zuhr and Maghrib forward. Zuhr and Maghrib may never be performed before their appointed times, whereas ‘Asr and ‘Ishā’ may be performed during the times of Zuhr and Maghrib, respectively, since these are also valid times for them when there is an excuse. Uncertainty constitutes an excuse, so combining the two prayers when their times are uncertain is preferable to praying in doubt.

This incorporates the precaution mentioned by those who offered the first explanation, but it is a precaution that ensures the prayers are performed within their shared time. Do you not see that they did not recommend such a delay for Fajr, nor for ‘Ishā’ and ‘Asr? If this were prompted by awareness of the risk of praying before the appointed time, it would apply consistently to Fajr, and likewise to ‘Asr and ‘Ishā’.

A hadith from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructs that ‘Asr be prayed early on an overcast day. He said:

“Pray early on an overcast day, for whoever abandons the ‘Asr prayer, his deeds have come to nothing.”

If it is said, “If delaying Maghrib in overcast weather is recommended, then breaking the fast should likewise be delayed,” the answer is:

Delaying Maghrib is recommended only when ‘Ishā’ is brought forward, so that both are performed before the twilight disappears. Delaying Maghrib until one fears that the twilight will disappear is not recommended, nor is delaying the breaking of the fast until that point. Thus, the prescribed way of combining prayers when it rains is to bring the later prayer forward and offer both during the time of

---

## Page 12679

Maghrib. It is not recommended to delay the congregation’s Maghrib prayer until the twilight disappears. Rather, this causes people great hardship, whereas combining prayers was prescribed precisely to spare Muslims hardship.

Moreover, the recommended delaying and bringing forward do not require performing the two prayers back to back. Rather, Zuhr should be delayed and ‘Asr brought forward, even if there is an interval between them. The same applies to Maghrib and ‘Ishā’: people may perform one prayer and wait for the other, without needing to go home and then return. Likewise, according to the more correct of the two opinions, performing the prayers in immediate succession is not a condition for combining them, as we have explained elsewhere.

Furthermore, it is established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī that Asmā’ bint Abī Bakr said:

“We broke our fast on a cloudy day in Ramadan during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and then the sun appeared.”

This indicates two things. First, in cloudy weather, it is not recommended to delay breaking the fast until sunset is certain. They did not do so, nor did the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instruct them to do so. The Companions, together with their Prophet, were more knowledgeable and more obedient to Allah and His Messenger than those who came after them.

Second, making up the fast is not obligatory. Had the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed them to make it up, this would have become widely known, just as their breaking the fast was reported. Since no such instruction was transmitted, this indicates that he did not instruct them to do so.

If it is objected, “But Hishām ibn ‘Urwah was asked, ‘Were they instructed to make up the fast?’ He replied, ‘Was there any alternative to making it up?’”

---

## Page 12680

The reply is that Hishām said this on the basis of his own opinion; it was not reported in the hadith. Evidence that he had no knowledge of the matter is Ma‘mar’s report: “I heard Hishām say, ‘I do not know whether they made up the fast or not.’” Al-Bukhārī recorded both statements from him. Hishām narrated the hadith from his mother, Fātimah bint al-Mundhir, from Asmā’. Hishām also reported from his father, ‘Urwah, that they were not instructed to make up the fast, and ‘Urwah was more knowledgeable than his son.

This is also the view of Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, a contemporary and peer of Ahmad ibn Hanbal who agreed with him in both the foundational principles and detailed rulings of his legal approach. Their opinions are often cited together. Al-Kawsaj put his questions to both Ahmad and Ishāq, as did Harb al-Kirmānī and others. This is why al-Tirmidhī cites the opinions of Ahmad and Ishāq together: he transmitted their views from al-Kawsaj’s collection of questions.

Likewise, Abū Zur‘ah, Abū Hātim, Ibn Qutaybah, and other leading scholars of the early generations, the Sunnah, and hadith studied jurisprudence according to the approach of Ahmad and Ishāq, giving their views precedence over those of others. The leading hadith scholars, such as al-Bukhārī, Muslim, al-Tirmidhī, al-Nasā’ī, and others, were also among their followers and acquired knowledge and jurisprudence from them. Dāwūd was one of Ishāq’s students.

When Ahmad ibn Hanbal was asked about Ishāq, he would say: “Am I to be asked

---

## Page 12681

about Ishāq? Ishāq is the one to be asked about me.” Al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ishāq, Abū ‘Ubayd, Abū Thawr, Muhammad ibn Nasr al-Marwazī, Dāwūd ibn ‘Alī, and others like them were all jurists of the hadith tradition. May Allah be pleased with them all.

Furthermore, Allah says in His Book:

“And eat and drink until the white thread of dawn becomes distinct to you from the black thread.”

This verse, together with the authentic hadiths from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), makes clear that a person is instructed to eat until dawn becomes apparent. Thus, while uncertain whether dawn has broken, he remains instructed to eat, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Section

As for applying kohl, using an enema, instilling drops into the urethra, and treating a head wound reaching the membrane surrounding the brain and a wound penetrating a body cavity, scholars have differed over these matters. Some hold that none of them invalidates the fast. Others hold that all of them invalidate it except kohl; others, that all invalidate it except instilling drops into the urethra; and still others, that neither kohl nor such drops invalidates it, whereas the remaining treatments do.

---

## Page 12682

The more evident view, however, is that none of these things invalidates the fast. Fasting is part of the Muslims’ religion that learned people and laypeople alike need to understand. If these things were among those that Allah and His Messenger had forbidden during fasting, and if they invalidated the fast, the Messenger would have been obliged to explain this. Had he mentioned it, the Companions would have known it and conveyed it to the Muslim community, just as they conveyed the rest of his teachings. Since no scholar has transmitted anything from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) concerning this—neither an authentic nor a weak hadith, whether with a connected or an interrupted chain—it is known that he mentioned none of these things. The hadith reported concerning kohl is weak. Abū Dāwūd narrated it in his Sunan, and no one else narrated it. It is found neither in Ahmad’s Musnad nor in the other authoritative collections.

Abū Dāwūd said:

Al-Nufaylī narrated to us: ‘Alī ibn Thābit narrated to us: ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn al-Nu‘mān narrated to me: Ma‘bad ibn Hawdhah narrated to us, from his father, from his grandfather, that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) instructed people to use perfumed antimony kohl at bedtime and said:

“Those who are fasting should avoid it.”

Abū Dāwūd said: Yahyā ibn Ma‘īn said, “This is a rejected hadith.”

[Al-Mundhirī] (*) said: As for ‘Abd al-Rahmān, Yahyā ibn Ma‘īn declared him weak, while Abū Hātim al-Rāzī described him as truthful. But who knows his father, or his integrity and accuracy in transmission?

Ma‘bad’s hadith has likewise been opposed by another weak hadith, namely the report that al-Tirmidhī narrated through his chain from Anas ibn Mālik, who said:

“A man came to the Prophet

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 266) said: Perhaps it should read: “Ibn al-Jawzī said.” Al-Tahqīq, 2/90.

---

## Page 12683

(peace and blessings be upon him) and said, ‘My eye is troubling me. May I apply kohl while I am fasting?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “This report is not strong, and nothing authentic has been reported from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) on this subject.” Its chain includes Abū ‘Ātikah, of whom al-Bukhārī said: “He transmits rejected hadiths.”

Those who held that such things invalidate the fast—including enemas and treating wounds that reach the membrane surrounding the brain or penetrate a body cavity—had no evidence from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). Rather, they based their position on what they considered sound analogical reasoning. Their strongest evidence was his statement:

“Draw water deeply into your nose, unless you are fasting.”

They argued that this indicates that anything reaching the brain through a fasting person’s own action invalidates his fast. By analogy, the same applies to anything that reaches his bodily interior through his own action, whether by an enema or otherwise, regardless of whether it reaches the place where food and nourishment go or some other internal part of the body.

Those who exempted the instillation of drops into the urethra argued that the drops do not descend into the bodily interior but merely seep through. Thus, what enters his urethra is like what enters his mouth or nose.

Those who exempted kohl argued that the eye is unlike the genital opening and the anus: it absorbs kohl just as the body absorbs oil and water.

Those who held that kohl invalidates the fast argued that it penetrates into his body to the point that he coughs it up

---

## Page 12684

when fasting, because there is a passage from within the eye to the throat.

If their main reliance is on these analogies and others like them, a fast cannot be declared invalid on the strength of such analogies, for several reasons:

First, although analogy constitutes proof when its conditions of validity are met, we have explained in discussing legal theory that all religious rulings are also clarified by the scriptural texts, even where sound analogy indicates the same ruling that a text indicates only subtly. Thus, when we know that the Messenger neither prohibited something nor made it obligatory, we know that it is neither prohibited nor obligatory, and that any analogy establishing its obligation or prohibition is unsound. We know that nothing in the Quran or Sunnah indicates that these things mentioned by some jurists invalidate the fast; we therefore know that they do not invalidate it.

Second, the rulings that the Muslim community needs to know must necessarily have been explained publicly by the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him), and the community must necessarily have transmitted them. Where this has not occurred, we know that the matter is not part of his religion. In the same way, we know that he did not make fasting any month other than Ramadan obligatory, nor pilgrimage to any House other than the Sacred House, nor any prescribed prayer other than the five. He did not require a ritual bath following physical intimacy with a woman without ejaculation, nor ablution because of intense fright—even though a bodily discharge might be expected in such circumstances—nor did he prescribe

---

## Page 12685

two units of prayer after walking between al-Safā and al-Marwa, as he prescribed two units of prayer after circumambulating the House.

By the same reasoning, we know that semen is not ritually impure: no report with a chain of transmission that can serve as proof states that he commanded Muslims to wash semen from their bodies and clothes, despite its being a common occurrence. Indeed, he instructed a menstruating woman to wash menstrual blood from her garment, although the need for this was less frequent, yet he did not command Muslims to wash semen from their bodies and clothes.

The report that some jurists relate—“Clothing is to be washed of urine, feces, semen, pre-ejaculatory fluid, and blood”—is not a statement of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him). It appears in none of the reliable hadith collections, nor has any hadith scholar transmitted it with a chain that can serve as proof. It has only been reported from ʿAmmār and ʿĀʾisha as their own statements.

ʿĀʾisha’s washing semen from his garment and rubbing it off does not establish that doing so is obligatory. Clothes are also washed to remove dirt, nasal mucus, and spittle. An obligation can only be established by his command, particularly since he did not command the rest of the Muslims to wash their clothes of it, nor is it reported that he commanded ʿĀʾisha to do so. Rather, he tacitly approved her action, which indicates that it is permissible, or that it is good and recommended.

---

## Page 12686

Obligation, however, requires evidence. By these same methods, we also know that he did not make ablution obligatory after touching women or after ritually impure substances issue from anywhere other than the urinary or anal passages. No one has transmitted from him, through a reliable chain, that he commanded this, although people were known to undergo cupping, vomit, and sustain wounds in jihad and elsewhere. Some of his Companions even had a vein opened to release blood—a procedure known as bloodletting—yet no Muslim has reported that he commanded his Companions to perform ablution because of this.

Likewise, men would frequently touch their wives, with or without desire, yet no Muslim has reported that he commanded people to perform ablution because of this. Nor does the Quran indicate such a requirement. Rather, what is meant by “touching” is sexual intercourse, as explained at length elsewhere.

His command to perform ablution after touching the penis is only a recommendation, either in all cases or when doing so arouses desire. Likewise, it is recommended for someone who touches women and becomes sexually aroused to perform ablution. The same applies to someone whose thoughts arouse his desire and cause an erection, and to someone who touches a beardless youth or anyone else and has an erection.

Performing ablution when desire is aroused is thus akin to performing ablution when angry. This is recommended because of the report in the Sunan collections that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Indeed,

---

## Page 12687

anger is from Satan, and Satan is from fire. Fire is extinguished only with water, so when one of you becomes angry, let him perform ablution.” Likewise, overpowering desire is from Satan and fire, and ablution extinguishes it. It also extinguishes the heat of anger. Performing ablution for this reason is recommended.

Likewise, his command to perform ablution after eating food touched by fire is a recommendation: since food touched by fire enters the body, one should perform ablution, for fire is extinguished with water. Nothing in the texts indicates that this command was abrogated. Rather, the texts indicate that it is not obligatory. The view that ablution is recommended is among the most balanced positions, between the view that makes it obligatory and the view that considers it abrogated. This is one of the two views in the school of Ahmad and others.

By the same method, we also know that the urine and dung of animals whose meat is lawful to eat are not ritually impure. These are things people commonly encounter. Those people kept camels and sheep, and would sit and pray in their enclosures, which were full of their droppings. Had these places been comparable to privies, they would have counted as latrines, and the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) would have commanded them to avoid them, not to soil their bodies or clothes with their droppings, and not to pray there.

How could that be, when authentic hadiths establish that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and his Companions used to pray in sheep pens, and that he commanded prayer in sheep pens and forbade

---

## Page 12688

prayer in camel enclosures. It is therefore clear that this was not because their droppings are ritually impure. Rather, it was akin to his command to perform ablution after eating camel meat, whereas concerning sheep meat he said:

“If you wish, perform ablution, and if you wish, do not.”

He also said:

“Camels were created from jinn, and atop every camel is a devil.”

And he said:

“Pride and arrogance are found among the boisterous camel owners, while tranquility is found among those who tend sheep.”

Since camels possess satanic qualities that Allah and His Messenger dislike, he commanded ablution after eating their meat, for this extinguishes those satanic qualities. He prohibited prayer in their enclosures because they are places where devils dwell, just as he prohibited prayer in bathhouses because they are places where devils dwell. Places inhabited by foul spirits are all the more deserving of being avoided for prayer, as are places containing foul substances. Indeed, foul spirits love foul substances.

This is why latrines are frequented by devils. Prayer in them is even more deserving of prohibition than prayer in bathhouses, camel enclosures, or on ritually impure ground. No specific text was transmitted concerning latrines, because the matter was too obvious to Muslims to require explanation. Thus, no Muslim would sit in latrines or pray in them, and they would go out into the open country to relieve themselves

---

## Page 12689

before latrines were installed in their homes. When they heard his prohibition of prayer in bathhouses or camel enclosures, they understood that the prohibition of prayer in latrines applied with even greater force. Moreover, a hadith has been narrated containing “the prohibition of prayer in cemeteries, slaughterhouses, refuse dumps, latrines, thoroughfares, camel enclosures, and atop Allah’s Sacred House.” Hadith scholars disagree concerning this report.

Ahmad’s followers hold two views on the matter. Some regard these as places where prayer is prohibited. Others say: “I have found nothing in this hadith, nor have I found anything in Ahmad’s statements either permitting or prohibiting this.” Yet Ahmad did disapprove of prayer in places where divine punishment had descended, as his son ʿAbd Allāh reported from him, on the basis of the hadith transmitted with its chain from ʿAlī and recorded by Abū Dāwūd. Ahmad explicitly mentioned only latrines, camel enclosures, and bathhouses. These are the three mentioned by al-Khiraqī and others.

Those who uphold this ruling may establish it by analogy with the cases explicitly addressed in the texts, or on the basis of the hadith. Anyone who draws a distinction must challenge the hadith and explain the relevant difference. Furthermore, a prohibition may indicate disapproval or strict forbiddance.

Since rulings concerning matters that commonly affect people must be explained publicly by the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and must be transmitted by the Muslim community, it is clear

---

## Page 12690

that using kohl and similar substances is a matter that commonly affects people, just as applying oil, bathing, using incense, and wearing perfume do. If any of these invalidated the fast, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would have explained this, just as he explained that other things invalidate it. Since he did not explain any such ruling, it is known that kohl belongs in the same category as perfume, incense, and oil.

Incense smoke may rise into the nose, enter the brain, and condense into solid matter. Oil is absorbed by the body, enters its interior, and strengthens a person. Likewise, perfume considerably invigorates a person. Since he did not prohibit the fasting person from these things, this indicates that a fasting person may use perfume, incense, and oil, and likewise apply kohl.

During his lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, Muslims would sustain injuries, whether in jihad or otherwise: wounds reaching the membrane surrounding the brain and wounds penetrating a body cavity. If this invalidated the fast, he would have explained that to them. Since he did not prohibit the fasting person from it, it is known that he did not make it something that invalidates the fast.

(Third argument: Establishing that something invalidates the fast by analogy requires a valid analogy. This must rest either on an effective cause, through establishing the factor shared by both cases, or on ruling out any relevant distinction between them. Either evidence must establish the effective cause in the original case, allowing its ruling to be extended on that basis to the new case, or it must be known that the two cases do not differ in any attribute recognized as relevant by Islamic law. No such analogy holds here. This is because nothing in the evidence indicates that what has been designated as invalidating the fast by Allah

---

## Page 12691

and His Messenger is whatever reaches the brain or the body, enters through an opening, reaches an internal cavity, or meets any similar criterion that the proponents of these views regard as the basis for the ruling laid down by Allah and His Messenger. They maintain that Allah and His Messenger made food and drink invalidate the fast only because of this common feature, shared by food and drink, medicines that reach the brain or an internal cavity when applied to wounds reaching the membrane surrounding the brain or penetrating a body cavity, and substances that reach an internal cavity through kohl, enemas, drops introduced into the urethra, and the like.

If there is no evidence that Allah and His Messenger made the ruling depend on this attribute, then to say, “Allah and His Messenger made this invalidate the fast only for this reason,” is to speak without knowledge. Likewise, to say, “Allah has forbidden the fasting person to do this,” is to declare things lawful or unlawful without knowledge. This entails saying about Allah what one does not know, which is impermissible.

Any scholar who believes that this shared feature is the basis of the ruling is like someone who believes an incorrect legal position to be correct, or believes that an expression conveys a meaning the Messenger did not intend. This is an exercise of scholarly judgment for which they are rewarded. It does not necessarily follow, however, that their view rests on evidence from Islamic law that a Muslim is obliged to follow.

---

## Page 12692

The fourth point is that, when the Lawgiver’s words do not indicate the effective cause of a ruling, analogy is valid only if we examine the attributes of the original case and find that none is suitable as the effective cause except the particular attribute identified. Whenever we establish the effective cause in the original case through suitability, concomitance, or consistent resemblance—for those who accept the latter—such examination is indispensable. If the original case has two suitable attributes, it is impermissible to say that the ruling rests on one rather than the other.

It is well known that the revealed texts and scholarly consensus establish that eating, drinking, sexual intercourse, and menstruation invalidate the fast. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade anyone performing ablution from drawing water deeply into the nose while fasting. Their analogy with drawing water into the nose is their strongest argument, as noted above, yet it is a weak analogy. When someone draws water in through his nostrils, it descends into his throat and internal cavity, producing the same effect as drinking through the mouth: his body is nourished by that water, his thirst is quenched, and the food in his stomach is digested, just as happens when he drinks water. Even without a revealed text concerning this, reason would establish that it belongs to the same category as drinking. The two differ only in whether the water enters through the mouth, and that difference has no bearing on the ruling.

Indeed, water merely entering the mouth does not invalidate the fast. Its entry into the mouth is neither something that invalidates the fast nor a constituent part of what invalidates it, since it has no effect in itself; it is merely a route to the fast being invalidated. The same does not apply to kohl, enemas, or the treatment of wounds penetrating a body cavity or reaching the membrane surrounding the brain. Kohl provides no nourishment whatsoever, and no one introduces kohl into his internal cavity through either his nose or his mouth.

---

## Page 12693

And likewise, an enema provides no nourishment; rather, it empties the body, just as smelling a laxative might do, or suffering a fright that causes the bowels to empty. An enema does not reach the stomach. The medicine that reaches the stomach when treating wounds penetrating a body cavity or reaching the membrane surrounding the brain is unlike the nourishment that reaches it. Allah, glorified be He, says:

“Fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you.”

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Fasting is a shield.”

He also said:

“Satan flows through the son of Adam as blood flows, so constrict his pathways through the hunger of fasting.”

The fasting person is forbidden to eat and drink because doing so strengthens the body. Thus, one abstains from the eating and drinking that generate the abundant blood through which Satan flows. Such blood is produced only by nourishment, not by an enema, kohl, drops introduced into the penis, or medicine used to treat wounds reaching the membrane surrounding the brain or penetrating a body cavity. Blood is, however, produced from water drawn in through the nose, since water is among the things from which blood is produced. Prohibiting this therefore completes the fast.

If these and other considerations are present in the original case established by revealed text and scholarly consensus, then their claim that the Lawgiver made the ruling dependent on the attributes they have mentioned

---

## Page 12694

is countered by these other attributes. Such a counterargument invalidates every form of analogy unless it is shown that the attribute they have asserted, rather than this alternative, is the effective cause.

The fifth argument is that revealed text and scholarly consensus establish that a fasting person is forbidden to eat, drink, or have sexual intercourse. It is also established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Satan flows through the son of Adam as blood flows.”

There is no doubt that blood is produced from food and drink. When a person eats or drinks, the pathways of the devils widen. Hence he said:

“So constrict his pathways through hunger.”

Some transmit this wording as a saying traced back to the Prophet. Hence the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“When Ramadan begins, the gates of Paradise are opened, the gates of the Fire are closed, and the devils are shackled.”

For the devils’ pathways, namely the blood, become constricted. When they become constricted, hearts are moved to perform the good deeds through which the gates of Paradise are opened, and to abandon the evil deeds through which the gates of the Fire are opened. The devils are shackled, and their shackling diminishes their strength and activity, so they cannot do during Ramadan what they do at other times. He did not say that they were killed or that they died; rather, he said, “shackled.” A shackled devil may still cause harm, but this is less frequent and less severe than outside Ramadan. This varies according to the completeness or deficiency of the fast. Someone whose fast is complete repels Satan in a way that an incomplete fast cannot. This rationale is therefore evident in prohibiting the fasting person from eating

---

## Page 12695

and drinking. The ruling accords with this rationale, and the Lawgiver’s words indicate that this attribute is relevant and has a causal effect. This prohibition does not apply to enemas, kohl, and the like.

If it is said: But kohl may descend into the stomach and be transformed into blood.

The answer is: The same could be said of vapour that rises through the nose to the brain and is transformed into blood, or of oil absorbed by the body. What is prohibited is only that which reaches the stomach, is transformed into blood, and is distributed throughout the body. We make this (a sixth argument: we draw an analogy between kohl, enemas, and similar things, on the one hand, and incense and oil, on the other. Their common attribute is that they are not substances that nourish the body and are transformed into blood in the stomach. This attribute is what entails that these things do not invalidate the fast, and it is also present in the disputed case. A case may lend itself to comparison with two precedents, being linked to each through the attributes in which it resembles it.

If it is said: This is digested by the stomach and transformed into blood through which the body grows, but it provides deficient nourishment. It is therefore like eating poison or some other harmful substance, and is comparable to the case of someone who

---

## Page 12696

eats so much that it causes him indigestion and illness. Prohibiting this while fasting is all the more emphatic, since it is prohibited even when one is not fasting, and fasting further reinforces the prohibition. This is like prohibiting unlawful sexual intercourse: if lawful intercourse is forbidden to him, then unlawful intercourse is forbidden all the more.

If it is said: But sexual intercourse invalidates the fast, even though this rationale does not apply to it?

The answer is: These rulings are established by explicit texts and scholarly consensus, so establishing them does not require analogy. Indeed, their underlying causes may differ: prohibiting food and drink and their invalidation of the fast may serve one wisdom; prohibiting intercourse and its invalidation of the fast may serve another; and the invalidation of the fast through menstruation may serve yet another. Menstruation itself cannot be described as prohibited. This is because the things established by explicit texts and scholarly consensus as invalidating the fast fall into two categories: voluntary acts that are prohibited to a person, such as eating and intercourse, and things beyond one’s control, such as menstrual bleeding. Their underlying causes likewise fall into different categories.

We therefore say: Insofar as intercourse causes the emission of semen, it is comparable to induced vomiting, menstruation, and cupping—as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills. It involves evacuation, rather than taking things into the body, as eating and drinking do. Insofar as it satisfies one of the two appetites, however, it is comparable to eating and drinking. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, related in an authentic hadith that Allah Most High said:

“Fasting is for Me, and I shall reward

---

## Page 12697

him for it. He gives up his desire and his food for My sake.” A person’s giving up what he desires for Allah’s sake is itself an intended act of worship for which he is rewarded, just as a pilgrim in ihrām is rewarded for giving up his customary clothing, perfume, and similar bodily comforts.

Sexual intercourse is among the greatest bodily pleasures and sources of joy and delight for the soul. It stimulates desire, the blood, and the body more than eating does. Since Satan flows through the son of Adam as blood flows, and nourishment increases the flow of blood, which is his pathway, eating or drinking makes the soul more inclined toward desires and weakens its resolve and love for worship. This effect is even more pronounced in intercourse: it increases the soul’s inclination toward desires and weakens its resolve to worship to a greater degree. Indeed, intercourse is the ultimate sensual pleasure, and the desire for it is stronger than the desire for food and drink. For this reason, one who engages in intercourse is required to offer the expiation prescribed for zihār: freeing a slave or performing its prescribed substitute, as established by the Sunnah and scholarly consensus. This is because the act is more serious, the urge toward it stronger, and its harmful consequences greater.

This, then, is the greater of the two underlying wisdoms in prohibiting intercourse. Its weakening of the body, like bodily evacuation, is another wisdom. In these two respects, it is comparable to eating and menstruation, but it exceeds them in these effects. Its invalidation of the fast is therefore more serious than invalidation through eating or menstruation.

Let us now explain the wisdom underlying the ruling on menstruation and how it accords with analogy. We say:

Islamic law has brought justice in all things. Excess in worship is a form of injustice

---

## Page 12698

which the Lawgiver has forbidden, enjoining moderation in worship. For this reason, he commanded hastening the breaking of the fast and delaying the predawn meal, prohibited fasting continuously without breaking the fast, and said:

“The best and most balanced fasting is that of David, peace be upon him: he would fast one day and not fast the next, and he would not flee when he met the enemy.”

Balance in worship is among the Lawgiver’s greatest objectives. Hence Allah Most High said:

“O you who believe, do not forbid the good things Allah has made lawful for you”—and the rest of the verse.

He thus regarded forbidding what is lawful as a transgression contrary to justice. Allah Most High also said:

“For the wrongdoing of those who were Jews, We forbade them good things that had been lawful to them, and for their hindering many from Allah’s way,”

“and for their taking usury, though they had been forbidden it.”

Because they were unjust, they were punished by having good things forbidden to them. By contrast, for the balanced and just community, He made good things lawful and prohibited impure things.

Since this is so, just as the fasting person is forbidden to consume food and drink that strengthen and nourish him, he is likewise forbidden to expel substances in a way that weakens him and depletes the material that nourishes him. Otherwise, allowing him to do this would harm him, and he would be exceeding proper bounds in his worship rather than observing balance.

Bodily discharges are of two kinds. One kind consists of those he cannot prevent, or whose passing causes him no harm. These are not prohibited, as with urine and feces: their passing does him no harm, nor can he avoid it. Even if he induces their passage, it does not harm him; rather, it benefits him. Likewise, if he is overcome by vomiting, he cannot prevent it. The same applies to a wet dream

---

## Page 12699

during sleep, which he cannot prevent. But if he induces vomiting, it expels the food and drink transformed in the stomach from which he derives nourishment. Similarly, masturbation, besides involving sexual pleasure, expels semen, which is formed in the stomach through the transformation of blood. It thus expels the blood that nourishes him. This is why excessive discharge of semen harms a person, and it comes out red.

The blood discharged during menstruation likewise entails a loss of blood. A menstruating woman can fast at other times, when she is not bleeding. Her fasting then is balanced: she does not lose the blood that strengthens her body and sustains it. Fasting during menstruation, however, entails losing the blood that sustains her, depleting and weakening her body, and taking her fasting beyond moderation. She was therefore commanded to fast outside the times of menstruation.

This differs from the case of a woman with irregular non-menstrual bleeding, for such bleeding is not confined to particular times, leaving her no particular time at which she can be instructed to fast. Nor can she avoid it. It is like being overcome by vomiting, bleeding from wounds and boils, wet dreams, and similar occurrences that have no fixed time allowing a person to avoid them. These were therefore not deemed incompatible with fasting, unlike menstrual bleeding.

---

## Page 12700

By extension, the same principle applies to drawing blood through cupping, venesection, and the like. Scholars disagree over whether cupping breaks the fast. There are numerous hadiths reporting the Prophet’s statement (peace and blessings be upon him):

“Both the cupper and the person cupped have broken their fast.”

The leading hadith masters have discussed these reports in detail. Several Companions disapproved of cupping for someone who was fasting, and some would undergo cupping only at night. When Ramadan began, the people of Basra would close the cuppers’ shops. The view that cupping breaks the fast is held by most jurists versed in hadith, such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, Ibn Khuzaymah, Ibn al-Mundhir, and others. Hadith scholars who understand hadith deeply and act upon it are foremost in following Muhammad (peace and blessings be upon him).

Those who did not consider the fast of the person cupped to be broken relied on the report established in the Sahīh:

“The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) underwent cupping while fasting and in ihrām (the consecrated state for pilgrimage).”

Ahmad and others challenged the additional wording, “while fasting,” and said: “What is established is that he underwent cupping while in ihrām.” Ahmad said that Yahyā ibn Saʿīd reported that Shuʿbah said: “Al-Hakam did not hear Miqsam’s hadith concerning cupping for a fasting person.” This refers to the hadith narrated by Shuʿbah, from al-Hakam, from Miqsam, from Ibn ʿAbbās:

“The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) underwent cupping while fasting and in ihrām.”

---

## Page 12701

Muhannā said:
“I asked Ahmad about the hadith narrated by Habīb ibn al-Shahīd, from Maymūn ibn Mihrān, from Ibn ‘Abbās: ‘The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) underwent cupping while fasting and in ihrām.’ He replied: ‘It is not authentic; Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Ansārī rejected it.’”

Al-Athram said:
“I heard Abū ‘Abd Allāh reject this hadith and declare it weak. He said: ‘Al-Ansārī’s books were lost during the days of al-Muntasir. Thereafter, he narrated from his servant’s books, and this report was among those.’”

(Muhannā said: “I asked Ahmad about the hadith narrated by Qabīsah, from Sufyān, from Hammād, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās, etc. He replied: ‘This is an error on Qabīsah’s part.’”) (*).

“I also asked Yahyā about Qabīsah. He replied: ‘He is a truthful man, but the hadith he narrates from Sufyān, from Sa‘īd, is an error on his part.’”

Muhannā said:
“I asked Ahmad about the hadith of Ibn ‘Abbās: ‘The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) underwent cupping while in ihrām and fasting.’ He replied: ‘It does not contain the words “while fasting”; it says only “while in ihrām.” Sufyān narrated it from ‘Amr ibn Dīnār, from Tāwūs, from Ibn ‘Abbās: “The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) underwent cupping on his head while in ihrām.” The same was reported from Tāwūs and ‘Atā’, from Ibn ‘Abbās, and from ‘Abd al-Razzāq, from Ma‘mar, from Ibn Khuthaym, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās. These companions of Ibn ‘Abbās do not mention “while fasting.”’”

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 202):
> The insertion of “etc.” appears to be the copyist’s doing. The original wording abbreviated here is the text transmitted through this chain: “The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) underwent cupping while fasting and in a state of ihrām (ritual consecration).”

---

## Page 12702

I say:

What Imam Ahmad mentioned is precisely what the two imams, al-Bukhārī and Muslim, agreed upon. For this reason, Muslim omitted the hadith mentioning cupping while fasting and included only the report of cupping while in ihrām.

They offered weak interpretations of the hadiths concerning cupping, such as saying, “The two of them were backbiting,” or, “He broke his fast for another reason.” The best interpretation offered is that mentioned by al-Shāfi‘ī and others: that this ruling was abrogated. They argued that this statement was made in Ramadan, whereas his undergoing cupping while in ihrām occurred later, since that ihrām came after Ramadan.

This, too, is weak. Rather, he—may Allah’s blessings be upon him—entered ihrām for ‘umrah in Dhū al-Qa‘dah of the year 6 AH, the year of al-Hudaybiyah. The following year, he entered ihrām for the make-up ‘umrah in Dhū al-Qa‘dah. On the third occasion, in the year of the Conquest, he entered ihrām from al-Ji‘rānah for ‘umrah in Dhū al-Qa‘dah. In the year 10 AH, he entered ihrām for the Farewell Pilgrimage in Dhū al-Qa‘dah. Thus, it has not been specified on which of these occasions he (peace and blessings be upon him) underwent cupping while in ihrām and fasting.

What supports the view that the ihrām during which he underwent cupping preceded the conquest of Mecca is his statement:

“Both the cupper and the one being cupped have broken their fasts.”

He undoubtedly said this in the year of the Conquest, as stated in the strongest hadith reports. Ahmad narrated through his chain of transmission from Thawbān:

“The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) came upon a man undergoing cupping in Ramadan and said, ‘Both the cupper and the one being cupped have broken their fasts.’”

---

## Page 12703

And Ahmad said:

Ismā‘īl informed us, from Khālid al-Hadhdhā’, from Abū Qilābah, from al-Ash‘ath, from Shaddād ibn Aws, that he passed with the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him), at the time of the Conquest, by a man undergoing cupping at al-Baqī‘, when eighteen nights of Ramadan had elapsed. He said:

“Both the cupper and the one being cupped have broken their fasts.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: I asked al-Bukhārī, and he replied, “There is nothing more authentic on this subject than the hadith of Shaddād ibn Aws and the hadith of Thawbān.” I asked, “What about the inconsistencies in their transmission?”

He replied, “I consider both authentic, because Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd narrated both hadiths from Abū Qilābah, from Abū Asmā’, from Thawbān, from Abū al-Ash‘ath, from Shaddād.”

I say:

This point made by al-Bukhārī is among the clearest proofs of the authenticity of both hadiths narrated by Abū Qilābah.

Further on, he said:

Another consideration supporting the view that the abrogating ruling is that cupping breaks the fast is that this was narrated from him by his closest Companions, who attended to him both at home and on journeys and were privy to his private affairs. These included Bilāl and ‘Ā’ishah, as well as his two clients, Usāmah and Thawbān. It was also narrated from him by members of the Ansār who belonged to his inner circle, such as Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj and Shaddād ibn Aws. In Ahmad’s Musnad, Rāfi‘ ibn Khadīj reports that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Both the cupper and the one being cupped have broken their fasts.”

Ahmad said, “The most authentic report on this subject is the hadith of Rāfi‘.”

He cited the hadiths stating, “Both the cupper and the one being cupped have broken their fasts,” before going on to say: “They then differed, adopting several views.”

---

## Page 12704

The first view:
The person being cupped breaks his fast, but the cupper does not. Al-Khiraqī mentioned this view. However, the explicitly stated position of Ahmad and the majority of his followers is that both procedures break the fast. The text indicates this, so there is no justification for setting it aside.

The second view:
The person who undergoes cupping and has blood drawn breaks his fast, but undergoing venesection or similar procedures does not break it, because these are not called cupping. This is the view of al-Qādī and his followers. Later scholars disagreed over whether scarification of the ears falls within the meaning of cupping.

Some say that scarification is equivalent to cupping. This is the view of our shaykh Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, and the statements of the scholars universally indicate it. None of them discusses scarification separately. Had they considered it outside the scope of cupping, they would have mentioned it separately, as they did venesection. It is therefore evident that they regarded scarification as a form of cupping. Our shaykh Abū Muhammad said, “This is the correct view.”

Further on, he said:

The fourth view—which is the correct one, adopted by Abū al-Muzaffar ibn Hubayrah, the learned and just vizier, and others—is that cupping, venesection, and similar procedures break the fast. This is because the relevant feature of cupping is also present in venesection, from both a legal and a physiological standpoint. Whenever the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) encouraged and prescribed cupping, this also constituted encouragement of equivalent procedures, such as venesection and others. In hot regions, however, the heat draws the body’s blood

---

## Page 12705

so that it rises to the surface of the skin and is drawn out by cupping. In cold regions, blood retreats into the veins to escape the cold, for like is drawn to like, just as the body’s interior grows warm in winter and cool in summer. Thus, venesection and opening the veins serve people in cold regions as cupping serves those in hot regions. Neither revealed law nor reason distinguishes between the two.

We have already explained that cupping’s invalidation of the fast accords with the fundamental principles and analogical reasoning, and that it is akin to the invalidation of the fast through menstrual bleeding, deliberately induced vomiting, and masturbation. Accordingly, whatever means a person deliberately uses to have blood drawn invalidates his fast, just as deliberately inducing vomiting invalidates it by whatever means—whether by inserting his hand, smelling something that makes him vomit, or placing his hand beneath his abdomen to induce vomiting. Those are methods of bringing up vomit, and these are methods of drawing blood. Hence, blood discharged by either method has the same ruling in (the chapter on ritual purification).

This demonstrates the perfection, balance, and coherence of the revealed law, and shows that the scriptural texts and their meanings confirm and agree with one another:

“Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have found in it much contradiction.”

As for the cupper, he sucks the air out of the cupping vessel, and the air draws along the blood within it. Some blood may therefore rise with the air and enter his throat without his noticing. When the underlying rationale is hidden or variable, the ruling is made dependent

---

## Page 12706

on the circumstances in which it is likely to be present. Thus, just as a sleeping person who passes wind without knowing it is required to perform ablution, some blood may enter the cupper’s stomach with his saliva without his knowing it. Blood is among the most potent substances that invalidate the fast. Consuming it is forbidden in itself because it causes desire to become excessive and leads away from moderation. The fasting person is commanded to cut off the source of this, whereas blood increases the blood in the body and thus belongs to the prohibited category.

This is why the cupper’s fast is invalidated, just as a sleeping person’s ablution is invalidated even if he is not certain that he has passed wind: he may do so without knowing it. Likewise, blood may enter the cupper’s throat without his knowing it. As for someone who merely makes the incisions, he is not a cupper, and this rationale does not apply to him; hence his fast is not invalidated. Likewise, if we suppose that a cupper does not suck on the cupping vessel, but instead sucks on something else or draws the blood by another method, his fast would not be invalidated. The Prophet’s words—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—concerned the familiar, customary kind of cupper.

When the wording is general, even if he intended a particular individual, all others of the same kind share the ruling. This accords with the established practice of the revealed law: whatever ruling applies to one member of the Muslim community applies to them all. Here, this applies all the more strongly. His words therefore cannot establish a ruling for something that both their wording and their meaning clearly exclude, especially when such an extension is at odds with revealed law and reason.

Allah knows best. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bestow blessings and abundant peace upon our Prophet Muhammad, his family, and his Companions.

---

## Page 12707

He was asked:

A man was intimate with his wife while hearing someone speaking at the predawn meal, but he could not tell whether the man was eating his predawn meal or making the call to prayer. He then thought it more likely that the man was eating, so he had intercourse with his wife. Shortly afterwards, dawn broke. What is required of him? Please give us a ruling, and may you be rewarded.

He replied:

The scholars hold three opinions on this matter:

The first is that he must make up the fast and offer expiation. This is one of the two opinions reported from Ahmad.

Mālik said that he need only make up the fast. This is also the other opinion reported from Ahmad, and it is the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Abū Hanīfah and others.

The third is that he is required neither to make up the fast nor to offer expiation. This is what the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, and it is the strongest opinion; also because Allah Most High has pardoned mistakes

---

## Page 12708

and forgetfulness, and He, glorified and exalted is He, has permitted eating, drinking and intercourse until the white thread becomes distinct from the black thread. There is scholarly agreement that someone who is unsure whether dawn has broken may eat, drink and have intercourse, and need not make up the fast if that uncertainty remains unresolved.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man intended to have intercourse with his wife during the daytime in Ramadan, so he first broke his fast by eating and then had intercourse. Must he offer expiation or not? And what is required of someone who breaks his fast without an excuse?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

The scholars have two well-known opinions on this matter:

The first is that expiation is obligatory. This is the view of the majority, including Mālik, Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah and others.

The second is that it is not obligatory. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī. These two opinions rest on differing views of what incurs expiation: breaking a fast, or breaking a valid fast, through intercourse, or through intercourse or other acts, depending on the school. For Abū Hanīfah

---

## Page 12709

takes account of the highest category of fast-breaking, whereas Mālik considers fast-breaking without qualification. Their disagreement therefore concerns someone who breaks his fast by swallowing a pebble, a date stone or something similar. There is also a report from Ahmad that someone who breaks his fast through cupping must offer expiation, as with other acts that invalidate the fast. Through acts of intercourse—as for eating, drinking and similar acts, no expiation is due for these.

They then disagreed over whether this requires the breaking of a valid fast. Al-Shāfiʿī and others stipulate this condition. Thus, if someone eats and then has intercourse, begins the day without intending to fast and then has intercourse, or has intercourse, offers expiation and then has intercourse again, no expiation is due from him, because he did not have intercourse during a valid fast.

Ahmad, according to the apparent position of his school, and others maintain that expiation is indeed obligatory in these and similar cases. This is because the person was obliged to abstain during Ramadan, so his fast is invalid, resembling an invalid state of ihrām. Just as someone in ihrām for Hajj who invalidates his ihrām must continue in it, refraining from the acts it prohibits, and incurs the same consequences for committing any of them as he would in a valid state of ihrām, so too must someone obliged to fast Ramadan continue to abstain, even though his fast has been invalidated by eating, intercourse or the absence of an intention. He remains obliged to refrain from the acts prohibited during fasting. If he commits any of them, he incurs the same consequences as during a fast

---

## Page 12710

that is valid. In both cases under discussion, he must make up the fast. This is because the sanctity of the month is violated in both cases; indeed, the violation is more serious here, since he already sinned by breaking his fast in the first place. He has thus sinned twice, making the obligation of expiation all the more emphatic.

Moreover, if expiation were not obligatory in such a case, this would provide a means for everyone to evade it. Anyone wishing to have intercourse during Ramadan could simply eat first and then have intercourse. Indeed, eating would help him achieve his purpose. Thus, before eating a meal he would be liable for expiation, but if he and his wife ate and then he had intercourse with her, he would owe no expiation. This is an abhorrent consequence that the Sharia would never prescribe.

For it is firmly established in reason and religion that the greater the sin, the more severe the punishment, and the stronger the resemblance, the stronger the punishment. Expiation combines an element of worship with an element of punishment, and was prescribed both to deter and to erase sin. In every case, therefore, a stronger cause calls for a stronger consequence.

Furthermore, even if breaking the fast by eating is not an independent cause requiring expiation—as Abū Hanīfah and Mālik hold it to be—it should at least reinforce the independent cause; instead,

---

## Page 12711

it would prevent that cause from taking legal effect. This is far removed from the principles of the Sharia. Moreover, a man engaging in intercourse often breaks his fast before penetration; according to this view, he would thereby be exempt from expiation. This is plainly untenable. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man deliberately broke his fast during the day in Ramadan and then had intercourse. Must he make up the fast and offer expiation, or only make up the fast without expiation?

He replied:

He must make up the fast. As for expiation, it is obligatory according to the schools of Mālik, Ahmad, and Abū Hanīfah, but not according to al-Shāfiʿī.

He was asked:

A man had intercourse with his wife at dawn, believing that it was still night. It then became clear that dawn had already broken. What is required of him?

---

## Page 12712

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah.

The scholars hold three opinions on this issue:

The first is that he must make up the fast and offer expiation. This is the well-known position in Ahmad’s school.

The second is that he must make up the fast. This is another position in Ahmad’s school, and it is the position of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, and Mālik.

The third is that he is required neither to make up the fast nor to offer expiation. This is the view of a number of the early scholars, such as Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, Mujāhid, al-Hasan, Ishāq, and Dāwūd and his followers, as well as scholars of later generations. They say that whoever eats believing that dawn has broken, then discovers that it has not, need not make up the fast.

This is the soundest of these opinions and the most consistent with the principles of the Sharia and the evidence of the Quran and Sunnah. It also follows by analogy from the principles of Ahmad and others. Allah does not hold accountable those who forget or make a mistake, and this person has made a mistake. Allah has permitted eating and intercourse until the white thread of dawn becomes distinct from the black thread, and He has recommended delaying the pre-dawn meal. Whoever does what he has been encouraged and permitted to do has not been negligent. Such a person has an even stronger claim to be excused than one who forgets. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12713

He was asked:

If a man kisses or embraces his wife and emits pre-ejaculatory fluid, does this invalidate his fast or not?

He replied:

According to most scholars, this invalidates his fast.

He was asked:

About someone who breaks his fast in Ramadan, etc.

He replied:

If he breaks his fast in Ramadan, declaring it lawful despite knowing that it is prohibited, he must be put to death. If he acts in sinful disobedience, he is to be punished for breaking his fast in Ramadan as the ruler sees fit, and the prescribed punishment for unlawful sexual intercourse is to be inflicted on him. If he is ignorant, he is to be informed of this, and the prescribed punishment for unlawful sexual intercourse is to be inflicted on him. The matter is referred to the ruler’s considered judgment. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 12714

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About rinsing the mouth, drawing water into the nose, using a tooth-stick, tasting food, vomiting, bleeding, applying oil, and applying kohl.

He replied:

Rinsing the mouth and drawing water into the nose are prescribed for the fasting person by scholarly consensus. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and the Companions used to rinse their mouths and draw water into their noses while fasting. However, he said to Laqīt ibn Sabira:

“Draw water thoroughly into your nose, unless you are fasting.”

Thus, he prohibited doing so excessively, not drawing water into the nose itself.

Using a tooth-stick is permissible without dispute. However, scholars differed over whether it is disliked after the sun passes its zenith, holding two well-known opinions, both transmitted from Ahmad. No evidence from Islamic law establishes that it is disliked in a way that would warrant restricting the general scope of the texts concerning the tooth-stick. Drawing an analogy between it and a martyr’s blood, or similar matters, is weak on several grounds, as explained at length elsewhere.

Tasting food without a need is disliked, but it does not invalidate one’s fast. As for tasting it when there is a need,

---

## Page 12715

it is like rinsing the mouth.

As for vomiting, deliberately inducing it invalidates the fast, whereas being overcome by vomiting does not.
